# A2.1 Executive Summary

## Ethiopia: Highly Vulnerable to Climate Change Impacts

### 1. Ethiopia, highly vulnerable to climate change impacts, faces a series of climatic challenges including increased temperatures, increasing precipitation, and prolonged droughts.

### 2. The unpredictability of rainfall patterns, coupled with the increasing frequency of droughts have severely compromised agricultural productivity, leading to food insecurity and malnutrition in many parts of the country. This has pushed vulnerable communities deeper into poverty and increasing their overall vulnerability.

### 3. Ethiopia is a predominantly rural country where agriculture accounts for about 40% of its GDP (Gross Domestic Product) and employs over 85% of the population, making it critical to their livelihoods, which renders them highly vulnerable to climate impacts such as precipitation and temperature changes that affect crop yields.

### 4. The projected increase in extreme weather events due to climate variability, Regional* and global** climate models project an increasing occurrence of extreme weather events such as droughts, floods, heatwaves, and heavy rainfall, which will have a negative impact on the GDP (GDP) growth* and spur climate vulnerability of rural households, especially concerning the livelihoods of smallholder farmers who are already highly vulnerable to climate change.

### 5. In Oromia, the livelihoods of pastoralists center on livestock which are similarly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as droughts, floods, heatwaves, and heavy rainfall. This will have a critical decline in quantity and quality of feed, reducing productivity and increasing mortality of animals. While in Amhara and Benishangul-Gumuz regions, the livelihoods of smallholder farmers are highly dependent on agriculture.

### 6. The proposed project seeks to enhance climate resilience in the Goba-Kirari Valley in Amhara and the Borena Zone in Oromia by providing sustainable access to safe water through the deployment of Solar Water Pumps (SWP) and Irrigation Systems (IS). This will provide a reliable source of water for livestock, power to the pumps, thereby offering a reliable source of water for irrigation, drinking, and livestock. By improving water availability, this project will increase agricultural productivity by reducing the cost of water and increasing the efficiency of water use in agriculture.

### 7. The Ministry of Finance (MOF), the Ministry of Irrigation and Landworks (MILW), the Ministry of Water and Energy (MOWE) in collaboration with the regional authorities will facilitate and provide financial and technical support to ensure that the project is implemented effectively, efficiently, and sustainably.

### 8. This project will promote sustainable water extraction practices in the design and management of systems coupled with efforts to build institutional capacity and improve policy frameworks, will be creating enabling environments for the adoption of climate-resilient agricultural technologies such as solar-powered irrigation digital technologies, such as smart water meters and digital payment systems, to monitor and manage water use.

### 9. The project is expected to deliver significant climate mitigation and adaptation benefits. It aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 24,250 tCO2e annually (2024–2046), with a reduction of 24,250 tCO2e during the project implementation period (2024–2036) in terms of carbon sequestration and avoided deforestation. The project will directly benefit women and men, and indirectly support the resilience of 1.75 million people. These benefits will manifest through increased agricultural productivity, improved food security, and enhanced livelihoods particularly in the drought-prone areas of the project sites.

### 10. This project is expected to deliver significant economic and social co-benefits. It will create job opportunities, stimulate local economies, and promote gender-sensitive initiatives by empowering women and men through capacity building activities. The project will benefit particularly those most vulnerable, benefit from the project's interventions. These indirect benefits consist of both direct and indirect impacts on people’s livelihoods (e.g., improved access to water for private or public sources for similar projects “lighthouse effect”), as well as a lower potential of water-related conflicts in the region. The project will also contribute to climate change adaptation by addressing potential risks such as foreign currency shortages, limited technical expertise, and regional

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 6

**C**

**8\.** The project places communities at the center of the implementation approach, providing a governance and implementation arrangement that is carried by local communities represented in legally established irrigation user associations (IUAs). These IUAs are recognized as legal entities under Ethiopian law and have established links with other water access schemes. Maintenance and operation of the assets is provided by beneficiary communities through their IUAs. The project ensures that interventions are inclusive and responsive to the needs of marginalized groups, including women, youth, and persons with disabilities. By promoting joint ownership among beneficiaries but also enhances the long-term sustainability of the project outcomes.

**9\.** Moreover, the project will provide smallholder farmers with access to microfinance loans and grants, to make modern agriculture inputs and irrigation services accessible to smaller farms. The involvement of local financial institutions in providing these services is critical for increasing farm productivity and for livestock. It will be also accessed by the Irrigation Service Providers (ISPs) who will be provided with training on water management and service delivery.

**10\.** The project also emphasizes the importance of monitoring and evaluation to ensure that the interventions are achieving their intended outcomes. The use of digital technologies, such as smart meters, will allow for precise measurement of water usage and efficient allocation of resources. This will help in managing water resources sustainably, preventing over-extraction of groundwater, and ensuring the benefits of the project reach all stakeholders.

**11\.** This initiative aligns with Ethiopia's broader Climate Resilient Green Economy (CREGE) strategy and National Development Plan (NDP). It contributes to the achievement of Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and the Paris Agreement. The project's integrated and comprehensive approach offers a significant opportunity for scaling up climate-resilient agriculture, contributing to global efforts to combat climate change and enhance resilience in vulnerable communities.

B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately two pages)

13. The climate context for Ethiopia highlights the pressing need for climate adaptation measures to tackle the challenges posed by climate change in various sectors of the economy and the welfare of its population.

14. Ethiopia is one of the world’s most drought-prone countries and has been ranked 5th out of 184 countries in terms of its risk of drought. The country is faced with increasingly unpredictable rains, and in some years, such as those between 2009 and 2010, twelve extreme droughts were recorded (killing over 40,000 people and affected over 54 million others).

15. Ethiopia is also ranked 34th out of 162 countries in terms of flooding risk, and 5th out of 162 in terms of landslide risk. The increase in severity of short, heavy rains in the highlands leads to increased flooding in the lowlands, which can cause soil erosion, loss of topsoil, reduced agricultural productivity (and hence fertility). Changes in temperature and rainfall increase the frequency and severity of extreme events.

16. Historical climate trends indicate exacerbated droughts, and desertification in the lowlands of the country. The highlands are also affected by increased temperatures and more frequent heatwaves and prolonged droughts that may affect livestock rearing. The highlands on the other hand suffer from more intense and frequent rainfall, which can lead to flooding and landslides. Higher temperatures will also lower agricultural production.

17. Future projections of temperature and rainfall patterns in Ethiopia exhibit a high likelihood but not certainty that mean annual temperature is likely to increase between +2°C to 5°C by 2050. The frequency of hot days and nights will substantially increase. About 15–29 percent of days will be considerably hotter than the current average, while about 36-48% of nights will be considerably warmer (by up to ~30 percent) by 2050. There is an increase in the proportion of total rainfall that falls in “heavy” events with a return period of less than one year. This will lead to more frequent and intense flooding in the country, but most severely in areas that are already water scarce, such as densely populated parts of the

# Health, social, and environmental concerns due to water stress

18. Ethiopia has abundant water resources and contains the headwaters of numerous transboundary rivers, including the Nile. Key water stress metrics suggest Ethiopia is water stressed. Total annual renewable water resources are estimated at 205 billion cubic meters (BCM), with per capita availability being 943 m³/year, which is below the threshold for water stress and just above the water scarcity threshold. The ratio of water withdrawals to supply is 32 percent, which exceeds the threshold for water stress. Water stress is highest in the central highlands, where rainfall is low and seasonal in some locations[1].

In Ethiopia, about one-third (or ~26 million people) travel more than 30 minutes to collect drinking water; 28% use untreated surface water. 43 million people do not have hand washing facilities[2] and 60-80% of communities lack access to safe drinking water. In addition, the majority of Ethiopia’s rivers and other sources are often contaminated with human and animal waste and related diseases are the leading cause of death in children under five years old.

20. Irrigation is the largest source of demand for surface water. Approximately 85 percent (around 93,000 MCM per year) of irrigation withdrawals come from rivers and lakes. The remaining 15% comes from groundwater and industrial withdrawals are from surface water. Almost 2 million hectares (ha) are irrigated, mainly in the Oromia/Amhara regions.

21. Groundwater accounts for 90 percent of domestic/municipal and industrial supply. Around 70 percent of rural areas depend on groundwater as their primary source of drinking water. The level of groundwater dependency is highest in the more and Wabi-Shebelle and Ogaden Basins, although groundwater is not very accessible in some regions due to its depth. 46 Pastoralists in these basins also depend on groundwater for

## Climate change impacting already weak economy, the poorest, and the most vulnerable

* The World Bank (2020) estimates that climate change will cost Ethiopia $3 billion per year on average. Climate change will have significant economic effects on the Ethiopian economy, and on the livelihoods of its citizens. These include: increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events; altered growth cycles leading to delay planting, and increased incidence of pests and diseases. Ethiopia’s agricultural sector is particularly vulnerable as it accounts for 35% of GDP, employs about a third of the workforce. Specialized analysis showed severe droughts to recur increasingly frequently, with herd mortality amounting to 10-20%.

## Research increasingly underpins the persistence of economic costs caused by climate extremes as a major driver of losses in GDP**

* It has been estimated that climate change will affect the country’s GDP growth at -5.3% per year on average. The impact is expected to be felt across all sectors, but it is projected to be primarily driven by impact on agricultural productivity. It is estimated that about US $2 billion will be lost in the agricultural sector due to climate change impacts over a 10-year period (2015-2024). According to the Ministry of Agriculture GDP (Annex II, refer to paragraph No. 27 provides a detailed assessment of the economic impact of climate change on Ethiopia’s economy. The study shows that climate change will have significant impacts on the livestock and energy sector of Ethiopia. The impacts of climate change are further compounded by dependence on imports for food security, which is expected to increase from 15% in 2018/19 (year) and energy prices, and 35% inflation in the context of current geopolitical crises.19

## Although Ethiopia is diversifying its economic base, the Home-Grown Economic Resilience (HMER) outlines that climate change will continue to be a major driver of losses in GDP**

* The impact on agricultural productivity has grown rapidly in the past decade due to intensification of modern seeds, fertilizer use, and farm mechanization. This has led to an increase in food production and income for smallholder farmers, cooperatives and private actors. In 2011, the Ethiopian Climate Resilient Green Economy (CREG) strategy was launched as a national framework for climate change adaptation and development. Ethiopia’s CREG initiative aims to “climate-proof” its National Development Plan goals.

## Large percentage of population below the poverty line live in most climatically vulnerable areas**

* An important proportion of the Ethiopian population lives in areas that are highly vulnerable to climate change. The five vulnerable states are Amhara, Oromia, Somali, and Tigray. These regional states are all heavily agricultural and pastoralist-based economies. In 2018/19 (year), 54% of those living in the Amhara and Tigray and Allot-67% of those living in Oromia and Somali support their life on less than US $2 per day.

## Climate change also has a determinantal impact on labor productivity and health. According to a UNFCCC/WHO report, considering a high-emissions scenario, i.e., increase in temperatures of 4°C will bring about a significant reduction in agricultural output, with an estimated loss of 50% by the year 2030.

* Agriculture and construction) and an increase in heat related diseases among elderly people (65+) to over 65 deaths per day. The report also states that climate change will have a negative impact on health.

## Women – and especially girls – are disproportionately affected by the impacts of climate change

* While Ethiopia achieved significant progress towards girl’s education (enrollment rates increased from 51% in 2003/4 to 78.6% in 2019/20), the quality of education is still poor and access remains limited, especially for girls.
* Some of their primary responsibilities include, the collection of water for drinking, cooking, and washing, the collection of firewood, and the collection of food. The collection of water is particularly challenging in rural areas where women and girls spend 10-20 hours per week collecting water from distant sources resulting in girls investing up to 8 hours a day in water fetching, thereby preventing them from using this time for other productive activities.

# National Socio-economic Vulnerabilities:

## Population and Demographics:
Ethiopia’s population is diverse, with over 80 ethnic groups and numerous languages spoken. The largest ethnic group is the Oromo (34% of the population), followed by the Amhara (27%). Other significant groups include the Somali (5%), Tigray (6%), and SNNPR (12%). Despite its large population, Ethiopia has a low density of 80 people per square kilometer. The country is experiencing rapid urbanization, with cities like Addis Ababa and Dire Dawa offering opportunities for development efforts. As of May 2024, Ethiopia’s population is approximately 129.7 million, making it the second most populous country in Africa after Nigeria.

## Agriculture-Based Economy:
Ethiopia's economy is primarily agrarian, with agriculture being the main livelihood for over 85% of its population and contributing to about 30% of GDP. The reliance on rain-fed agriculture makes the country vulnerable to climate variability and recurrent droughts. In recent years, Ethiopia has experienced significant losses in arable land due to desertification and soil erosion, leading to reduced agricultural productivity. Droughts alone can cause total GDP loss by 1-6 percent annually (World Bank Report). The country's food security is also at risk as it imports about one-third of its cereal needs from neighboring countries like Sudan and Egypt.

## Poverty and Inequality:
Ethiopia faces high levels of poverty, particularly in rural areas. Although national poverty rates have fallen from 30% to 24%, there were strong differences in the nature of poverty reduction: urban (reduction from 28%-15%) versus rural areas (reduction from 30% to 26%) over the same period.

There are significant disparities between urban and rural areas, with urban areas experiencing faster economic growth. Efforts to reduce poverty and inequality have been ongoing but there remain significant challenges. The percentage of people living below the national poverty line is higher in rural areas (41%) than in urban areas (29%). In Ethiopia, the Gini index, which measures income inequality, is 39.1 as of the latest data available.

## Economic Challenges:
Ethiopia's economy faces several challenges, including inadequate infrastructure and limited access to markets and jobs. The median income in 2022 was estimated at USD 850. Access to basic services such as healthcare and education remains a challenge for many rural communities due to inadequate facilities, shortages of health professionals, long distances to health centers, and poor educational resources.

## National Economy:
Ethiopia is a least developed country with growing economy. In 2023 Ethiopia reached an estimated GDP scale of 156 billion dollars nominal, for PPP the country's economy has reached an estimated $487 billion. The country has a large foreign debt and very low foreign reserves.

### Political landscape affected stability and development progress in some regions: Ethiopia has experienced political instability due to the ongoing process of economic reforms and democratization. However, there have been ethnic tensions and conflicts that have affected stability and development progress.

#### Limited infrastructure for irrigation and safe water supply to improve livelihoods and address climate shocks:
- Only 5% of land is irrigated if irrigation is being practiced; energy generated from emission-intensive diesel generators.
- As large tracts of the rural population do not have access to electricity, existing diesel-based systems are often inefficient and expensive. The use of solar-powered water pumps for irrigation and water pumping has so far been widely used due to access to finance and lack of experience (RMI 2018).
- Solar PV is a promising technology that can be deployed on a large scale in Ethiopia.

### Green grid with limited access to energy to drive economy and development:
- Ethiopia boasts significant potential for renewable energy, with abundant sources like wind, solar, and geothermal. However, despite this potential, 70% of Ethiopians lack access to electricity.
- The challenge is to address the Government launched the National Electrification Program (NEP) in 2017.

### The NEP not only acknowledges climate change's impact on hydropower but also integrates the Energy-Water Nexus and builds on the Agricultural Transformation Agency (ATA) and the Water Sector Development Strategy (WSDS). In Ethiopia, the availability of shallow groundwater offers the potential to use solar-powered irrigation systems for smallholder farmers. However, many smallholder farmers are unable to afford such systems.
- The Ministry of Irrigation and Lowlands (MoIL) distributed 54,000 water pumps in 2017/2018, with a focus on green energy options to end imports of water pumps that run on diesel or gasoline.

## Situation in project target areas

### Kobo-Girara Valley - Amhara Region

Kobo Girara is one of the eight rural districts in North Woldie Zone in the North Eastern part of Amhara. Historically, the district has been characterized by low agricultural productivity due to a combination of population growth, subsistence agriculture, limited access to markets and credit, significant poverty and food insecurity, and poor infrastructure. The project area within the Kobo Girara Valley is a primarily rural area with a high proportion of smallholder farmers. The majority of the population in this region lives on less than $1 per day.

The agricultural system relies heavily (over 85 percent) on rainfed subsistence methods, utilizing basic and traditional farming tools with limited access to improved inputs and services. The district is characterized by significant rainfall variations, and crop pest attacks exacerbate the challenges faced by farmers, leading to low agricultural productivity.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 16

## Livestock production in the Kobo Grana valley is primarily for subsistence and traction power, with minimal output for sale. The majority of livestock are kept by pastoralists who depend on livestock production as their livelihoods. However, husbandry practices plague the sector, while non-farm activities remain undeveloped as alternative livelihoods. Livestock production in the Kobo Grana valley is largely pastoralist-based and depends heavily on rainfall due to limited competitiveness, access to credit, technical support, and entrepreneurship, compounded by the application of traditional husbandry practices.

### The Kobo prescriptured irrigation project was launched in 1998 to address food insecurity in the valley. Followed by the establishment of the Kobo Grana Valley Development Program Office in 1999 to enhance food security through water resources development and diversification, the project aimed at improving livelihoods for pastoralists.

The project area is characterized by a variety of soil types including clay, loam, sand, silt, and clay with sediment aquifer thickness ranging from a few meters to 170 meters. Simulated rainfall shows that the area experiences more favorable conditions expected near dry riverbeds and deeper depths along the volcanic ridge of Mendele.

### Large-scale irrigation is absent in the area, with groundwater-based irrigation initiated by the regional government in 2004. The project has drilled 35 boreholes to provide water for livestock watering and 16 to enhance food security by reducing reliance on rain-fed agriculture. Although the Kobo District has a potential irrigable land of about 78,000 hectares, only 9% is currently under irrigation. There are 240 productive wells in the area and 236 highly productive boreholes were identified and drilled for irrigation purposes. At a later stage, additional boreholes will be drilled to meet the demand for water supply. The existing boreholes were powered by diesel generators. Currently, only 32 boreholes powered by diesel generators remain operational due to high maintenance costs and frequent breakdowns. As a result, the cost of running these generators have become expensive to run, not reliable, frequently fail, discharging the carbon that has been generated.

### Farmers utilizing complementary irrigation methods have experienced increased income and improved livelihoods, but further investment in submersible pumps and irrigation systems is needed to fully utilize the remaining potential.

## Crops with both Meher and Belg season rainfall—but, due to shortages of rainfall, not all cultivated land is currently productive.

In November 2021, approximately 68,000 animals have died (losing almost 75 percent of their livestock) and another 34,000 are in poor condition. This has led to a significant reduction in the number of cattle herds over the past two years. According to ECCS/WC, a total of over 30,000 households in Merrebe kebele and Ekuwe subkebe (Tewolde Tsehay) have been displaced due to drought and climate change. Men with the remaining cattle have been displaced in search of pasture and water, with women and elderly people on the brink of starvation within the villages.

### 51. The feasibility study has shown that pastoralists in the Borana farming systems adopted a wide range of adaptive strategies over time to meet their livelihood needs, including livestock services as a cash income source. These include supplementary feeding, off-farm employment, and herd mobility to remote areas where the most consistent rainfall is experienced on longer pastures and their current zones pursued as responses to climate change.

(Ref Annex 2)

### 52. The Borana basin, situated in southern Ethiopia within a semi-arid climate and adjacent to Kenya, spans approximately 180,000 km². It is characterized by high rainfall variability and low water availability. The area relies primarily on groundwater from intricate volcanic formations. Erratic rainfall patterns influence agricultural productivity significantly.

### 53. Management of water sources in the Borana basin varies, with traditional wells being particularly deep and well-complexed as eku tula, representing the most intricate systems. Each well is overseen by a chief responsible for managing its use. The system ensures that women collect water daily, since three-day rotations ensure equitable watering of cattle, fostering high levels of cooperation among community members.

### 54. Extensive studies were conducted in the area and 113 boreholes have been drilled and constructed of which, only 13 powered by diesel generators are operational.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 18

## Aim for carbon neutrality by that year. This ambitious target, contingent on international support and conducive investment environments, is based on Ethiopia's Climate Resilience Green Economy (CRGE) Strategy. The CRGE strategy aims to achieve this goal through a comprehensive approach in sectors such as agriculture and forestry, which are vital for national income and livelihoods. Ethiopia’s climate change adaptation plan focuses on integrating climate considerations into national development policies and programs, aiming for a coherent and integrated approach that builds the adaptive capacity of communities, promotes sustainable resource management, and builds climate resilience in specific sectors. The plan also highlights the importance of participatory approaches, engaging stakeholders at all levels, including local communities, to ensure inclusiveness and ownership in this implementation process.

### National Adaptation Plan (2017): Ethiopia has developed its National Adaptation Plan (NAP) to address the multiple dimensions of climate change adaptation. The NAP is a strategic document that aims to integrate climate considerations into national development policies and programs for a coherent and integrated approach. It focuses on building adaptive capacity, promoting sustainable resource management, and building climate resilience in specific sectors.

### Key Components:
- **Actively involving local communities** in the planning and implementation of adaptation measures.
- **Promoting programs that empower women and youth to participate in adaptation activities**, ensuring their full and effective participation.
- **Establishing platforms for collaboration among various stakeholders**, including government agencies, non-governmental organizations, private sector, and local communities, to foster shared responsibility and collective action.
- **Enhancing the capacities of stakeholders at all levels through training and education** to improve their ability to adapt to climate change.
- **Incorporating participatory planning processes that involve stakeholders in the decision-making**, making adaptation measures more effective and sustainable.
- **Implementing feedback mechanisms** that allow stakeholders to provide input and suggestions, thereby improving the effectiveness of adaptation measures.
- **Strengthening decentralized governance structures** to facilitate local-level decision-making and implementation of adaptation measures.
- **Providing institutional support to local governments and community organizations** to enhance their role in adopting planned adaptation activities.
- **Supporting community-led initiatives and traditional ecological knowledge in developing adaptation activities.**
- **Ensuring a gender-balanced participation in adaptation activities**, ensuring that women and marginalized groups are equally involved and benefit from adaptation measures.
- **Enhancing the capacity of communities for planning, implementation, and participation.**
- **Encouraging the adoption of community-based natural resource management practices to ensure sustainable use of resources.**

### References:

[2] Ethiopia’s Climate Resilient Green Economy National Adaptation Plan May 2019 Federal Democratic Republic of Ethiopia

## 65. Alternative solutions of the rationale behind the consideration of solar technology as opposed to diesel pumping has been provided in detail below

### a) Ground Water vs Surface Water


---
**Page 28**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROGRAM V3.0 | PAGE 25

Groundwater extraction emerges as the best alternative under the given circumstances in Ethiopia for several compelling reasons:

1. **Water Availability:** As mentioned, surface water is not readily available in the targeted areas. In arid and semi-arid regions, groundwater often represents the only reliable water source, especially during prolonged dry periods.
2. **Climate Change:** Groundwater sources are generally more resilient to short-term climate variability and droughts compared to surface water. The IPCC's Africa fact sheet notes that climate change is exacerbating water scarcity in many parts of Ethiopia, making groundwater extraction increasingly critical.
3. **Reduced Evaporation Losses:** In hot, and climates like parts of Ethiopia, surface water sources are subject to high evaporation rates. Groundwater extraction minimizes these losses, ensuring more efficient use of the available water resources.
4. **Year-round Availability:** Unlike seasonal surface water sources, groundwater can often be accessed throughout the year, providing a more consistent water supply for agriculture and domestic use. This is crucial in areas where rainfall patterns are highly variable or unreliable.

5. **Scalability and Decentralization:** Solar-powered groundwater pumping systems can be implemented at various scales, from individual farms to community-level projects. This decentralized approach aligns with the Ethiopian government's focus on rural electrification and improving access to clean water within the context of electricity access.
6. **Potential for Agricultural Productivity:** Access to groundwater can dramatically increase agricultural productivity when combined with efficient irrigation techniques. This is crucial for building resilience in the agricultural sector, which the IPCC identifies as highly vulnerable to climate change in Africa.
7. **Reduced Pressure on Surface Water Ecosystems:** By relying on groundwater, the project can help reduce pressure on potentially fragile surface water ecosystems, which may already be stressed due to climate change and other factors.

8. **Alignment with National and International Goals:** Sustainable groundwater extraction aligns with Ethiopia's development goals and contributes towards achieving the Sustainable Development Goals, particularly those related to food security, food safety, and good health.

**66. However, it’s crucial to emphasize the importance of sustainable groundwater management and has been highlighted in the 2015 National Groundwater Management Strategy. Improper or excessive groundwater extraction can lead to depletion of water from surface water recharge and other environmental issues and hence has emphasized necessary measures to avoid over-abstraction.**

## Grid vs Distributed Energy Supply

Access to reliable electricity supply is a challenge in many rural and off-grid areas of Ethiopia for several reasons:

1. **Geographic terrain:** Ethiopia's rugged and mountainous terrain, particularly in rural areas, makes it difficult and expensive to extend the national electricity grid to remote communities.
2. **Dispersed settlements:** Rural settlements in Ethiopia are often widely dispersed with low population densities, making it economically unfeasible for utilities to invest in grid infrastructure for a relatively small number of customers.
3. **Limited infrastructure:** Many rural areas lack the necessary infrastructure, such as roads and transmission lines, which increases the costs of extending the grid.
4. **Poverty and affordability:** A significant portion of the rural population in Ethiopia lives in poverty, making it challenging for them to afford the costs associated with grid connection and electricity tariffs.


---
**Page 29**
---

7. Regulating the costs of extending the grid versus using minigrid systems, there is a significant difference:

   * **Grid extension costs:** The cost of extending the national grid to rural areas in Ethiopia can range from $15,000 to $30,000 per kilometer, depending on factors such as terrain, distance from the existing grid, and the number of households served.
   * **Minigrid system costs:** The costs of installing a minigrid system vary based on the size, technology, and location. For example, the cost can range from $150 to $3,000 per kWp of installed capacity (Source: International Renewable Energy Agency, 2020).

8. For example, a 100 kW solar PV battery minigrid system suitable for a remote village might cost between $500,000 and $750,000 to install. In comparison with extending the national grid, this is significantly cheaper.

9. A study by the International Water Management Institute found that solar-powered irrigation systems are more cost-effective than diesel-powered systems in Ethiopia (https://iwrmi.org/).

10. The cost of solar water pumping systems in Ethiopia can be as low as $0.10 per kWh, making it more cost-effective than diesel-powered pumps in the long term.

11. Environmental impact: Solar-powered irrigation systems in Ethiopia can offset approximately 1.5 metric tons of carbon dioxide emissions annually compared to a diesel-powered pump (Source: ClimateWorks Foundation).

12. Potential: A World Bank report estimates that Ethiopia has the potential to develop 5 GW of solar power, enough to meet its energy needs and improve the adaptive capacity of its communities. (World Bank: Solar irrigation in Ethiopia, worldbank.org)

13. As the cost of solar PV technology continues to decline and awareness of its benefits grows, more farmers will adopt this technology, leading to improved agricultural productivity and food security while contributing to sustainable development goals.

### Public Sector Considerations

This proposal should be funded through public financial resources as opposed to the private sector:

* **Novelty of Solar Technology and Adoption in Ethiopia:** Solar-powered irrigation is a relatively new technology in Ethiopia with limited local experience and expertise. This novelty increases perceived risks for private investors, but it also means that there are fewer existing competitors. As solar technology adoption is thriving in Ethiopia, the uptake of solar irrigation technology is still in its early stages.

* **Financial Institution Awareness:** Ethiopian financial institutions are more familiar with diesel-based irrigation systems than solar-powered ones. However, as awareness grows and financing for solar projects becomes more accessible, this barrier may decrease significantly.

* **Need for De-risking:** Given the above factors, there's a clear need to de-risk investments in solar-powered irrigation to attract private sector participation. Public and grant resources can play a crucial role in this de-risking process.

## Project programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

This project targets partnerships between federal and regional governments, stakeholders and communities for pioneering SWP in cooperation with local SMEs and small businesses in establishing and utilizing solar water pumping systems (SWP) to provide access to safe drinking water in the Northern Amhara region of Ethiopia. The areas that are self-sustainable and replicable, thereby accelerating the GCF's objective for universal access to safe water as it is a key component of sustainable development, were selected based on their high climate vulnerability.

### B.3 Project programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

#### Climate Vulnerability: This area is particularly susceptible to climate change impacts, with erratic rainfall patterns and increasing temperatures.

- **Groundwater Potential:** The Kobo Grana Valley has abundant but largely untapped groundwater resources. The Ministry of Water and Energy (MoWE) (2017) Groundwater Resources Evaluation and Assessment Project of Borena Zone includes a Hydrogeology & Groundwater Modeling Annex, which provides hydrogeological data and groundwater reserve estimates.

- **Population Density:** The valley is densely inhabited, with an estimated population of about 1.6 million people in the project area.

- **Existing Infrastructure:** There are 60 boreholes in the region that have been capped and are considered non-functional. The project aims to rehabilitate these boreholes and install engines, which will provide significant benefits to approximately 4,500 smallholder farmers.

The selected areas for this project include the Kobo Grana Valley and the Borena zone where water is the predominant source of food and income for the agro-pastoral population. In this target zone, the project area covers an estimated total land area of 193,280 km² with a total population of approximately 503,373.

#### Ministry of Water and Energy (MoWE) (2017): Groundwater resources evaluation and assessment project of Borena Zone: Volume V - Hydrogeology & groundwater modeling annex | main report. Hydrogeology, Addis Ababa

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 30

### C

inhabitants as well as a livestock population of approximately 1,469,000 (Annex 2). On average, the modeling results for the hydrogeological system, consisting of four sub-basins: Laga Batul, Ribba, Magori, and Borena, indicate that the groundwater recharge rate is low. The following considerations were made for the selection of Borena as the project intervention site:

* **Geographical Location:** Borena is located in the central part of Ethiopia, where frequent droughts and water scarcity make it one of the most climate-vulnerable areas in Ethiopia.
* **Groundwater Recharge Rate:** The recharge rate of groundwater in Borena is low due to the highly fractured nature of sub-surface water aquifers. Sensitivity analyses indicate a high recharge potential for hydrogeological systems of the region, with basaltic aquifers supporting sustainable groundwater extraction.

### Livestock Dependency:

The Borena zone is predominantly dependent on livestock, and the project will address this by rehabilitating existing boreholes and developing new ones to provide water for 85,640 households.

#### Existing Water Infrastructure:

The region has 40 boreholes that will be rehabilitated and powered by solar energy to support both potable water supply and agricultural needs. The rehabilitation of these boreholes is part of the Kobo-Girara Valley Development Programme (KGVDP) [1], which aims to provide sustainable access to clean drinking water for 85,640 households in Borena Zone.

#### Future Water Infrastructure:

The project will also develop new boreholes and wells. The rehabilitation estimate, which is expected to remain the same after 20 years, of these projects includes:

* **Borehole:** 2 waters
* **Well:** 1 water

Rehabilitation will not tap into reserves of the ground water aquifer system but only extract water from surface water resources. The project will also develop a groundwater recharge system to increase the recharge rate of sub-basin, which will require continuous monitoring to ensure the sustainable extraction and use of groundwater.

#### Water Management:

The project will implement a groundwater management plan that includes developing a safeguarding system that will be put in place to prevent unsustainable abstraction level. Salinity tests for boreholes and wells are being conducted by the Ethiopian Institute of Agricultural Research (EIAR) and results will be used to determine the extent to which new boreholes and wells are developed.

#### Hydrogeological Considerations:

The fractured form of sub-surface water basins in Borena, no robust estimates or data exist on static groundwater supply. However, sensitivity analyses in the feasibility study estimate that, given the depth of the aquifer system, it is unlikely to be tapped into by boreholes and wells. The project will not tap into reserves of the ground water aquifer system but only extract water from surface water resources.

#### Water Supply Projects:

The project will develop new boreholes and wells in Borena Zone, particularly high potential to use SWP. Existing deep wellfields include the Galchiro-Sante supply project which is currently being rehabilitated by EIAR.

#### Groundwater Balance Studies:

Groundwater balance studies annexed under 4 in the feasibility study (Annex 2) provides a detailed account of groundwater recharge and discharge processes, including precipitation volume, infiltration, evaporation, percolation, precipitation volume, variability, surface water run-off, and the transmissivity of sediments, which are all critical factors for understanding the hydrogeological system. These studies have been put into consideration when planning groundwater development initiatives. This understanding leads to more informed decision-making.

### Assumptions for Irrigation Purposes

a) The radius of influence and well interference is r as the distance between the 60 boreholes considered for this project and the additional 176 boreholes is beyond 500 meters.

b) Annual groundwater abstraction has been assumed at an average 20 hours per day for all 40 wells (0.3 liters/second), with a total of 8,960 cubic meters extracted annually from each well over the period of six months/year.

c) Considering the number of 40 wells in the Borena basin targeted for this purpose, a total of 1.5 MCM of ground water will be extracted per annum from a total recharge amount of 82.4 MCM.

d) Thus at the current rate, also considering the amount extracted by the community, which is 8.4 MCM, and the amount that will be used for other purposes such as livestock watering, only about 0.6 MCM of ground water will be extracted per annum leaving a net discharge of 82.4 MCM to the surface.

e) Considering the number of 176 wells in the Borena basin targeted for this purpose, the total amount of groundwater extracted will increase to 39.4 MCM/year within 20 years. While this approach does not take into consideration increasing population and livestock numbers, it is assumed that the total recharge amount remains constant at 82.4 MCM/year.

f) The water balance study based on baseline scenarios and putting the implementation of this project over a period of 20 years will show that the groundwater extraction rate will increase to 39.4 MCM/year, tapping to the existing groundwater reserve of 2,564.74 BCM, which will remain the same after 20 years.

### Borena Region

Conversely, for the Borena region with limited data availability, the Direct Precipitation Recharge Method utilizing the average annual rainfall (138 cm/year) and the total area of the Borena basin (457 km²), hilly regions, to calculate groundwater recharge particularly along regional fault or fracture systems. Based on this method, it is estimated that 20% of the precipitation will be infiltrated into the ground and discharged to the surface and extracted for irrigation purposes, following the assumptions have been applied:

a) The radius of influence and well interference is r as the distance between the 40 boreholes considered for this project and the additional 176 boreholes is beyond 500 meters.

b) Annual groundwater abstraction has been assumed at an average 20 hours per day for all 40 wells (0.3 liters/second), with a total of 8,960 cubic meters extracted annually from each well over the period of six months/year.

c) Considering the number of 40 wells in the Borena basin targeted for this purpose, a total of 1.5 MCM of ground water will be extracted per annum from a total recharge amount of 82.4 MCM.

d) Thus at the current rate, also considering the amount extracted by the community, which is 8.4 MCM, and the amount that will be used for other purposes such as livestock watering, only about 0.6 MCM of ground water will be extracted per annum leaving a net discharge of 82.4 MCM to the surface.

e) Note that the Borena basin is a basaltic Aquifer, which is intensely fractured, resulting in strong connectivity between the aquifers and the surface water bodies. The groundwater recharge occurs mainly from the Kobo-Girara valley within the water from surface water recharge, in Borena it flows within the frequency of 10-20 years.

f) However, the amount of groundwater extracted from this project is 1.5 MCM or 8.0 percent of the recharge amount of 82.4 MCM/year. This extraction rate will not have a significant impact on the surface water and net outflow into the groundwater aquifer system. Thus, the amount of groundwater extracted in this project can be considered as a small value.

### Financial and Technical Assistance

The project provides financial and technical assistance to overcome key market barriers described in section 82 (shortage of forex, access to finance by local communities, institutional and technical capacity and knowledge gaps) and to ensure that the project is implemented in accordance with the requirements of the Accredited Entity (AE), to the regional and local entities. MoU will define the technical requirements, extractive limits, water quality standards, environmental impact assessment, monitoring and evaluation targets, regional irrigation and water bureau, and other stakeholders.

### The Green Climate Fund

The GCF is a global mechanism that provides financial resources for projects and programs managed by a group of GCF and Adaptation Fund Projects. One of the main learnings from the first rounds of projects implemented by the fund is that it is important to have a strong project management team with experience in implementing and writing a project. This will provide support to the MoU, Programme Coordination Team with regional partners.

### Project Transformation

The project transforms previous plans for developing the groundwater wells with submerged pumps and diesel engines into solar-powered systems. The project also aims at improving water quality by installing existing deep weirsheets in the Kobo-Girara Valley and the Borena with solar PV to make them productive

### B.3.1: Complementary Program Components

#### Description of Component Objectives

86. The program is designed in two components. Component 1 centers on direct interventions and activities that will be implemented by the project team to ensure the successful implementation, support, and capacity building to sustain and expand these interventions. Collectively, these two components are expected to address the immediate needs of water supply and sanitation services in the targeted regions.

87. Both components emphasize on enhancing the policy and regulatory framework, cultivating a conducive environment for the deployment of Solar Water Pumping (SWP) systems and governance structures that will ensure their sustainability over time. The financial mechanisms established under Component 2 will be indispensable for tracking the performance and impact of SWPs in the targeted regions. In addition to ensuring the technical and managerial skills needed for implementing, operating, maintaining, and scaling up SWPs, both components also emphasize on sharing activities communicate best practices and lessons learned from Component 1’s implementation, encouraging peer learning among communities.

88. Both components underscore the significance of gender inclusion and community participation in planning, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and decision-making across varied initiatives, and Component 2 integrates these considerations into policy development, financial mechanisms, and governance structures.

#### Component 1: Community Resilience

89. This component focuses on enhancing water access and agricultural productivity in drought-prone areas through the deployment of Solar Water Pumping (SWP) systems. It aims to install a minimum of 100 solar-powered submersible pumps in the Koba-Grana Valley and the Borena Zone, addressing the critical need for reliable water supply services in these regions. The component also includes the installation of new water infrastructure and testing water quality to ensure safe drinking water. This initiative will be implemented through a community-driven approach that involves local communities and supporting the irrigation needs of around 4500 smallholder farmers. In addition to infrastructure development, Component 1 will provide training for women’s groups on water management practices and supporting the irrigation needs of around 4500 smallholder farmers.

90. The component also introduces smart water metering systems to monitor and manage water usage efficiently in both urban and rural areas. This initiative is expected to reduce water wastage, especially in arid and regions facing water scarcity. The project includes developing fair and sustainable water tariffs, ensuring equitable access for all users, and promoting the use of water-efficient technologies among members. The comprehensive approach of Component 1 not only addresses immediate water needs but also builds long-term resilience against agricultural productivity, food security, and overall community resilience to climate change.

#### Output 1.1: Solar Water Pumps Installed

91. The installation of Solar Water Pumps (SWPs) aims to enhance water accessibility in drought-prone regions of Ethiopia, focusing on sustainable and renewable energy solutions. This output includes rehabilitating existing SWPs that are not functioning properly or have been abandoned due to lack of maintenance. The installation of high-quality solar PV systems and submersible pumps will provide reliable water supply for communities living in these areas. By promoting the use of solar-powered water pumps, it reduces the community’s dependence on traditional, less sustainable energy sources, contributing to environmental sustainability and resilience against climate change.

### Activity 1.1: Rehabilitation of Wells and Testing of Water Quality (Executing Entity-MOWR)

This activity will begin with a comprehensive assessment and rehabilitation of existing wells in the target communities, followed by testing of water quality to ensure that the rehabilitated wells are safe for drinking and functional for long-term use. The rehabilitation will include the installation of new well liners, clearing of debris from the well head, cleaning of the well casing, and ensuring that the well is properly sealed after structural rehabilitation. Rigorous water quality testing will be conducted to ensure that the water meets safety standards before it can be used by communities.

### Sub-activities:

* Conduct a comprehensive assessment of existing wells and water sources.

### Activity 1.1.2: Procurement and Installation of Solar PV Systems, Submersible Pumps, and Irrigation Systems (Executing Entity: MOVE and MOIL)

#### Objectives:
- Ensure the project's sustainability by tendering maintenance contracts to responsive firms.
- Upgrade successful governance practices from the Kono Group Initiative (KGI) for local communities.

#### Key Activities:
1. **Procurement**: Select suppliers based on site visits, ensuring they meet quality standards.
2. **Installation**: Install solar PV systems and submersible pumps in a phased manner.
3. **Training**: Conduct training sessions to ensure proper use of equipment and maintenance practices.

#### Expected Outcomes:
- Improved water management through the installation of solar-powered irrigation systems.
- Enhanced agricultural productivity and resilience, particularly for smallholder farmers.
- Sustainable energy solutions that reduce reliance on traditional, less sustainable sources.

#### Partnerships:
- Ministry of Water Resources (MOWR)
- Ministry of Irrigation and Livestock Industries (MOLI)

#### Roles and Responsibilities:
- **MOVE**: Responsible for procurement and installation of solar photovoltaic systems, submersible pumps, and other related infrastructure.
- **MOIL**: Will lead training programs for local communities on sustainable water management practices.

#### Additional Information:
- The project will work closely with contractors during the implementation phase to ensure quality standards are met.
- MOIL will be responsible for logistics coordination and facilitating the establishment of a regional water distribution zone, drawing from successful governance practices from the Kono Group Initiative. This includes drafting and implementing agreements among users on shared resources.

#### Expected Benefits:
- Improved water management through solar-powered irrigation systems.
- Enhanced agricultural productivity and resilience in targeted regions.
- Sustainable energy solutions that reduce reliance on traditional sources.
- Training for local communities to ensure proper use of equipment and maintenance practices, including the use of smart meters for water accounting and reporting.

### Activity 1.1.3: Operations and Maintenance of Solar Water Pump and Water Supply Infrastructure (Distribution Pipelines)
103 Sustainable operation and maintenance (O&M) of the solar water pumps and associated infrastructure will be ensured through a comprehensive O&M plan that includes regular inspections, preventive maintenance, and prompt repairs. The project will train local technicians to carry out these tasks independently.
104 To support these efforts, an O&M manual will be developed and distributed among the community. This manual will provide step-by-step instructions on how to operate and maintain the solar water pumps.

Additionally, the project will establish a local support network, possibly involving partnerships with nearby technical institutions or organizations that can provide ongoing assistance in case of complex issues.
The network will help in addressing more complex issues that the local technicians might not be equipped to handle. The project will also ensure that all community members are aware of and have access to the latest technologies and best practices, ensuring that the water supply systems remain efficient and reliable.

The O&M plan will include a comprehensive maintenance schedule for regular inspections and repairs during daily operations, conduct routine maintenance, and address any technical issues that may arise with the solar water pumps. The project will train local technicians in these tasks to ensure sustainability by gaining a clear framework for managing the water resources sustainably.
They will be equipped with the knowledge of how to operate and maintain the solar water pumps and associated infrastructure systems, troubleshooting common issues, and coordinating repairs when necessary. Additionally, the association will provide ongoing support through regular visits from project staff or local technicians.

The system fee system coupled with phased-in tariffs for connection fees and maintenance costs will ensure efficient water distribution and minimizing wastage.
This fee system will cover the costs of O&M. This approach not only ensures the infrastructure's longevity but also fosters a sense of ownership among the community members, strengthening their capacity to manage their resources independently.

### Sub-Activities
* Develop and implement a comprehensive operations and maintenance plan.
* Conduct regular inspections and monitoring of the SWP and water supply infrastructure.
* Provide training for local technicians on system operation, maintenance procedures.
* Establish a system for recording and addressing any issues or repairs needed

## Deliverables:
* O&M manual.
* Regular maintenance logs.
* Technical support network contacts.

### Activity 1.2.4 Operation and Maintenance Manual of Water Supply Infrastructure (UGCI)
This activity will develop a comprehensive operation and maintenance manual for the water supply infrastructure, a comprehensive operation and maintenance (O&M) manual will be developed. This manual will serve as a practical guide for ISPs that are responsible for the day-to-day operations of their respective water systems. The manual will include operational procedures, routine maintenance schedules, and troubleshooting common issues. In addition to providing detailed instructions on how to operate and maintain the infrastructure, it will also highlight tasks that should be performed regularly to prevent system failures. This proactive approach will help extend the lifespan of the infrastructure and reduce the need for costly repairs.

### Sub-activities:
- Develop and implement a comprehensive operations and maintenance plan for the water supply infrastructure.
- Conduct regular inspections and monitoring of the SWP and water supply infrastructure (UGCI 2.3).
- Provide training to ISPs on the operation, maintenance, and management of their respective systems.
- Establish a system for recording and addressing any issues or repairs needed in a timely manner.

## Deliverables:
- O&M manual.
- Training session reports.
- Statistical data and follow-up support contacts.

### Output 1.3 Gender-Sensitive Irrigation User Associations (IUAs)/Water Use Associations (WUAs) Established and Capacitated
The establishment of gender-sensitive IUAs/WUAs is a key component of the project, aimed at promoting inclusive governance of water resources. These associations are structured to ensure equal representation of men and women in decision-making processes and to promote greater involvement of women. By drafting and approving by-laws that mandate minimum participation levels for women as a priority, this initiative enhances gender inclusivity, creating a more equitable decision-making process.

The project will focus on the following aspects:
- Strengthening governance.
- Financial management.
- Technical aspects of water resource management.
- Water user association (WUA) capacity building to ensure that members are equipped with the necessary skills and knowledge to manage water resources effectively. Special emphasis will be placed on building the capacity of women, enabling them to participate fully in decision-making processes.

The establishment of IUAs/WUAs is critical for the sustainability of water resources, ensuring that water use is regulated and conflicts are minimized. By involving a diverse group of stakeholders, including women, men, youth, and other vulnerable groups, this initiative ensures that the needs and concerns of community members are considered. This output enhances community cohesion and supports the achievement of project objectives by promoting equitable access to water resources.

To achieve these outputs, the following activities will be implemented:
- Establishing IUAs/WUAs.
- Developing gender-sensitive by-laws for IUAs/WUAs.
- Providing training on governance, financial management, and technical aspects of water resource management.

## Activity 1.3.1: Facilitate the Formation of IUAW/UWA with a Focus on Gender Inclusion (MGL)

## Activity 1.3.2: Draft and Approve By-laws Mandating Minimum Participation Levels for Women (MOWE and MGL)

To institutionalize gender inclusivity within the IUAW/UWA, the project will draft and approve by-laws that mandate minimum participation levels for women. These by-laws will set clear guidelines on how to ensure equal representation of men and women in leadership positions and decision-making structures.

The process of drafting the by-laws will be participatory, involving consultations with community members, including women leaders, to ensure they are culturally sensitive and practically feasible. Once drafted, the by-laws will be reviewed and approved by the membership through a democratic voting process. The project team will emphasize the importance of these rules and garner support from both men and women in the community.

The by-laws will include specific provisions for women’s participation at various levels within the IUAW/UWA, including leadership positions (at least 33% leadership position for women) and decision-making processes. Additionally, the project team will establish mechanisms to ensure accountability and compliance with the by-laws, such as appointing gender focal points or committees within the associations.

These measures will help create a more inclusive and representative governance structure.

**Output 1.4: Small Scale Revolving Fund Facility Established**

120 The project will establish a small-scale revolving fund facility aimed at enhancing financial inclusion and support community members in accessing funding for agriculture and ISPs for O&M costs. The project collaborates with existing microfinance institutions (MFIs) to provide revolving funds to IUAs/WUAs, as described in section 8.4 of the proposal. These funds will be used by IUAs/WUAs to purchase high-yield agricultural inputs and equipment, which are then sold at a profit. The project also collaborates with cooperative financial institutions designed to provide financial services to rural communities, particularly those that do not have access to formal banking systems. These institutions will provide revolving credit facilities to rural members, facilitating access to financial resources for agricultural and small-scale business development.

The project will establish revolving funds in accordance with the guidelines of the Microfinance Act No 965 (2018) and operate on democratic principles where each member has one vote, regardless of the amount invested. The revolving fund facility will be managed by a committee consisting of members who provide training to IUAs/WUAs through the RUSAICOD on financial literacy and management skills.

The project aims to enhance the financial resilience of target communities by providing access to finance for productive investments. It enables community members to invest in agriculture and other activities, thereby improving their livelihoods and contributing to sustainable development. The project also empowers women and marginalized groups through the establishment of revolving funds, which is crucial for long-term economic stability and growth.

The project will significantly enhance the economic resilience of the target communities by providing access to finance for productive investments. It enables community members to invest in agriculture and other activities, thereby improving their livelihoods and contributing to sustainable development. The project also empowers women and marginalized groups through the establishment of revolving funds, which is crucial for long-term economic stability and growth.

By supporting the implementation of the revolving fund facility, the project will contribute to a culture of financial management, which is crucial for long-term economic stability and growth. By supporting the implementation of the revolving fund facility, the project will contribute to a culture of financial management, which is crucial for long-term economic stability and growth.

### To ensure that the funds are used for their intended purposes after the GCF exits the project, several measures have been proposed:
* The Cooperative Promotion Desk situated in each IUAs/WUAs office will be responsible for assisting in providing auditing, inspection, and supervisory roles once they are established. Additionally, the RUSAICOD will provide technical assistance to IUAs/WUAs on financial management.
* The revolving fund facility will also ensure and enforce implementation of the bylaws. The project will also establish a revolving fund committee that will be responsible for monitoring market conditions, such as inflation or economic challenges, during project implementation or after. These measures will allow the interest rates to be adjusted based on market conditions and other development partners' requirements.
* Regarding the replenishment of the fund at attracting additional local financial sources, the project design includes a mechanism for maintaining the revolving fund's continuity through regular contributions from operating costs, and potential revenue streams. There are also plans to establish mechanisms for monitoring and evaluating the performance of IUAs/WUAs in order to ensure that they can maintain the fund's continuity and sustainability.

### Action 2.6: Revolving Fund Management and Administration Guideline/Manual (MOU)

137. To ensure the revolving fund is adequately capitalized, a dedicated account will be set aside by the CDFE to hold funds from the Green Climate Fund (GCF). This initial capitalization will provide the necessary funds to start disbursing the revolving fund to community members and other stakeholders in the GCF project areas of Kano, Jigawa, Kaduna, and Osun states. This initial capitalization will also allow the CDFE to explore opportunities to leverage additional funding for the revolving fund, such as contributions from local governments, private sector partners, and other development partners. The dedicated account will help build the fund's capital base and enable it to provide more funds to a larger number of community members by securing additional resources over time.

138. Two RUSACCOs could be formed through World level Cooperative Development Plans as per the CDFE’s guidelines for the project areas of Kano, Jigawa, Kaduna, and Osun states with the same purpose. The disbursed finance will be disbursed to these RUSACCOs, in a form of a revolving fund, which will be managed by the respective RUSACCOs. In addition, two separate RUSACCOs could be formed for Kano Girra Valley and Bode zone; one will open a separate bank account to administer the revolving fund for the project area of Kano Girra Valley while another will manage the revolving fund for the project area of Bode zone.

139. Provision of revolving fund service to ISPs: As stated in the proposal (and above), the RUSACCOs will provide revolving funds to ISPs, which will be used by them to finance their operations and maintenance activities related to irrigation facilities. The ISPs should be members of the RUSACCOs in order to access finance from the revolving fund. This arrangement will ensure that the ISPs are financially viable and sustainable. The CDFE will ensure no members will not access finance from the revolving fund. A total of USD 825,000 has been managed under the revolving fund banner.

* Implement robust financial controls and auditing mechanisms to ensure transparency and accountability.
* Develop strategy on replenishing and growing the revolving fund over time.

# Output C: Digital Monitoring, Reporting, and Verification (MRV) and Payment System Established

## MRV and MO&L

The project establishes a digital MRV system to enhance the transparency and accountability of water resource management. This system includes the installation of smart meters to monitor groundwater extraction and water use in Ethiopia.

### Real-time Data

The MRV system provides real-time data on water usage, enabling more informed decision-making and efficient management of water resources. It also ensures consistency with national frameworks, ensuring coherence in data reporting and management. Training is provided for users to understand the system's functionality, how to collect and interpret its data.

### Promoting Sustainable Use

The project explores the use of the MRV data for informing better pricing structures and promoting sustainable water use and reducing wastage. The digital MRV system will be integrated with national frameworks and evidence-based decision-making processes.

### Climate Impacts

The climate impacts of supported activities, including gender responsiveness, will be measured and integrated into the project design. This includes tracking and reporting framework. MoT will commission the development and integration of digital technologies to improve water resource management in Ethiopia. Water extraction and water use will be measured through smart meters. The IT platform for tracking key impact indicators (water extraction and water use) will be developed, tested, and deployed. Smart meters enable digital payment mechanisms (enabling the use of Teleset/Ethio Telecom or comparable mobile phone service providers). Data generated by smart meters is integrated into the project design.

### Enhanced Transparency

Enhanced transparency, monitoring, and data availability will enable quality-based payments based on actual water usage. This will improve the efficiency of water usage and resulting behavioral change. The proposed digital technologies including smart meters are expected to reduce administrative overhead associated with traditional payment methods. Moreover, they will help simplify and streamline the process for users.

### Digital Technologies

Digital technologies will generate large amounts of data that can provide transparency needed for evidence-based decision-making. This will also enable better climate finance for reporting GCF supported interventions across Ethiopia. The connected digital platform will be used to collect and report data on water extraction and use, as well as the smart meters provided to each customer household. This entity faces in the local context as smart meters are connected to a central server and the collected data is available online.

### Training and Support

The project will provide training and technical support to help users adopt and use effectively. This includes training on how to collect, interpret, and report data using the digital MRV system.

### Output 2.3: Gender-Sensitive Business Models and Financial Instruments Identified

This output involves conducting a comprehensive assessment to identify gender-specific needs and barriers in accessing business models and financial instruments related to water resource management. Based on findings from the assessment, the project will develop and implement gender-sensitive business models for women in agriculture and water use. These models are designed to enhance the participation and benefit of women in these sectors by providing them with access to resources such as land, credit, and technology.

In addition to implementing these business models, the project provides training programs on financial literacy, business planning, and microfinance. The project also works closely with local banks and other financial institutions to ensure that they are sensitive to marginalized groups, empowering them to access and benefit from financial services. The project also works in collaboration with government agencies such as the Ministry of Finance and Economic Development as microfinance and credit guarantees.

The project aims to promote gender equality and economic empowerment in the target communities. By addressing gender-specific needs and barriers, the project ensures that both women and men can participate equally in business models and financial instruments related to water resource management. This inclusive business models and financial instruments also supports the broader goals of sustainable development and poverty reduction.

### Activity 2.3.1: Identify Gender-Specific Needs and Barriers in Current Business Models and Financial Instruments

This activity will involve conducting a comprehensive assessment to identify the gender-specific needs and barriers that women face when accessing current business models and financial instruments related to water resource management. The assessment will include surveys, interviews, and focus groups with both men and women participants from marginalized groups. The findings will provide valuable insights into the different roles and responsibilities of all community members.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 55

### Activity 2.4.2: Organize Training Workshops in Collaboration with Academia and Regional Bureaus

170 Based on the findings of the needs assessment, the project will organize training workshops in collaboration with academia and regional bureaus to build capacity among water resources technicians and other stakeholders with the knowledge and skills needed for the effective operation and maintenance of SWP irrigation systems. The training workshops will be organized at universities, academic institutions, technical schools, and other specialized organizations, ensuring that participants receive comprehensive training on the installation, operation, and maintenance of SWP irrigation systems. These workshops will focus on real-world applications and problem-solving. Participants will have the opportunity to work with hands-on equipment and materials during practical sessions covering topics such as system installation and maintenance. The training will also include sessions on safety and best practices, ensuring that participants are well-prepared for their roles in water resource management.

172 Sub-activities:
* Establish partnerships and collaborations with the Faculty of Technology at the Addis Ababa University, Ethiopian Water Technology Institute (EWTI) and relevant regional bureaus nationally for organizing the training workshops.
* Develop comprehensive training curricula and materials based on the identified skill requirements and job profiles.
* Engage experienced trainers and subject matter experts from EWTI, regional bureaus, and other relevant organizations.
* Organize practical, hands-on training workshops covering topics such as system installation, maintenance, and troubleshooting.
* Incorporate field visits and on-site demonstrations to ensure real-world experience for the local technicians.

Deliverables:
* Workshop materials and attendance records.
* Partnership agreements.
* Training completion certificates

### Output 2.5: Knowledge Sharing and Coordination Platform Established

173 The project will establish a knowledge-sharing and coordination platform to enhance collaboration and information exchange among stakeholders involved in water resource management. The platform is designed to facilitate the sharing of best practices, innovative solutions, and lessons learned through coordination. It includes features such as a knowledge repository, discussion forums, and collaborative tools, enabling participants to access resources, share ideas, and work together on developing and implementing innovative solutions. The project also develops a knowledge management and dissemination strategy to guide the implementation of this platform.

174 This output contributes directly to the following climate mainstreaming and policy coordination across all GoE agencies. Hence, the project aims to stimulate knowledge sharing and coordination among stakeholders involved in water resource management. This will enable the Ministry of Planning and Development (MoPD), Ethiopia's NDA) to benefit from its converging power and networks.
