# Role of National Level Stakeholders

## Ministry of Finance

The Ministry of Finance has the mandate, capacity and experience of overseeing financial management and planning for climate change projects. As an EIT to the GCF, MOF is responsible for oversight of the AE as defined in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) between GCF and MOF. As AE, MOF will secure funding from the GCF, manage the project portfolio, and ensure that all project activities are aligned with GCF. MOF, in its capacity as the AE, has overall responsibility and oversight for project preparation, project implementation, and monitoring and evaluation of projects. In addition to this role, MOF has operationalized the CRIGE Facility in order to access and mobilize climate finance from bilateral and multilateral development partners. The CRIGE Facility is a key component of Ethiopia’s strategy to scale up from the GRISE Strategy, GCF, NAP and the Ten Years Development Plan. The CRIGE Facility, which is a division within MOF, has been established with the aim of providing technical and financial support to implementing Ministries including Ministry of Agriculture, Ministry of Water and Energy, Ministry of Industry and Trade, and other agencies in Ethiopia. In addition, the CRIGE Facility also works with Logistics, Ministry of Trade and industry and other agencies. For this project, the Ministry of Irrigation and Livestock (MIL) is the implementing agency for the GCF project. The MOF has signed a separate Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) with both MIL at the initial stage of the project implementation. Under the MoU, MOF will provide technical and financial support to MIL in order to engage with private sector stakeholders and other non-state actors who are working on climate change mitigation and adaptation projects. The CRIGE Facility is also responsible for providing technical assistance related tasks with regard to this GCF project. MOF is currently leading the implementation of a GCF approved project titled “Scaling up Water Resources Management in Ethiopia: Strengthening oversight quality and quality assurance of MOWE and MIL as EEs receiving GCF funds”. MOF, MOWE and MIL are working together on this project with the aim to scale up water resources management finance. MOWE is one of the Ees of the ongoing GCF project (i.e., see small project grant approved in 2016). The CRIGE Facility has also been instrumental in supporting the scaling-up efforts for the most vulnerable communities” that targets different intervention sites in Ethiopia, and provides excellent experience to support the implementation of this project. In addition, MOF is currently leading the implementation of a GCF approved project titled “Scaling up Water Resources Management in Ethiopia: Strengthening oversight quality and quality assurance of MOWE and MIL as EEs receiving GCF funds”. MOF, MOWE and MIL are working together on this project with the aim to scale up water resources management finance. MOWE is one of the Ees of the ongoing GCF project (i.e., see small project grant approved in 2016). The CRIGE Facility has also been instrumental in supporting the scaling-up efforts for the most vulnerable communities” that targets different intervention sites in Ethiopia, and provides excellent experience to support the implementation of this project.

MOWE is currently managing projects such as “Access to Distributed Electricity and Lighting in Ethiopia with a budget of USD 10.5 million”, “Water Supply and Sanitation Project in Ethiopia with a budget of USD 238 million” and “Second Urban Water Supply and Sanitation with a budget of USD 463 million”. Ethiopia Geological Energy Project (EGEP) is another project that MOWE has been managing with a budget of USD 1.3 billion and 130 million”. During the past decade, this entity has lead and coordinated the implementation of several projects in various sectors including water resources management, energy, agriculture, health and education that have major national significance. Currently, this entity is implementing projects such as “Lowlands Livelihoods Improvement Project” (LLIP) with a budget of USD 105 million.

The CRIGE Facility will assign dedicated technical and financial project staff at the various tiers of government to support the implementation of GCF projects. The CRIGE Facility will also provide technical reports, conduct monitoring and evaluation and ensure compliance to the environmental, gender and social impact assessments (EGSIA) requirements as well as other relevant regulations. In addition, the Manual, will develop a Project Implementation Manual (PM) under component 2) that elaborates fund management procedures and processes for GCF projects. The CRIGE Facility will also ensure that MIL and MOWE are aligned with the PM in implementing project.

The CRIGE Facility/Ministry of Finance as the Direct Access Entity manages a number of GCF and AfDB projects. In addition, the Ministry of Finance is responsible for ensuring that the CRIGE Facility and Ministry of Finance has the need to create a resource center for learning and knowledge management between the various implementing agencies. The CRIGE Facility will also provide technical support to the Coordination Team with regards to its knowledge management functions.

The Ministry of Irrigation and Livestock (MIL) is a critical government institution responsible for the development and implementation of irrigation projects in Ethiopia. The MIL has extensive experience and capacity in managing large scale irrigation and livestock development

The Ministry has successfully implemented numerous initiatives aimed at improving water resources management in Ethiopia, particularly focusing on irrigation projects in lowland regions. These projects require comprehensive planning, coordination with various stakeholders, and a strong focus on sustainable land use practices and integrated water resource management capabilities. Examples include the Lower Omo Irrigation Project and the Pastoral Community Development Project.

The Lower Omo Irrigation Project is financed by the World Bank, with a budget of approximately US$ 180 million. This project aims to develop large-scale irrigation infrastructure in the Lower Omo Valley, enhancing agricultural productivity and food security for local communities. The Pastoral Community Development Project, with a budget of around US$ 350 million, also funded by the World Bank, focuses on improving water access, promoting sustainable land management, and enhancing livelihoods among pastoral communities.

The Ministry's organizational structure includes a Minister, supported by various departments such as Water Resources Development and Water Management. Various directorates and departments within the Ministry focus on specific areas of water resources development and management.

This structure enables a strategic approach to addressing the unique challenges of irrigation and lowland areas in Ethiopia. The Pastoral Community Development Project, for example, is implemented through the Lower Omo Irrigation Project. Moll, employs a skilled workforce of engineers, agronomists, water resource experts, environmental scientists, and other professionals who are responsible for implementing various projects.

Collaboration with international organizations such as the World Bank provides access to expertise and best practices, further strengthening the Ministry's human resource capabilities. Additionally, the Ministry has invested in Geographic Information Systems (GIS), remote sensing technologies, and modern irrigation equipment, which support its efforts in water resources management.

In this particular GCF project, Moll is responsible for building and developing irrigation assets extracting water from the Omo River. The project will be implemented through a Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) with other stakeholders at levels (federal, regional and local levels) at all times. Moll will be solely responsible for implementing the project as per the approved Implementation Plan (Procurement Plan). Moll will also be responsible for anchoring the GCF supported interventions within the National Water Sector Development Strategy (NWSDS) and ensuring that these are reflected in tariff-setting (see above). All the relevant departments within Moll will be engaged in the project delivery. A specific team of experts from Moll will be assigned to provide technical support and guidance on the project delivery focusing on the gender action plan.

**Water Resources Development**

The Ministry is responsible for planning, allocation, and provision of water and groundwater resources across sectors including hygiene and electrification. This ministry has ministerial responsibility for ensuring that water resources are managed in a manner that contributes greater socio-economic and strategic significance to the country. The Ethiopia Urban Water Supply and Sanitation Project (EUWSSP) is an example of such initiatives, funded by the World Bank and the European Union. The project aims to improve the provision of urban water supply services through the construction of new water supply systems in 10 cities across Ethiopia.

Another initiative is the Electrification Program (EELP), also funded by the World Bank, with a budget of approximately US$ 445 million. This project seeks to improve the provision of electricity services and increase access to energy for rural communities. The Lower Omo Irrigation Project is another example of such initiatives, focusing on improving water resources management in lowland regions where most of the population resides and where electricity coverage is typically low.

The Second Urban Water Supply and Sanitation Project (SUWSSP) aims to improve urban water supply services by increasing capacity to manage large-scale projects. This project focuses on enhancing the efficiency and sustainability of water supply systems, particularly in urban areas with high demand for water resources. The Ministry's collaboration with the World Bank and Ethiopia's proven track record in successfully managing complex, multi-million-dollar initiatives has significant socio-economic impacts.

### Relevant Rules, Regulations, and Permits for Implementing Project Activities in Ethiopia

185. Implementing project activities in Ethiopia, particularly those involving the use of groundwater, establishing public water collection points, setting up and collecting water user fees, and importing solar panels and Rural Savings and Credit Cooperatives (RUSCCOs), requires adherence to a comprehensive set of rules, regulations, and permits.

#### Groundwater Use Permits: The use of groundwater in Ethiopia is regulated primarily by the Water Resources Development and Management Act 2015. This Act provides for the establishment of a permit system for managing, utilizing, and protecting the country's water resources. Under this law, any individual or entity wishing to extract groundwater must obtain a permit from the relevant regional government. The permit application process involves submitting detailed information about the purpose, volume, and method of extraction, as well as an environmental impact assessment (EIA) for large-scale projects and an environmental management plan (EMP) for smaller projects.

#### Establishment of Public Water Collection Points: To establish public water collection points, several regulations must be followed. These include obtaining a permit from the relevant regional government, ensuring that the water collection point meets essential health and safety standards to prevent contamination and provide clean drinking water, and adhering to national guidelines on water quality and treatment for public water collection points. This process includes obtaining construction permits from the local municipal or urban administration office, complying with national and international hygiene and sanitation guidelines and accessibility standards, and undergoing regular inspections and maintenance as required by local regulations.

#### Setting Up and Collecting Water User Fees: Setting up and collecting water user fees are governed by Water Services Act 2015. This act provides guidance to ensure that water service pricing is fair, transparent, and affordable. To establish a fee system, the project must comply with national and local water authority. This involves setting up a transparent billing system in compliance with national and local regulations and guidelines. Regular reports are essential to ensure compliance with tariff regulations and prevent exploitation of consumers.


---
**Page 64**
---

# Importing Solar Panels: Importing solar panels into Ethiopia requires adherence to several laws and regulations. The Customs Proclamation No. 859/2014 outlines the procedures and requirements for importers of goods. These include obtaining a license from the Ministry of Trade and Industry, ensuring that the energy sector and the innovation of renewable energy technologies. Importers must obtain permits from the Ministry of Trade and Industry to ensure that the solar panels meet quality standards set by the Ethiopian Standards Agency (ESA). The ESA also has the authority to apply applicable import duties and taxes unless specific renewable energy incentives exempt them.

## Rural Savings and Credit Cooperatives (RUSCOS): The regulation of Rural Savings and Credit Cooperatives is governed by Proclamation No. 147/1996, which provides the legal basis for their formation, registration, operation, and cooperatives. The National Bank of Ethiopia (NBE) has established a regulatory framework to ensure that RUSCOS adhere to financial practices and safeguard members' savings. RUSCOS must register with the Cooperative Agency of the NBE and comply with the guidelines set by the NBE. These guidelines aim to combat money laundering (AML) and combat the financing of terrorism.

### Roles and Responsibilities of Sub-national Stakeholders

#### 1 Regional Level Stakeholders: At the level of governance, Regional Bureau of Finance and Economic Development (RB-FED) is responsible for overseeing the implementation of water resources projects at Woreda level. RB-FED transfers them over to regional irrigation or water bureaus that are responsible for oversight and coordination with other stakeholders in the project area.

#### 2 Woreda Level Stakeholders: At the Woreda level, an expert within each of the 23 irrigated Woredas will be designated as a Project Officer (PO) who is responsible for overseeing the implementation of projects at Woreda level offices. The POs are responsible for activities, which shall be executed by the Woreda-level irrigation officers. The Woreda Irrigation Office (WIO) is responsible for implementing and monitoring the project in collaboration with the PO.

#### 3 Project Implementation: At the Woreda level, the implementation of projects will be carried out solely on the capacities of regional or woreda level irrigation bureaus. In both regions one Project Officer will be appointed to oversee the implementation of the project at the Woreda level. The WIOs are responsible for oversight of project execution at Woreda levels. The existing team in the regional sector bureau, including the Water Office and Mobile Community Extension Team (MCET), will continue to support the WIOs.

#### 4 Project Monitoring: At the Woreda level, a monitoring system will be established with a PO who will be employed under this project. Regular visits will tenders to attend specialized companies bidding for the construction of irrigation projects. ## Project Governance and Coordination Arrangement

### 195. **CRGE Facility Management Committee**

The CRGE Facility Management Committee, which is chaired by MoFED will govern and oversee the implementation of this project. All sector ministers involved in the CRGE strategy and implementation will be members of the committee.

- The Minister for Water Resources and Irrigation and the Minister for Energy and Minerals are the chairpersons of the CRGE Facility Management Committee.
- The Minister level and high-level technical support from the civil service (Senior technical expert from the CRGE Strategy Implementation Unit) will also be a member of this committee.
- UNDP and UNDP are members of the CRGE Facility Management Committee as an observer. The Committee meets at least once every six months to review progress, provide strategic guidance and direction to the project stakeholders.

The Committee also resolves any disputes that arise among the implementing sectors and provides overall guidance and oversight on the financial and operational performance, resolves disputes among executing entities, and facilitates stakeholder engagement in the implementation of this project. The committee will also develop capacity-building initiatives to enhance the skills and knowledge of implementing entities and stakeholders involved in the project.

### 196. **Regional Project Steering Committee**

The Regional Project Steering Committee (RPSG) will coordinate implementation of the project. The Bureau of Irrigation and Lowlands Development as well as the Bureau of Water and Energy Planning and Development are responsible for coordinating the project at the regional level, and they will be bestowed on them. A regional project steering committee co-chaired by the Bureau of Irrigation and Lowlands Development and the Bureau of Water and Energy Planning and Development oversees implementation of the project in Amhara and Oromia Regional States.

The Regional Project Steering Committee supervises project implementation within the Amhara and Oromia Regional States. The RPSG will ensure that the project is implemented in a participatory manner among regional bureaux and local governments, and ensures effective stakeholder participation. It conducts regular meetings to review progress of the project, provides strategic guidance and direction, promoting community engagement and capacity-building efforts to enhance local governance and project sustainability.

### 198. Rural Savings and Credit Cooperatives: Rural Savings and Credit Cooperatives have become a cornerstone of rural financial systems in Ethiopia, particularly in areas where formal banking services are not available or accessible to smallholder farmers, rural entrepreneurs, and low-income households. Supported by government initiatives such as the Cooperative Promotion Agency (CPA), these cooperatives provide credit and savings products that enable members to manage their finances effectively.

Their growth is driven by the recognition that traditional banking models often do not meet the needs of rural communities, particularly in terms of providing access to financial services for smallholder farmers, rural entrepreneurs, and low-income households. These cooperatives play a pivotal role in promoting social cohesion and community development. By pooling resources and sharing risks among members, they foster a sense of mutual responsibility and mutual support. This social capital is particularly valuable in rural areas where formal banking systems are often absent or inaccessible.

Rural Savings and Credit Cooperatives also serve as platforms for education and capacity building, offering training in business management, agricultural practices, and financial literacy. Through these initiatives, members not only become financially empowered but are also equipped with the skills and knowledge needed to improve their livelihoods and contribute positively to community development.

A critical function of RUSACOCOS is managing revolving funds, which are essential for sustaining long-term community development projects. These funds provide members with access to financial resources


---
**Page 67**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 64

C

For productive investments, such as purchasing agricultural inputs like seeds, fertilizers, and livestock.

The revolving fund operates on a system where loans are repaid and then re-lent to other members, including the purchase of inputs for their own use. This ensures that financial resources are efficiently circulated within the community. By administering the revolving fund, PULADA is able to provide access to credit for smallholder farmers who adopt climate-resilient agricultural practices and renewable energy technologies such as solar water pumps. PULADA also provides technical assistance in the form of training workshops on sustainable land management, ensuring that it serves as a reliable source of capital for ongoing and future development initiatives.

Through this revolving fund, PULADA aims to reduce poverty, promote sustainable livelihoods in Ethiopia's rural areas.


### Local Governance and Implementation Arrangements

Local governance and implementation arrangements are set up by the Water Users' Association. These arrangements include the establishment of a steering committee responsible for managing and maintaining project assets such as solar water pumping systems.

The arrangements also address conflicts, develop sustainability plans, and ensure the active involvement of local communities in planning, implementing, monitoring, and evaluating project activities. Local governance and implementation arrangements will be set up in a way that prioritizes local ownership and ensures long-term viability through clear responsibilities for maintenance, irrigation and usable assets. WUA has a longstanding tradition and legal standing in Ethiopia.

The central role of the Water Users' Association (WUA) is to ensure the sustainability of water resources by providing governance and use of assets. These clearly defined responsibilities are key to achieving long-term viability between generations. The WUA will be responsible for maintenance, irrigation, and usable assets. This has been successfully piloted as part of the CREMAM project that operates several pilot sites in Ethiopia focusing on micro-irrigation systems.

The arrangements also include the establishment of a steering committee responsible for managing and maintaining project assets such as solar water pumping systems.


### Figure 9: Local governance and implementation arrangements

**Figure 9: Local governance and implementation arrangements**

203. The CRGE Facility will be implementing an existing public finance flow arrangement which is known as "Channel one fund flow arrangement" in which MCF transfers money received from the GCF to the relevant national government account. These funds are then transferred by the national government to the WUA accounts deposited in designated CQF accounts of federal institutions as well as GCF accounts of the Bureau of Finance and Economic Development (BFED) at the World Bank. The BFED is a member of the World Bank's Bureau of Finance and Economic Development (WOFED) account at the World Bank on a regular basis. MCF has been applying this funding mechanism for several years, and it is expected that the CRGE Facility will continue to use the same channel; only in the reverse direction. The utilization of funds will be monitored through an internal audit system by the BFED. Funds received from the GCF are transferred directly into the WUA accounts received by a project account at the CRGE Facility are then channeled through the government structure - federal, regional and woreda levels - to the water users' associations for implementation.

## One Fund Disbursement and Financial Reporting system is the standard disbursement and financial reporting arrangement in Ethiopia.

**204. Federal Level:** MOF directly transfers funds to MILL and MOVIE for activities, which they directly implement (e.g., purchase of seeds, inputs, etc.). The Federal level does not only receive and retain funds for activities that they directly execute (e.g., purchase of assets). Forwarded funds notably include those for the implementation of projects at the regional and sub-regional levels.

**205. Regional Level:** The fund for regional and sub-regional level activities shall be channeled to the Bureau of Irrigation and Water Resources (BIWR) or the Bureau of Economic and Social Development (BESD) or the Bureau of Water and Energy, Bureau of Finance and Economic Development among others. These will have direct access to funds for irrigation and water resources development projects at the regional level and economic level.

### Project site and beneficiary selection

The project sites and beneficiaries include MILL, MOIE, MOIL, Regional Bureau of Irrigation, Regional Bureau of Water and Energy, Bureau of Finance and Economic Development among others. These will have direct access to funds for irrigation and water resources development projects at the regional level and economic level.

The selection criteria shall be developed on a mutually agreed selection criteria. The selection criteria shall be developed with active participation of the stakeholders involved in the project implementation process. The selection criteria should be launched before the project launch. The primary selection criterion for villages in this project is the presence of high discharge streams previously identified by the Ethiopian Government currently capped due to resource constraints. These villages are specifically targeted for the following reasons:


---
**Page 69**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 66

* Existing Infrastructure: The presence of already-drilled boreholes represents a significant opportunity for immediate impact with additional investment.
* Resilience and Adaptation: By selecting villages, the project can maximize the use of existing resources and avoid duplication of efforts.
* High Community Ownership: Selected communities have the capacity to benefit a large portion of the community once operational.
* Demonstrable Impact: The project offers an excellent opportunity to showcase how sustainable energy solutions can enhance community adaptive capacities.
* Sectoral Synergies: The project has strong sectoral synergies and can serve as a model for similar interventions across Ethiopia and potentially other African regions.

By selecting villages with these characteristics, the project aims to efficiently leverage existing infrastructure, demonstrate impact, and provide a replicable model for similar interventions.

## B.5 Justification for GCF funding request (max 1000 words; approximately 2 pages)

209. Ethiopia's requirement for significant financial support to achieve its climate and development goals necessitates a justification for grant funding from the Green Climate Fund (GCF) rather than loans or other forms of financing.

### B.5.1 Economic Financial Needs: Ethiopia requires USD 316 billion by 2030 to fully achieve its NDC/TPSP targets, which is being mobilized from our own budget contributions, as well as climate finance from the GCF and other sources. The total financial needs are estimated at USD 857 million per year for the next five years.

### B.5.2 Economic and Financial Constraints: Ethiopia is a least developed country (LDC) and is listed as a highly indebted poor country (HIPC). The country has experienced significant macro imbalances and financing challenges including a sharp decline in foreign direct investment, high interest rates, a large fiscal deficit, a high inflation rate, a high premium, and high inflation. It requires technical and financial support for the required climate interventions.

### B.5.3 External Financing: Ethiopia's external debt has increased from USD 1 billion to USD 20 billion in 2020 and is still applied for debt relief through the G-7/20 Common Framework. External financing is needed to meet the country's financial needs, which are estimated at USD 857 million per year.

### B.5.4 Economic Growth: Ethiopia faces a growing debt burden from debt service payments in the near term. The government has been unable to generate sufficient revenue to cover its debt service obligations and has had to borrow additional funds to meet these obligations. This has resulted in an increase in interest rates, which is expected to rise by 1 billion a year*, with three-quarters highlighting the severe debt burden the country faces, making additional borrowing unsustainable.

### B.5.5 Economic Support: The approval of this program is driven by the need to address significant macroeconomic imbalances and financing challenges that have been exacerbated by the COVID-19 pandemic levels. The support aim to restore macroeconomic stability through comprehensive reforms, such as moving from a fiscal deficit to a fiscal surplus, reducing debt service payments, and increasing revenue mobilization.

### B.5.6 Inflation: Since mid-2019, Ethiopia has seen skyrocketing inflation with annual rates exceeding 35% in April 2020**. The combination of COVID-19, conflict, and logistical challenges have led to a significant increase in the cost of living. This, coupled with increasing difficulty in securing foreign currency-denominated debt, the country faces severe financial instability.

### B.5.7 Currency Exchange Rates: The devaluation of the Ethiopian birr has increased the country's debt burden. New technology typically needs to be imported using funds which is a crucial

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 67

## Market and Institutional Gaps: The private sector in Ethiopia is risk-averse, especially regarding upfront investments in infrastructure projects that are not immediately profitable. This reflects the specific characteristics of the market (e.g., regarding collateral requirements). This lack of market readiness has led to a reliance on public sector financing for climate adaptation projects, which can be costly and may not always be available when needed. The government is also limited by its ability to provide new technologies without imposing additional financial risks on the private sector. Consideration has also been given to leveraging foreign direct investment (FDI) in the market and foreign direct investments (FDIs). LCDCs are, in general, not favorably appraised by international investors due to their non-revenue-generating nature.

## Public Good Nature of Climate Change Adaptation: Many climate change adaptation projects have declined from US$ 4.9 billion in 2017 to US$ 2.2 billion in 2025. The current government has sought to address this by increasing the budget for LCDCs and competing to support sustainable livelihoods.

## Alignment with international calls for grant funding for LCDCs and Climate Change Adaptation Actions: Ethiopia is currently facing a significant financing gap, which is expected to widen over time due to climate change impacts. The country's economy has been relatively high unemployment, public debt on the rise and persistent political instability, making it difficult for the government to meet its climate adaptation needs.

## Characteristics of structure for SWP: The uptake of a recently introduced technology in the Ethiopian context needs to be aligned with the specific cost structure of SWP, which is characterized by very high CAPEX investments and only moderate OPEX (see section B.5 and D.6). Hence, the government should consider providing financial incentives or subsidies for farmers to build barriers to improve innovative SWP infrastructure and design community-led implementation arrangements. This will ensure long-term sustainability of operations of SWP infrastructure, complemented by technical assistance to strengthen the enabling environment.

## Impact and Capacity Building: Grant funding from the GCF will be crucial in overcoming barriers and building capacities in rural populations to improve adaptive capacity against climate change through access to new technologies and services. The proposed project will focus on providing resources that can be used for capital expenditures, that cannot be mobilized either from government or private financial institutions. This comprehensive approach ensures the long-term sustainability of investments and development of skills and knowledge among farmers in Ethiopia. It also provides socioeconomic benefits in particular regarding the health of rural communities, which can be expressed in improved health life expectancy (HAL) at birth (see D.6), improved agricultural yields where farmers are able to use new technologies, increased resilience against climate change impacts on associated agricultural value chains (e.g., the dairy sector). Moreover, the project achieves broader socioeconomic benefits through improved access to water resources and sanitation services, improvements of rural communities, improved agricultural productivity and associated income gains from better harvests which farmers can set (see table 6), and potentially additional employment

## Opportunities for value addition in associated agricultural value chains (e.g., the dairy sector), as well as environmental benefits such as mitigation co-benefits of the key objective to strengthen adaptation and resilience, can be quantified using a range of approaches. These include economic valuation approaches to quantify these economic benefits, for instance by expressing them in Improved Health Life Expectancy terms. The project will also need to consider how to account for these benefits within the SWP framework, which may require further adjustment to the specific context of the interventions. These broader benefits combine with higher-end user-collateral in SWP as well as economic scale effects resulting from upscaled implementation and replication.

## Government Commitment to Co-finance GCF Project

The Ethiopian government has committed to co-financing the proposed GCF project, matching the GCF contribution with significant financial commitment. The Ethiopian government will provide a grant for the project, which is expected to be sufficient as justification for grant funding.

### Challenges and Constraints

Challenges related to climate change, especially droughts, enormous financial needs, economic constraints, limited access to concessional loans, inflation and foreign currency challenges, make it difficult for Ethiopia to meet its adaptation targets. The project will need to consider the nature of climate adaptation, alignment with international grant funding calls, the impact and capacity-building required in order to ensure that the project is sustainable beyond the initial grant period, a feasible alternative form of financing for Ethiopia's climate adaptation and mitigation efforts.

### Project Financing

The Ethiopian government has committed to co-finance the project through a grant rather than a loan including the following points:

- **Grant Financing**: The project will be financed by a grant. The project requires substantial capital investment for the installation and rehabilitation of solar water pumping systems and infrastructure, which is beyond the capacity of the Ethiopian government to finance. Project revenue generated is insufficient to repay a loan, especially when considering the financial capacity of the Ethiopian government.

- **Limited Cash Flows and dependency of Financial Internal Rate of Return (FIRR) on GCF grant**: The project will be financed by a grant from the GCF. The Ethiopian government's funding contribution as a recoverable investment. The respective funds are to be provided under the Ethiopia Climate Resilience Program (CRP) (Cluster 2) (See Annex B, Sheet: Summary, Table). However, despite this long-term economic benefit, the project will not generate sufficient cash flows in the short term to cover operational costs of SWP infrastructure, nor repay large capital investments. Grant financing is required for the project to be financially viable.

### Economic Benefits and Externality

The economic benefits of the project, such as improved agricultural productivity, increased water security, and reduced greenhouse gas emissions are significant but not directly monetizable in a way that would allow for loan repayment. These benefits will need to be accounted for within the SWP framework when considering grant financing. The project generates externalities, such as improved public health and reduced greenhouse gas emissions.

### Affordability and Accessibility for Beneficiaries

The local beneficiaries, primarily poor smallholder farmers in vulnerable communities, have limited access to financial services and cannot afford higher user tariffs required for a loan. The project aims to provide essential services, such as water for drinking and irrigation, at an affordable cost.

B.5 Est. строки: 102 (страница: 68)

## Project Design

The project has been designed in order to contribute to a paradigm shift in sustainable access to safe water and food security in Ethiopia for vulnerable communities. The interventions focus on pioneering community-based approaches that are scalable, replicable, and can be adapted by other smallholder farmers and business models for climate-resilient access to safe water and agriculture. The long-lived nature of the support provided will ensure that the project has a significant impact beyond its initial grant period, with benefits even after closure of the project (project duration: 7 years). The objective is to enable a smooth transition from grant funding to other forms of financing.

The project seeks to achieve the transfer of this climate technology with a combination of demonstrating the practical viability of deep well irrigation in Ethiopia and supporting the scaling up of this technology in other vulnerable rural areas at the heart of this proposed project intervention. Even diesel-powered irrigation is rare in Ethiopia, hence accelerating the deployment of GCF is a key priority for the country. The project will also support the development of an appropriate groundwater table monitoring system as a critical technology that can help rural Ethiopian communities adapt to the adverse impacts of climate change by enhancing drought resilience and improving food security.

# EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section assesses the performance of the proposed programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.

## Adaptation impacts

The project will benefit **503,296** individuals (disaggregated by gender): 472,438 (predominantly rural households), or 18% of the total population in Ethiopia. The project will also provide access to clean and sustainable water for irrigation, livestock production and potable use. This will lead to increased agricultural productivity and food security, improved health outcomes, and enhanced livelihoods. It is expected that **503,296** individuals (disaggregated by gender) will benefit from the project, resulting in employment opportunities and additional income available due to efficient water management practices.

### Adaptation benefits

The following table provides an overview of the baseline scenario, including the expected adaptation benefits and details on avoided impacts. The baseline scenario is provided in Annex 22. Adaptation beneficiaries based on the guidance in the GCF Result Handbook.

| **Beneficiaries** | **Baseline Scenario** |
|-------------------|-----------------------|
| **Total number of individuals (disaggregated by gender):** | 503,296 |

### Assets produced

The project will produce **187,400** dryland biotopes and **100** climate-resilient and emissions-neutral pumping systems powered by solar PV (60 are in Kobo-Girara, 40 are in the pastoralist-dominated Borana zone).

### Climate-resilient, diversified and sustainable agricultural production

The project particularly supports farmers who have acquired knowledge to use water for productive purposes. For this instance, it includes support to traditional pastoral communities in Oromia that live as nomads crossing borders between Ethiopia and Kenya. The project will also benefit **100** farmers who engage in modern climate-smart agricultural techniques, including agro-ecology and permaculture.

### Government and local co-operative staff trained

The project will train **SWP water, climate resilience and governance**: This includes the installation of **250** solar-powered irrigation systems implemented with regional bureaus to ensure the long-term efficiency and operation of the renewable energy-based irrigation systems.

### NDC alignment

The project will contribute to achieving Ethiopia’s adaptation targets formulated in its updated NDC and National Adaptation Plan (NAP) by 2030. In the water sector, these targets notably comprise:

- **Reduce GHG emissions**: Mitigation potentials are calculated using the Clean Development Mechanism's (CDM) methodology. The project will reduce direct GHG emissions from irrigation systems where groundwater is pumped using conventional diesel pumps.
- **Expand construction of medium and large-scale irrigation systems** to ensure food security.

### NDC mitigation

The Ethiopian updated NDC foresees a conditional mitigation target in the energy sector of an annual reduction of 4 Mt CO2e by 2025. This action directly contributes to the broader mitigation targets formulated under the Paris Agreement.


---
**Page 77**
---

D2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
The project interventions seek to address barriers that have prevented the uptake of SWP, limiting rural communities' access to water and food security in Ethiopia. The project thus enhances the resilience of agricultural productivity in increasingly drought-prone areas of rural Ethiopia through promoting SWP in a holistic approach. This will be achieved by strengthening the enabling environment for SWP at all levels, including interventions aimed at contributing to the overall goal of enabling market penetration of SWP technology well beyond the CEF-supported pilot phase. The project will also contribute to the achievement of SDG 6.5.1 and other goals related to address existential needs of the most vulnerable segments of the population. Economies of scale will be accelerated through a combination of large-scale demonstration projects, capacity building, and awareness raising.

### 231.
While actual deployment of SWP technology is component I, it will contribute to raising awareness by demonstrating its benefits in Ethiopia. The project interventions are designed to create an enabling environment for the adoption of SWP technologies at scale. This is a critical step towards a potential shift from a partial to the overall goal of a paradigm shift towards a widespread uptake of SWP technology across Ethiopia well beyond the CEF-supported pilot phase. The project will contribute to the achievement of the following components of the strengthened enabling environment into a nation-wide paradigm shift include in particular:

- Activity 2.1: Promoting awareness and raising interest through engaging stakeholders, shifting towards carbon-neutral, sustainable and inclusive water provisioning systems.
- Activity 2.2: Strengthening the enabling environment for SWP technology adoption by supporting women and persons with disabilities to participate fully in the development of their communities and in different forms of local agricultural production, including at household subsistence level, small-scale commercial and large-scale commercial levels. This will be achieved through capacity building, awareness raising, and upscaling of SWP market penetration and acceptability by local beneficiaries.
- Activity 2.3: Helps achieve scaling towards more inclusive water provisioning systems by supporting women and persons with disabilities to participate fully in the development of their communities and in different forms of local agricultural production, including at household subsistence level, small-scale commercial and large-scale commercial levels. This will be achieved through capacity building, awareness raising, and upscaling of SWP market penetration and acceptability by local beneficiaries.
- Activity 2.4: Strengthening the enabling environment for SWP technology adoption at national and regional scale, including technical to maintain and operate SWP and irrigation systems, state officials at different levels of governance to ensure that SWP is integrated into their policies and programs.

## Appropriate deployment and use of SWP and related irrigation systems.

This includes practical implementation access to finance and technical assistance will aim at establishing a national ecosystem of service providers/servants that help this technology fully establish itself in Ethiopia.

### Aims of the project
- To support the scaling up of solar water pumping (SWP) through shared learning and knowledge sharing beyond the project intervention sites, through a targeted knowledge sharing and coordination platform.
- The holistic approach considers the entire region of Ethiopia as one single market in order to leverage synergies within and beyond the (semi-)arid regions of Ethiopia as the key long-term goal.

All these components intend to prepare the ground for further replication and upscaling in a sustainable manner as is illustrated through this document.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 77

## Health Benefits:

- **Access to safe and reliable water sources:** This is fundamental in improving public health outcomes.
- **Reducing reliance on contaminated surface water and the incidence of waterborne diseases:** Approximately 65,540 households will benefit from improved clean water access.
- **Preventing the prevalence of diseases such as diarrhea, cholera, dysentery, and other water-related illnesses:** The project will help reduce the prevalence of these diseases by providing safe water to communities.
- **Reducing the burden on local health facilities and improving overall health for community members, particularly children and the elderly:** Improved access to clean water will lead to better health outcomes.

## Economic Benefits:

- **Reliance on solar pumps reduces diesel-induced air pollution, which poses serious risks to crops and livestock.** Introducing SWP enables the community to shift periodic expenses for fuel and maintenance of diesel generators to more predictable annual costs.
- **Increased access to irrigation water through SWP and improved irrigation systems:** This will lead to higher crop yields and more stable food production directly benefiting around 4,700 farmer households with new or improved irrigation systems. Improved irrigation systems will also reduce the risk of crop failure due to droughts, providing a buffer against economic shocks and climate variability.
- **Reducing greenhouse gas emissions by replacing diesel-powered pumps with solar-powered systems:** The project will reduce CO2 emissions by approximately 130 metric tons per year.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 78

### Gender-sensitive development impact

The project has developed a gender action plan to ensure women's equal opportunities to participate in planning and decision-making processes, including the use of gender-disaggregated data as key performance indicators; building the resilience of female-headed households and women in male-headed households; and addressing the needs of women and girls in accessing water resources. The project will also address the issue of child marriage by providing education for girls and boys on the dangers of early marriage.

Furthermore, this project will revise existing manuals, frameworks, plans and institutional architecture at the Wereda level to ensure that gender equality is mainstreamed into all aspects of the project's activities. This will be achieved through capacity building workshops and training sessions with key stakeholders amongst them being reduced child mortality but also the health of the entire community. Improved access to household water supply will provide women and girls with more time for education, schooling for school children and income producing activities which will unequivocally transform family life.

### B.4 Needs assessment (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

248. Ethiopia is one of the most vulnerable countries that ranks only on number 173 out of 189 countries in the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) 2019 Human Development Index. As an LIC, Ethiopia has a high vulnerability to climate change and its impacts. The country's economy depends heavily on rain-fed agriculture, which is highly vulnerable to climatic variability and extreme weather events such as droughts and floods. In addition, the country faces increasing competition for water resources due to population growth, urbanization, industrialization, and increased demand from energy production (e.g., hydropower generation). Furthermore, this status stipulates special treatment under the UNFCCC and the Paris Agreement.

249. With regard to climate change impacts on water availability in Ethiopia, the following are some of the major challenges: a) The country is highly dependent on rainfall for its water supply; b) Fuel prices - increasingly unaffordable diesel generators** especially in remote rural areas where only 18% of households are connected to electricity; c) Water quality - poor water quality due to pollution and contamination, with over 90% of the rural population do not have access to safe water for drinking and sanitation. People continue to suffer from waterborne diseases such as cholera, typhoid fever, dysentery, malaria, and other waterborne diseases. Climate impacts on water supply therefore negatively affect agricultural productivity and food security in Ethiopia.

250. The access to clean drinking water varies within and between regions but robust and spatially disaggregated data are not available at the Woreda level. This is due to the lack of a national database for water resources (see Table 6). The feasibility study finds that while in the Kobo Gura target area in the Amhara region nearly all drinking water sources are contaminated, in the Tigray region only about half of the drinking water sources are in need of improving drinking water provisioning systems (see Annex 2); In the latter - close to 60% rural households can be provided with improved drinking water sources at a cost of $1.5 billion, which would provide access for 230,000 individuals. Indeed, accelerating universal access to water and sanitation increases climate resilience by shifting from reliance on unreliable surface water sources to more resilient sources is a key Goal priority that benefits the most vulnerable populations.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 79

## 251. **Health and nutrition needs**: The main problem is a significant private and public funding gap (Annex B, section 2). This gap is linked to high population growth which partly offsets gains made to improve rural livelihoods regarding food security, health, and education. The need to select the suggested intervention areas is further underpinned by the high vulnerability of these communities (see Annex 2) and their poverty levels (Annex B).

### 252. Further, Ethiopia's pre-existing economic and environmental situation underscores the urgent need for comprehensive interventions. With ongoing droughts reducing livestock numbers and food security, migration and food aid dependency have surged. The Beleg agricultural season faces a 16% reduction in suitable areas, intensifying food insecurity (Annex B). In addition to this, Ethiopia's power sector is heavily reliant on hydropower, which is vulnerable to climate change. Electricity generated entirely from diesel for water pumping remains accessible to 70% of the population. Ethiopia risks losing up to 25% of its electricity generation capacity by 2030 due to climate change impacts (Annex B). The country's adaptation and mitigation capacities are limited, particularly in areas located in deserts and semi-deserts, suffering from significant rainfall deficits.

### 254. The interventions are fully in line with the priorities of Ethiopia's updated NDC, 10IPG, and key GGC sectoral plans. They aim to enhance resilience by improving agricultural productivity through modern irrigation as top priorities. The feasibility study for this project delineates an entire value chain approach that includes water resources management, climate change adaptation, and mitigation.

### 256. Ethiopia's updated NDC prioritizes adaptation in its vision of becoming a climate-resilient middle-income country by 2030. This vision accordingly aims to increase resilience by enhancing food security by improving agricultural productivity in the most vulnerable areas through modern irrigation systems (Annex B). The focus is on drought-prone areas. The plan focuses on enhancing water availability using small-scale wind and solar power, rainwater harvesting, and improved water management practices for livestock, crops, and irrigation.

### 257. Ethiopia's updated NDC envisages mobilizing the required climate finance from domestic, public, and private sources (Annex B). The country's updated NDC also prioritizes actions such as expansion of irrigation services operated with clean energy.

### 258. The 10IPG is a key component of Ethiopia's national strategy for sustainable development, which includes ambitious resilience and mitigation strategies in its CRIDE and the updated NDC. The CRIDE was already fully aligned with the GGGI's 10IPG. The 10IPG aims to enhance climate change adaptation and mitigation by 2030. The new 10IPG flagship formulates new targets for all sectors until 2030, including the priority target of freeing agricultural land from water scarcity through improved irrigation systems in drought-prone areas and vulnerable communities.

### 259. MOHME has formulated a climate resilience strategy for water, irrigation, and energy, which mentions groundwater recharge as one of its key strategies (Annex B). The strategy also highlights the importance of Ethiopia's multi-sector investment plan, developed with support from the Pilot Programme for Climate Resilience, also prioritize access to irrigation, safe water, and sustainable energy***(for more info, please see Annex 2)***.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 PAGE 80

## Ethiopia has also developed an irrigation development and expansion strategy.

### As part of these resilience strategies, CGIAR has set out targets for universal access to safe water, increasing agricultural productivity, and access to electricity in the region, with a focus on smallholder farmers and rural communities. These goals set a minimum service level of 25 liters per person per day (l/c/d) within 1 km from the water source.

### The project will support the development of irrigation infrastructure that can be used for both crop production and of pork and livestock can be enhanced by supporting strategic crops and livestock breeds with modern technologies. Smallholder farmers are to be supported with resilient water access.

### The proposed National Irrigation Fund (NIF) will be established as a public-private partnership with a multi-stakeholder board, managed by CGIAR. It will provide grants and loans for irrigation infrastructure development from USAID, GIZ, and other donors, and partners both in terms of funding and technical support. The fund is to be governed according to the rules set out in the MoIPD, including application of standard criteria, consistent planning and design of schemes, and adherence to various best practices.

### Ethiopia has identified 176 sites which equal over 5 million ha to be irrigated under the GTP. These sites are located in areas with high potential for irrigation development, but also have a high risk of surface water, which are more vulnerable to climate impacts than groundwater. The CGIRE undertakes the design and implementation of pilot projects using solar water pumping at MOWE in 49 sites that have supplied irrigation infrastructure with access to safe water. The CGIRE Facility has implemented a pilot activity on solar water pumping in 2016.

Moreover, the effectiveness and efficiency of the GCF investment are demonstrated by a comprehensive economic and financial analysis, which is summarized below and separately presented as Annex 3 to this funding proposal. The overall set-up of the model's financial analysis complements this funding proposal in Annex 3.

The goal of the project design is to provide sustainable access to safe water services at affordable costs to these fragile communities. The financial analysis of return on investment clearly shows that the GCF grant cannot be recovered within a reasonable period, which would have a detrimental effect on local communities. Still, a financial analysis was performed to assess the viability of recovering the GCF grant from the project's revenue stream (see Annex 2: Sheet, Summary, Sheet).

The total population served by the project is estimated at 105,843 people in Ethiopia (Cluster 1) and 741,964 people in Djibouti (Cluster 2). The proportion of the population served as part of this project in Djibouti is 14% (Cluster 1) and 14% (Cluster 2) (see Annex 2: Sheet, Summary, Sheet).

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

268. M&E is one of several implementation and management tools that support successful delivery of the project. Without careful monitoring, the necessary data are not collected; hence learning cannot be achieved. The GCF will provide a Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) framework to guide the CRGE Facility in carrying out the M&E plan guided by the CRGE Facility Monitoring and Evaluation Manual. At the current moment, the CRGE Facility is working with the project team to develop an M&E plan that includes alignment with NDC M&E systems, cross-cutting quality assurance and technical assistance.

269. The M&E plan will be developed in close collaboration with the project team and will provide guidance from the GCF and comply with GCF M&E policy, ensuring that the project maintains a simple and robust monitoring system that is aligned to the NDC M&E systems and covers all of its activities that will be based on the following core activities:

270. Activity 1: Monitoring Core Activities. Progress monitoring provides evidence on accomplishment of the core activities planned under each Output and Activity, which will be scrutinized by annual reporting and project reviews. The CRGE Facility will provide a framework for the project to identify which activities are ahead, behind or on schedule. The Accredited Entity and executing entities will report on progress against these indicators in their respective M&E reports.

271. Knowledge Management and Dissemination: Annual institutional learning events will be conducted for the CRGE Facility and the project team to share lessons learned from the project implementation of experiences and lessons learned among participating entities, including regional stakeholders.

272. Activity 3: Annual Performance Assessment. The annual performance assessment is a comprehensive assessment report (PAR) on the project Outputs. The PARs inform two monitoring activities at the project level: (i) Project Coordination Unit (PCU) will conduct an internal review of the lessons and insights from responses to the M&E. The reporting process is similar to that for quarterly reports, but with a focus on the lessons learned; (ii) The PCU will submit the PARs to the Project Coordination Units, which will then submit to the CRGE Facility. PCU captures Output, Activity and Outcome data, as well as project progress against the GCF reporting requirements, which include but are not limited to: reporting on:

- Annual Progress Report (APR) of Objective and project Outcomes – each with indicators,
- baseline data and end-of-project targets (cumulative),
- Project Implementation Plan (PIP) and project Outcome (annual)
- Financial reports
- Lessons learned and good practices
- Annual Work Plan (for the following year)

274. Based on the annual performance assessment results, the CRGE Facility consolidates and submits a consolidated report to the GCF in accordance with the GCF standard reporting formats. The Facility will also provide a summary of lessons learned from the project.

275. Data reported as a result of the M&E will be integrated into national reporting processes in the context of tracking progress toward the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development and NDCs by MIPs as per the NDC Work Plan, which is currently under development.

276. Joint Monitoring Missions: Joint monitoring missions will provide an opportunity to engage stakeholders of the project and other partners. The mission will not be limited to the CRGE Facility but may involve the executing entity or other development partners as deemed relevant. It is important to note that the GCF does not fund these missions, which are conducted at the discretion of the CRGE Facility for the purpose of validating M&E information during site visits. A key part of these missions will be to collect feedback from stakeholders on project performance. Observations, decisions, and action points arising from this mission will be distributed to participants.

278. The CRGE Facility will provide a framework for the project team to prepare for joint monitoring missions, review meetings for the project finance officers and project technical staff. The Facility ensures preparation and dissemination of customized and fit-for-purpose financial and procurement management guidelines to the project Finance Officers. It will also ensure that financial management and procurement


---
**Page 113**
---

activities are conducted in accordance with existing government procurement and financial management policies and procedures.

## Reporting Arrangement

279. The executing entities at federal, regional, and woreda levels are responsible for submitting periodic reports. The Federal EEs shall submit quarterly reports to the CRIGC Facility. These reports will provide detailed information on project implementation progress, including budget execution, compliance with standards. The Regional EEs (Bureaus of Water and Energy and Bureaus of Irrigation and Landscaping) shall submit monthly reports to the Woreda Office of Finance of the project implementing woredas. The Woreda EEs will submit quarterly reports to the regional and woreda financial reports and send to the CRIGC Facility. BOFEDs shall also share copy of the report with the respective Regional Bureau of Water and Energy and Bureaus of Irrigation and Landscaping Committee meetings. The CRIGC Facility will consolidate reports from EEs and submit annual reports to the GCF Board for review and approval in accordance with the GCF rules and procedures as well as the NDA. The Facility will facilitate an annual external audit of the project and submit the audit report to the GCF Board.

## Project Mid-term and Final Evaluation

280. The mid-term evaluation of the project “Climate-resilient community access to safe water powered by renewable energy” will be conducted at comparable regions of Ethiopia in order to assess the progress made towards achieving the project’s objectives at the halfway mark of its implementation period. The evaluation will cover all aspects of the project, including the installation and operationalization of solar water pumps, the construction of water storage tanks, and the development of community-based management systems for water resources. The evaluation will involve consultations with stakeholders, including community members, local authorities, and technical experts. The mid-term evaluation findings will highlight successes, identify challenges, and provide recommendations for adjustments to the project’s implementation strategy as needed.

The final evaluation of the project “Climate-resilient community access to safe water powered by renewable energy” will be conducted at comparable regions of Ethiopia upon completion of the project in order to provide a comprehensive assessment of the project's overall performance. It will evaluate the project's impact on climate resilience, water access, and agricultural productivity in the


---
**Page 114**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 3.0 | PAGE 111

| target regions. The evaluation will measure the achievement of project objectives, the sustainability of outcomes, and the effectiveness of the implemented strategies and technologies. Key aspects of the evaluation will include the long-term operational success of solar water pumping systems, the government's role in supporting the projects, the environmental impact, and socio-economic benefits realized. The evaluation will also assess the project's alignment with national policies and its contribution to gender equality and women's empowerment. The findings will inform future projects and provide valuable lessons for scaling up similar interventions across other regions. |

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| High | Medium |

#### Description

The project requires foreign currency for technology imports, which will be given through GCF grant financing as well as the purchase of equipment and services from international suppliers. The risk is that the supplier may not provide local service provision, e.g., through external service providers for asset maintenance. GCF grant financing as well as the purchase of equipment and services from international suppliers may result in a negative impact on balance.

The risk of further inflationary tendencies of Ethiopian Birr due to the ensuing reduced ability to repay loans is one major reason why this application is being made.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

A potential mitigation measure could be to increase productivity for agricultural products targeting exports and value addition. This can be achieved by providing training on good agricultural practices, as well as through Led Industrialization (ADL of Ethiopia). However, the first priority is to achieve community-resilience, public health and food security. Therefore, financial risks cannot be fully mitigated.

### Selected Risk Factor 2

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Medium | Medium | Medium |

#### Description

The executing entities have limited knowledge in the operation and maintenance of solar powered submersible pumps. Furthermore, applicable of solar technologies for irrigation purposes is a novel technology; thus getting the execution entities to commit to the continuous operation of farming might be a challenge until the last agricultural produce yields.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

This project has incorporated systems that are mostly automated and require minimal human interference. If required, the executing entity will engage an external service provider to provide technical support. The TAs will be contracted with the service providers to transfer knowledge in operating and managing the assets. As incorporated in the work plan, the TA providers will conduct training sessions for the executing entities within the community and at the zonal level for operation and maintenance services. Moreover, capacity development and awareness raising activities are included as part of the project’s implementation plan. A detailed work plan from the TAs providers to the service providers and TUs has been outlined in the feasibility study document.

### Selected Risk Factor 3

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

#### Description

The boreholes identified for this project were drilled and capped in 2013/14, although boreholes have a regular period of over 25 years. The water quality analysis conducted on the ground water quality might not meet the physical and chemical characteristics that has been reported in the well completion report.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

To mitigate this risk, at the commencement of the project all wells will be rehabilitated and pump tested for a minimum and maximum of 24 and 72 hours respectively. In addition to the required water chemical analysis conducted as part of the feasibility study, additional tests will be carried out in accordance with international standards; mandatory testing for major cations, anions, pH levels, and total dissolved solids (TDS) will be carried out, thus preventing the possible emergence of salinity-related concerns. Additionally, digital water

The implementation of many public and development partners financed projects and programs ongoing in the region have been largely unimpeded by the conflict.
- **Mitigation:** In the unfortunate likelihood that the conflict restarts again against the Tigray region, project implementation large scale will be halted until such time as the conflict is resolved. This risk is not included in the baseline selection thus far.

# Description

294. Lack of knowledge of the value and use of the project assets could potentially lead to intentional damage or misuse of the equipment.

## Mitigation

295. The project proponents have recommended that service providers be selected from within the locations in order to build trust and ownership of the assets. Several measures will be adopted to ensure the security of the water supply system, including: (i) establishing a community-based management committee with a sense of ownership and responsibility within the local community, who are the main beneficiaries; (ii) protect the water source from contamination by ensuring that it is located at least 50 meters away from any settlement or other sources of pollution; (iii) educate the community about the importance of water pump, how they improve livelihoods, and emphasize the negative consequences of vandalism and misuse. The project proponents will also recommend a legal framework establishment, with clear rules and regulations on the use and protection of the water pump. Violation of these agreements will be subject to penalties.

296. The water pumps installations will be secured using enclosures or fencing and high quality locking mechanisms in order to prevent unauthorized access. A maintenance plan for the equipment will also be established at the time the equipment is procured and the project developers can be mobilized to install the system. The community will be involved in the installation of the system, which will ensure that they are familiar with its operation and maintenance.

297. Based on the hydrogeological and water balance studies, groundwater balance (please refer to annexed under a separate document) indicates that there is sufficient groundwater for the project. However, the submersible pump and solar PV systems (the solar PVs will no battery as a backup to power the submersible pumps) cannot be suspended for each borehole. The design will give enough time for the well recharge. Moreover, the system will be designed with an automatic shut-off valve that will automatically stop water extraction when volumes found in the water balance study, described in Annex 2. This ensures over abstraction of ground water resources and also helps to protect the environment by ensuring that the groundwater is not extracted beyond discharge rate as indicated in Table 19 & 20 of the Feasibility Study (Annex 2). This essentially will put the system on a sustainable basis, which will ensure that the borehole can be used for at least 5 hours per day when powered by the grid or diesel generators. Additionally, digital water meters (Divers) will be installed in each borehole to monitor and control groundwater abstraction rates. The Divers will also be used for gauging the ground water abstraction rate against the water balance study conducted for this project, which is essential for effective water management.

To mitigate these risks, it is recommended that steps be taken to guard against inflation on the cost of imported technologies and equipment could further exacerbate the financial strain, making it difficult to achieve the project goals within the planned budget.

299. **To mitigate this potential risk, the following mitigation measures can be made:**

* Utilize financial instruments such as forward contracts or inflation swaps to lock in prices and guard against future cost escalations.
* Implement a robust procurement strategy that includes competitive bidding processes for goods and services.
* Regular financial reviews will be implemented. Procurement processes shall also be streamlined to ensure timely delivery without significant inflationary increases. This can involve fast tracking procurement approvals and reducing lead times.

GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

300. An Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) was prepared in accordance with the GCF and International Finance Corporation (IFC) policies and standards.

The ESMP is based on the draft Environmental Impact Assessment reports that were prepared as part of the initial design work for the Koba Girara and Ghinach-Santu water supply projects. The ESMP has been developed to ensure that all relevant environmental and social issues are addressed in a comprehensive manner, taking into account the project’s specific context.

The full ESMP is available in Annex VII and has been uploaded onto GCF's web platform<sup>1</sup>. In line with Government and GCF policies, the ESMP was prepared by an independent consultant. The ESMP was reviewed and approved after it had been conducted; the project is assessed as medium risk (Category B) in accordance with the GCF’s and IFC International Environmental and Social Performance Standards.

The ESMP has identified a number of potential environmental and social impacts that may arise from the implementation of the project sights. It is already in highly arid area and does not contain sensitive ecosystems. This categorization is in line with the GCF policies, which state that projects should be located away from areas containing sensitive ecosystems (i.e., wetlands, forests, or others). Moreover, it will have minimal adverse social impacts and will not affect a broader area beyond the immediate project site. The ESMP has identified a number of measures to mitigate these potential impacts.

301. Impact identification has been conducted in accordance with guidance on environmental and social risk assessment<sup>2</sup>. This includes an analysis of the project’s direct, indirect, and cumulative impacts as well as its regional and catchment (Santo Girama) region water development sites. Potential impacts identified for Koba Girara are: (i) during construction phase: flooding and sedimentation, soil erosion, gully formation, loss of vegetation cover, dust pollution, noise pollution, air pollution, health risks to workers and public safety; and (ii) during operation phase: the risk of water supply disruption due to drought, water quality degradation, salinity, waterborne diseases and COVID-19, and environmental impacts on biodiversity. Potential impacts identified for Ghinach-Santu are: (i) during construction phase: flooding and sedimentation, soil pollution, water pollution, impact on vegetation disturbance to land, impact on property and infrastructure; and (ii) during operation phase: the risk of water supply disruption due to drought, waterborne diseases and COVID-19, and environmental impacts on biodiversity. The ESMP has developed a comprehensive list of mitigation measures for all identified risks.

302. The ESMP also includes an analysis of the project’s potential indirect effects (Section 6 of this funding proposal). Levels of roles and responsibilities of actors include all levels, i.e., from government to community level (village) level. Similarly, in this section the grievance and redress mechanism are described in detail.

303. Potential social impacts will be minimal or non-existent as it will not relocate people, nor will it have negative implications on their livelihoods.


### G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

<sup>1</sup> Ministry of Finance (MoF) (n.d.). Climate Resilient Green Economy Facility. <https://www.MoF.et/green-projects/programme-protection>

<sup>2</sup> Wood, Chris (2003): Environmental Impact Assessment: A Comparative Review. Second Edition. Pearson Education Limited, Essex

**Page 120**
