308\. A full gender assessment and action plan document is available (Annex B). The final document was developed through a participatory assessment followed by inputs from gender focal persons of the GCFR Facility and Ministry of Water and Energy. It was then enriched during a project proposal validation workshop where community members participated.

309\. Ethiopia's economic growth and development, as well as the livelihoods of the rural population, are highly dependent on water resources. The majority of households in Ethiopia rely heavily on rainfed agriculture. Women make up 26% of female-headed households. As a result, impacts of climate change are directly felt by a significant proportion of women. Women's vulnerability is further increased due to their limited access to land and credit; women headed households are the most vulnerable, least prepared, and likely to be worst affected by climate change.

310\. The rural poor rely heavily on water for energy for the household, as well as maintaining overall household wellbeing. In the role of women, they are responsible for collecting water from distant sources, which increases their vulnerability. Their role in rainfed agriculture is also critical; however, they have limited access to irrigation programs due to gender biases and lack of resources.

310\. This project aims to contribute towards baseline efforts on the ground by identifying actions that will equally benefit women, especially those who are most vulnerable. The project will ensure that all recommendations made in this document are implemented. Key recommendations include ensuring consultations and meetings are arranged in a manner that takes the needs of women into account; ensuring that women's voices are heard through gender-sensitive participatory processes; providing legally binding statements on the participation of women both as members and leaders; ensuring the required resources for women to participate fully in decision-making, including financial resources; ensuring access to irrigation and small businesses; and conducting policy review of local water, irrigation, and/or agriculture policies to ensure that they are gender-sensitive.

309\. When implemented the project will result not only in the provision of the much-needed access to water both for household use and rainfed agriculture but also in the empowerment of women. Women will be better equipped at managing the resources in equal footing and thus sharing both the responsibilities and benefits. The project is expected to have a positive impact on women's economic empowerment, small businesses, and decision-making functions thus giving them a chance to equally influence local development.

310\. The project will also ensure that all gender-related activities are monitored and evaluated. Ongoing support is given through a well-established structure at various levels: federal, regional, woreda and Kebele. Community ownership is ensured through the KUA and M&E reports generated at different levels which will provide detailed information along with gender-disaggregated data.

# G.3 Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## 311. The financial management and procurement of this project will be guided by the public finance management and public procurement regulations of the Government of the Federal Republic of Ethiopia. The project will be implemented under a single-channel funding arrangement defined for the CRIGE Facility, which is consistent with the Channel One fund disbursement arrangement for the CRIGE Facility. The project will be funded through the GCF project account, into which the GCF fund for the current project will be deposited. After receiving funds from the GCF, the CRIGE Facility will make payments to the executing entities under the terms of the GCF project account for activities, which will be implemented by the Federal Executive Entity. For reporting purposes, the CRIGE Facility will provide a monthly report on the disbursement and utilization of the GCF project account funds to the GCF through the OFAG. The OFAG adopts international standards on Auditing and financial reporting, which will also apply to this project.

## 312. Project finance will be disbursed to the executing entities based on approved quarterly Work Plans. Disbursements will be made in accordance with the terms of the funding proposal and any subsequent advances. The CRIGE Facility Finance Officer will clear this before disbursement processing and sending letter of credit (LoC) instructions to the executing entity. The project finance will be disbursed through an internal control framework illustrated under section 7, which depicts the fund transfer and reporting process for the CRIGE Facility. The CRIGE Facility is a part of the Government structure – federal, regional and Woreda – and respond back through the same channels. The CRIGE Facility Finance Officer will be responsible for fiduciary assurance and facilitation of reporting.

### Procurement Management (PM)

## 313. Detailed annual Project Procurement Plans will be prepared by the Executing Entities. Most would utilize equipment procurement contracts (EPC) to procure power generation solutions on both gas- and irrigation schemes. Most would intentionally tender the EPC contracts by issuing detailed technical specifications and submitting a prequalification process for contractors who are expected to meet the specified technical standards and submit the least cost proposals. To ensure that project operations are not delayed, the CRIGE Facility will require that all EPC proposals submitted by the EAC may include scope of short-term operations and maintenance of the power plants and/or irrigation schemes. The CRIGE Facility will also require that all EPC proposals submitted by the EAC may include scope of short-term operations and maintenance of the power plants and/or irrigation schemes. The CRIGE Facility will also require that all EPC proposals submitted by the EAC may include scope of short-term operations and maintenance of the power plants and/or irrigation schemes.

# Secretariat's assessment of FP243

## Proposal name: Climate-resilient community access to safe water powered by renewable energy in drought-vulnerable regions of Ethiopia

### Accredited entity: Ministry of Finance (MOF)

### Country: Ethiopia

### Project size: Small

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The project is designed to directly enhance the resilience of smallholder farmers and pastoralists in Ethiopia's least developed regions, which are most vulnerable and affected by climate change. Over 3,300 ha of land will be irrigated, and close to 12,000 communities will gain access to clean potable water. Additionally, water for over 280,000 people in the region will last during extended drought seasons. The indirect benefits of this project are expected to reach over 1.7 million people. | The long-term sustainability of the project relies on the farmers' ability to increase income from agriculture and livestock using the technology and maintenance of the solar-powered water-pumping (SPWP) systems. |
| The project employs and establishes sustainable water extraction and usage practices, as well as the maintenance of the water-supply systems using renewable energy. It also promotes direct community engagement in the planning and implementation of both scientific and Indigenous/traditional water management practices. | The involvement of multiple institutions at the national, regional, and woreda (third-level administrative division) levels could result in coordination challenges, potentially affecting the timely implementation of the project. |
| Agriculture is a major sector contributing to Ethiopia's national gross domestic product and employing 75 per cent of the workforce. However, climate change has led to increasingly unpredictable rain patterns due to climate change. To empower smallholder farmers with sustainable water sourced from green-energy solutions, the project aims to enhance food security, improve social well-being, and increase resilience against droughts. | The project is in conflict-affected areas, which could lead to loss of property and delays. |


---
**Page 131**
---

being, increase income, promote better livelihoods at the farm level, reduce greenhouse gas emissions, improve environmental conditions, and decrease the need for foreign exchange to import fossil fuels.

The project transforms previous plans of the Government of Ethiopia to pump groundwater wells with submersible pumps and diesel generators by redirecting the focus to SWP. This will create a more sustainable environment for sustainable groundwater use across the country.

The project demonstrates effect and contributions to an improved environmental condition, especially through the use of solar energy for water supply in irrigation, livestock, and drinking water systems across Ethiopia.


## Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

### 2.1 Project background

1. Ethiopia faces significant challenges from climate change, which threatens various sectors of its economy and the welfare of its population.
2. The country is highly vulnerable to extreme weather events, ranking fifth globally in drought risk and experiencing severe impacts from unpredictable rainfall patterns, prolonged dry spells, recurring floods, increasing frequency of extreme weather events, which will severely impact water supply, especially in rural areas.

3. Water stress is a significant concern in Ethiopia, despite its abundant water resources.
4. Rural areas are particularly affected, with a significant portion of the population lacking access to adequate sanitation and drinking water services, leading to high rates of child mortality. Only 5 per cent of land is irrigated. Ethiopia's agriculture sector, which is vital to its economy, contributes about 38 per cent of GDP, employs more than half of the country's gross domestic product, 80 per cent of its exports, and employs 75 per cent of its workforce. The sector is highly vulnerable to climate change due to its dependence on rainfall variability.
5. Prolonged droughts and erratic rainfall patterns lead to reduced crop yields and food insecurity, affecting millions. Climate change also exacerbates poverty and economic disparity, with rural areas being the most affected.


---
**Page 132**
---

5. In the Tigray Goba Valley and Bereha zones (programme target areas), with a large percentage of the population below the poverty line, the traditional farming and pastoral systems are severely impacted by climate change, with increasing droughts, land degradation, and limited access to water. Recent instances include losing almost 75 per cent of livestock in the Bereha zone during 2021 due to lack of rainfall and increase in the instance of migration and dependence on food aid.

- Addressing these challenges requires significant investments in climate-resilient infrastructure, including the Ethiopian Climate Resilient Green Economy (CREGE) Facility. Significant investments are required for irrigation systems, water storage facilities, and other interventions that are crucial to mitigate and adapt to the impacts of climate change, and improve livelihoods. Many rural households currently have no access to electricity services and safe water, both for drinking and irrigation, which has been exacerbated by drought conditions. Access to clean water for drinking and irrigation is often unfeasible, as operating diesel generators is expensive. While there is a need for solar-powered boreholes in the target areas, limited experience with such systems of experience have so far constrained uptake in Ethiopia. In the two target project locations, the Government of Ethiopia has identified 10 sites where solar-powered boreholes can be installed. Groundwater boreholes were identified and drilled for irrigation purposes, but due to a lack of finance (of $50 per well [37 per cent]) are operational. These are all powered using diesel generators.

- The proposed project will improve community resilience to climate change in two target areas and provide sustainable livelihoods for communities through the promotion of climate-resilient agriculture. It comprises two main components: community resilience and enabling environment, and irrigation infrastructure development. The former includes solar-powered boreholes (SPB) systems, provision of water for potable use, establishing community-driven organizations, and the creation of a research and innovation framework; the latter component includes developing policy and regulatory frameworks; digital measurement, reporting and verification (MRV) and payment systems; creating business models and financing instruments; and providing technical assistance to communities. The project will also provide training and coordination. Furthermore, the project aims to position the intervention regions as models for climate-resilient agriculture.

- Aligned with the United Arab Emirates Framework for Global Climate Resilience from the twenty-eighth session of the Conference of the Parties to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), this project will directly benefit 1.75 million. It does this by establishing boreholes with SPW technology, improving irrigation infrastructure and water supply systems in Ethiopia. This will reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 238 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (t CO₂e) while providing equitable access to clean water, improved food security, and increased income for the target communities. The project is limited by financial resources for climate adaptation in Ethiopia and the extremely low-paying capacities of the impact communities.

- The total cost is USD 150 million in grants, including a USD 5 million contribution from the Government of Ethiopia. Federal ministries of the Government of Ethiopia will oversee the project implementation. The Ministry of Agriculture will lead the development of established irrigation user associations (IUAs) and water-user associations (WUAs). These associations, in cooperation with the Ministry of Water Resources, will manage boreholes and abstractions. The Ministry of Irrigation and Lowlands (MIL) and the Ministry of Water and Energy will lead the development of irrigation infrastructure and water-supply systems. Small rural irrigation user associations (SRIUAS) and small rural irrigation user cooperatives (SRUCOCs) in Ethiopia, which will provide reimbursable grants to farmers for investment in boreholes and other climate-resilient agriculture technologies, are eligible.

The project is classified as Category II of the environmental and social safeguards.


---
**Page 133**
---

2. **Component-by-component analysis**

10. The project establishes partnerships between government stakeholders, communities, and traditional leaders to improve access to drinking water and irrigation in Ethiopia’s Amhara and Oromia regions. It leverages local agricultural cooperatives for irrigation, focusing on areas like the Kobo-Girara Valley and Borena zone due to their climate vulnerability and groundwater scarcity.

11. The proposed project is divided into two components: component 1 involves direct interventions and infrastructure development, while component 2 creates an enabling environment for sustainable water management and access in the target regions. The project emphasizes gender inclusion and community participation, aiming to transform conflict-affected areas into peaceful, resilient, sustainable regions with improved access to essential resources like food and water.

Component 1: Community resilience (total cost: USD 43.2 million; GCF cost: USD 19.4 million)

(a) Output 1.1: Solar water pumps installed:

(b) Output 1.2: Efficient water distribution system constructed for households, irrigation, and livestock watering;

(c) Output 1.3: Gender-sensitive BIAA/ WIAAs established and capacitated; and

(d) Output 1.4: Small Scale Releving Funding Facility established.

Activities for output 1.1 will focus on deploying 100 solar water pumps in the Kobo-Girara Valley and Borena zone, with a total capacity of 250 cubic meters per hour, by providing water for drinking, livestock, and irrigation. The project will involve comprehensive rehabilitation of existing boreholes and handpumps, ensuring their sustainability through rigorous water-quality testing. Community-awareness programs will ensure proper use and maintenance of rehabilitated wells, while regular monitoring protocols will maintain water quality standards. The installation of solar-powered pumps will be managed by specialized firms supported by user-fee systems and gender-responsive service delivery.

Activities for output 1.2 will focus on deploying efficient water distribution and irrigation systems, aiming to provide equitable access to water resources for households, irrigation, and livestock watering. This includes laying distribution pipelines, storage tanks, and irrigation infrastructure, all optimized for local conditions. The project will employ advanced metering technologies to monitor water usage accurately, facilitating effective management and conservation. A fair and sustainable pricing structure will be established, ensuring financial viability while considering household income and water usage.

Additionally, the proposed project will employ irrigation service providers (ISPs) to manage the distribution of water for irrigation purposes. ISPs will ensure a reliability of at least 25% per day per capita in line with government goals. Community training sessions will be held to educate users on proper use and maintenance of water systems, and a comprehensive O&M manual will guide ISPs, ensuring the long-term sustainability of the water-supply infrastructure.

Activities for output 1.3 will focus on establishing gender-sensitive BIAA and WIAAs to promote inclusive governance of water resources. These associations are designed to ensure equal representation and participation. Training programmes will support these associations by


---
**Page 134**
---

covering governance, financial management, and technical aspects of water resource management. The SIAU/WIWA will play a crucial role in the sustainable management of water resources, regulating water use and minimizing conflicts. Agricultural extension workers will assist farmers in adopting new methods through regular training on modern irrigation techniques and other best practices.

Activities for output 1.4 will establish a small-scale revolving fund facility to enhance financial sustainability of SWP systems by reducing upfront costs associated with the installation of SWP systems and ICP costs. Partnering with RUSACON, this initiative provides returnable grants for improved crop varieties and irrigation technologies. The project also aims to ensure that women farmers in CECOSO, the inclusion of women and marginalized groups, and the establishment of comprehensive guidelines for fund management are all key components of the project's strategy to ensure long-term sustainability.

### Component 2: Enabling environment (total cost USD 7 million; GCF cost USD 1.9 million)

- **Output 2.1:** Realign regulatory policies, and guidelines created or strengthened.
- **Output 2.2:** Digital MRV and payment system established.
- **Output 2.3:** Comprehensive assessment to identify gender-specific barriers in water resource management models and financial instruments related to water resource management.
- **Output 2.4:** Local technicians trained on DWM of SWP and irrigation systems, and
- **Output 2.5:** Knowledge-sharing and coordinating platform established.

Activities for output 2.1 will enhance regulatory frameworks at both national and regional levels by aligning them with international best practices in water management, reviewing existing regulations, identifying gaps, and introducing new policies aligned with best water management practices. This includes the development of technical guidelines or standards for solar water pump technologies, including energy-efficiency standards for submersible pumps and motors.

Activities for output 2.2 will establish a digital MRV system using digital technologies to enhance the transparency and accountability of water resource management. This includes developing a national MRV framework that integrates data from various sources on water usage. The MRV system will be integrated with national frameworks for consistency in data reporting and management. The project also explores using M&E tools to implement tiered pricing, which is essential for the efficient operation and maintenance of water supply systems. Digital technologies enable efficient measurement and management of electricity consumption by solar pumps, leading to better resource allocation and streamlining financial transactions. This enhances transparency, accountability, and sustainability in water resource management.

Activities for output 2.3 will develop a comprehensive assessment to identify gender-specific barriers in water resource management models and financial instruments related to water resource management. Based on the findings, the proposed project will develop gender-sensitive policies and guidelines that address these barriers. These policies aim to improve the participation and economic benefits for both women and men. In addition, the project will provide training sessions tailored specifically for women and entrepreneurship, tailored to women and marginalized groups, and collaborates with financial institutions to create inclusive products like microfinance and credit guarantees.

Activities for output 2.4 will train local technicians in DWM of SWP systems to pinpoint the required skills and review of existing training resources. It will further develop a tailored training program that includes technical skills such as installation, operation, problem-solving, troubleshooting, and maintenance – soft skills, including communication, problem-solving, and teamwork. These training sessions, which will be delivered in collaboration with academic


---
**Page 135**
---

and regional institutions, will aim to provide high-quality, hands-on experience and foster the long-term sustainability of the SWP and irrigation systems. Equipping local techniques with the necessary skills and knowledge will help maintain and operate the systems effectively, minimizing reliance on external expertise.

The project also aims to create a sustainable business model that maximizes local employment opportunities and fosters the development of a skilled workforce, which enhances the overall resilience and growth of the target communities.


### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential
Scale: N/A

22. The project aims to rehabilitate at least 100 boreholes, equipping them with solar-powered submersible pumping systems, focusing on areas like the Robo-Gerar Valley and Borena zones. It supports improvements in agricultural yields using water, and promotes agriculture through the integration of climate-smart technologies, traditional knowledge, and Indigenous knowledge, as well as modern climate smart agriculture.

23. It will directly benefit 355,236 individuals and indirectly benefit 1,737,752 individuals, totaling approximately 40 per cent of Ethiopia's population, accounting for 1.8 per cent of the national population. At least 50 per cent of these beneficiaries are expected to be female. The project will contribute significantly to Ethiopia’s water security and sustainable water for irrigation, livestock production, and potable purposes improving their resilience against climate change.

24. The project also aims to increase agricultural productivity by enhancing soil fertility through the use of solar-powered submersible pumping technology. Beneficiaries will benefit from increased employment opportunities and more efficient water use in production systems, leading to improved food security and economic gains. It also targets significant CO₂ emission reductions, with an estimated reduction potential of at least 173,220 tCO₂ over 25 years, contributing to Ethiopia’s broader mitigation goals.

25. The project aligns with the National Irrigation Development Strategy (NIDS) and national adaptation plan (NAP), aiming to improve waterable area and expand irrigation systems.


#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential
Scale: N/A

25. The project aims to overcome barriers that have prevented the uptake of SWP technology in rural Ethiopia, leaving communities without access to safe water for household use and livestock. By providing a holistic solution integrating access to finance with a strengthened enabling environment, the proposed project enhances agricultural productivity and resilience against climate change.

The project will also contribute to the national enabling environment by policy and regulatory measures, digitization, capacity-building, and awareness raising. It aims to increase the adoption of solar-powered submersible pumping technology beyond the initial GEF investment, making it more affordable and widely adopted. The project will be implemented in alignment with Ethiopia’s target to expand modern irrigation from 2 to 20 per cent of agricultural areas by 2030. This will lead to increased productivity and income levels in rural households while ensuring sustainable groundwater use through smart meters and efficient irrigation systems.


---
**Page 136**
---

3.3 Sustainable development potential

The project will significantly contribute to multiple Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), particularly SDG 1 (No poverty), SDG 2 (Zero hunger), SDG 6 (Clean water and sanitation), SDG 7 (Affordable and clean energy), SDG 8 (Decent work and economic growth), SDG 10 (Reduced inequalities), and SDG 13 (Climate action). The project will address critical gaps in rural areas of Ethiopia, where access to reliable water supply is a major challenge. By providing access to safe and clean water and sanitation (SDG 6), the project will also support Ethiopia's goal of expanding solar energy (SDG 7), thereby increasing the population's access to affordable and clean energy. The project will contribute to SDG 8 by creating substantial returns on investment, with long-term community development impacts. The action plan ensures that the project is sustainable in the long term through reducing water collection time and alleviating burdens related to water collection, thus transforming community life.

3.4 Needs of the recipient

Ethiopia, a landlocked and culturally diverse country, ranks one-hundred and seventy-three out of 189 countries on the United Nations Development Programme's 2019 Human Development Index (HDI). The country is highly vulnerable to climate change due to its special treatment under international frameworks like the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) Paris Agreement. Despite these efforts, the population lacks access to reliable water supply and continues relying on surface water prior to climate impacts and diseases. In remote rural areas, only 16 per cent of households are connected to a piped water system, with most households using expensive and environmentally intensive diesel generators for irrigation. The feasibility study highlights significant regional disparities, with nearly all drinking water needs met in Amhara's Kofe-Ginza Valley, while the Borena


---
**Page 137**
---

### 3.5 Country ownership

The project interventions align with its updated NDC 10-year development plan (10YDP), and key government priorities, particularly those outlined in the NAP. The NAP emphasizes increasing agricultural productivity through modern irrigation techniques, such as SWP. This project, detailed in a comprehensive feasibility study, addresses these constraints and lack of experience that have hindered its adoption. By focusing on climate-smart agriculture (CSA) and water security, it aims to achieve the goal of becoming a climate-resilient middle-income economy by 2035, with an estimated NAP cost of USD 90 billion.

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The GCF copay for tonne CO₂ reduced is USD 42.12, which is in the higher end of the typical range of USD 8–USD 70 per tonne. This copay competitive gives the project significant adaptation benefits, including its contribution to increasing climate resilience, water security, and socioeconomic development.

The GCF provides full grant funding from GCF, which is crucial for its financial viability. The financial internal rate of return remains negative when the grant is replaced with a loan or concessional financing. However, the project has been designed to ensure that grant funding to maintain financial feasibility, particularly given the substantial upfront capital expenditures required and the necessary infrastructure for SWP systems.

Although the copay is competitive in terms of cost per tonne CO₂ reduced, these returns are not immediate. The delayed nature of these returns presents challenges for debt servicing and repayment. The project has been designed with a long-term perspective, where benefits accrue over time, particularly as the infrastructure matures and begins to generate consistent


---
**Page 138**
---

### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

**Project overview:** The project aims to strengthen the climate resilience in selected drought-prone areas of Ethiopia by deploying solar-powered irrigation systems as an action of ground water aquifers with sustainable water for agricultural production and drinking. By enabling the sustainable use of deep boreholes for irrigation and drinking water, the project will contribute to improving food security and livelihoods in the Amhara Zone in Ethiopia. Both are regions with substantial groundwater potential but currently facing severe climate change impacts.

The project seeks to improve access to safe and sustainable drinking water for smallholder farmers as its target area. The project is also expected to improve the local economy through creating local employment opportunities, rural electrification, and improved yield and food production.


| Environmental (ES) & Social (SS) risk category and safeguard instruments |
|---|
| The project is categorised by the AE as medium risk (Category B) given that the likely environmental and social impacts of the project are restricted to the project area. A detailed ESAP has been readily addressed through the ESMP, which includes overall mitigation measures and implementation arrangements. The Secretariat confirms the assigned risk category that is within the EAS risk accreditation scope of the AE. The AE has prepared a draft ESMP for the project, which identifies the potential environmental and social risks and impacts of the proposed activities. The ESAMP includes overall mitigation measures and implementation arrangements to develop


---
**Page 139**
---

### ESS1 (Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts)

The AE has conducted a baseline assessment at part of the ESAMP to map out the applicable environmental and social risks and impacts associated with the project during the implementation and operation phase of the project. The AE has also established mitigation measures for these risks and impacts, which are described in detail below.

#### ESS2 (Labour and Working Conditions)

The AE contained in the ESAMP has assessed medium to high risk regarding labour and working conditions particularly during the construction phases of the project. The AE will ensure that all workers have access to adequate and appropriate safety equipment and measures as well as obligations with all contractors to avoid and minimize the risks to workers and public.

#### ESS3 (Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention)

The ESAMP has identified medium risk regarding resource efficiency and pollution prevention during the implementation and operation phases of the project, considering the lack of sufficient solid and liquid waste management facilities in the area. The AE will ensure that all contractors are required to secure and protect the areas around the groundwater source to the limit of risk of pollution. The AE will adopt appropriate sanitation measures and put in place appropriate liquid and solid waste disposal systems for the construction phase of the project, which will enforce the dictates of the Environmental Pollution Prohibition of Ethiopia to ensure that the groundwater is not polluted during the implementation and operation phases of the intended use. The ESAMP also identifies moderate impacts on the environment due to agricultural activities associated with the project. The AE will adopt a no-till or reduced tillage method of drip irrigation system and adopt an Integrated Pest Management (IPM) approach to avoid and limit the use of agricultural hazardous substances during the operation of the project.

#### ESS4 (Community Health, Safety and Security)

The ESAMP has identified the potential for medium risk regarding community health, safety and security due to the spread of diseases like malaria through the provision of nets and other items that prevent mosquitoes from biting. The AE will ensure that all contractors are required to implement preventive measures and services. The ESAMP has also considered the conflict sensitivity related to the project in the area where Kobo-Girana is situated, as the agricultural industry can increase and lead to conflicts especially in the rainy season.

#### ESS5 (Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement)

Given the SWP rehabilitation will be conducted on the existing boreholes, the AE does not expect any displacement and resettlement of the community during the project development and implementation. However,


---
**Page 140**
---

as the project will construct the water distribution system for households, irrigation, and livestock use in the targeted areas, involuntary resettlement may arise as a result of the work of constructing pipe network, building up irrigation infrastructure and setting up point collection.
The AEF is included as part of the ESAMP to enumerate the strategies and approaches to ensure that such potential impacts are minimized. The project will also provide compensation for any affected households, and thus the livelihoods and welfare of project affected people and local communities will be restored.
The mitigation measures outlined in the ESAMP include activities such as the participatory land use planning process, which will identify areas where construction work is not feasible or necessary. The project will not allow anyone to engage in any activities that would trigger compensation rights, and it is paramount that all affected households are provided with adequate compensation for their losses.
The AEF is recommended to initiate and maintain the FPC processes throughout the project implementation period. This will ensure that all affected and/or affected vulnerable groups and other stakeholders prior to the implementation of any activities.


### ES6 (Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources) The project will not trigger this performance standard as it does not affect the biodiversity in the locations present, and the project will not trigger this performance standard given the high human settlement in the locations present. However, the project will potentially result in the encroachment on ecologically sensitive areas that are outside of the project sites, including the forest reserve in Awaleba, Faji, and Gembah, thereby resulting in a loss of biodiversity in these areas. The project will also result in increased demand for construction and farmland needs, loss of vegetation due to land clearing, and the loss of forestry resources such as timber, firewood, and other non-timber forest products (NTFPs) including fruits, products, and medicinal plants. The ESAMP therefore requires further due diligence on ES6 through a change in procedure for the project.
### ES7 (Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities) The AEF has provided a native communities engagement plan as part of its ESAMP. The former plan ensures that the project will not trigger this performance standard given the high human settlement in the locations present, and the importance of meaningful consultation. Pastoralist communities will have the opportunity to benefit directly from the project through access to water resources and improved livelihoods due to their knowledge of water use. In line with their roles and functions, the Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group is responsible for ensuring that the project complies with ES7.
### ES8 (Cultural Heritage) The ESAMP does not identify any cultural heritage sites to be protected by the project and therefore has not triggered this performance standard. However, the ESAMP requires a change in procedure for the project as it identifies the need for due diligence procedures to require for a change first procedure in the case of the presence of any cultural heritage sites within or vicinity of the project area. The AEF is requested to prepare a reasonable chance find procedure for the project and the ESAMP is to guide the AEF on how to proceed during the project implementation.


### Implementation Arrangements As related to safeguards operation, the AEF has specific arrangements and costing for the ESAMP implementation detailing the specific needs during the construction phase of the project. The ESAMP will be part of the tender for the Gore-Girama and Garbée-Santie sites. The project will conduct further due diligence on any existing or associated facilities (e.g., water distribution lines) that require further assessment.

### 4.3 Overall project assessment (medium risk)

#### 4.3.1 Overall project assessment (medium risk)

The proposed project aims to enhance climate-resilient access to safe drinking water and agricultural productivity in Ethiopia's rural areas through the adoption of SWP technology. The project will be implemented by a consortium led by Green Climate Fund, with support from the Ethiopian government and other partners. The project will focus on the development of solar-powered irrigation systems for provision of water for potable use and resilient agriculture, as well as capacity building for women in rural areas.

The project is expected to contribute to the achievement of several Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), including SDG 6 (clean water and sanitation) and SDG 2 (zero hunger). The project will also support the implementation of a final regulatory framework, digitization of MRPs and payments, strengthening and dissemination of gender equality policies, and the establishment of a knowledge-sharing and coordination platform. Total project costs are estimated at USD 50 million. The total


---
**Page 143**
---

### Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current project (low risk)

The MOF will serve as the AE. It has extensive experience in overseeing financial management and project implementation, currently managing FYMO under implementation in Ethiopia. The MOF also has a track record of successfully implementing projects for year-round drinking water and small-scale irrigation to address drought risks and other climate change impacts. In 2013, the Ethiopian government, with support from the Climate Change Commission, established the Ethiopia CREEC Within MOF, which now has operational experience in managing large-scale irrigation projects.

The AE’s key partners (KPs) include MoE, the Ministry of Water, Energy and Resources (MoWE), and the World Bank. The AE also has a strong track record in managing large-scale irrigation and lowland development projects, including the Lower Omo Irrigation Project and the Pastoral Community Development Program. The AE will continue to work closely with these partners as well as organizations such as the International Fund for Agricultural Development, the World Bank, the Ethiopian Rural Finance Corporation, and others to enhance rural financial systems in Ethiopia, focusing on access to credit and financial literacy.

### Project-execution risks (medium risk)

#### Inflation challenges: The funding proposal (FP) highlights economic and financial challenges that could impact project implementation. These include increased costs of goods and services and may affect project execution by increasing the costs of solar equipment and expenses for farmers. The Economist Intelligence Unit operational risk assessment also identifies inflation as a potential challenge, with concerns about the overvaluation of the Ethiopian birr, and liquidity stress in the banking sector, all of which could contribute to higher interest rates on loans.

#### Project implementation challenges: The FP notes that funds disbursed by GCF to the AE will be held in a United States dollar account to facilitate direct payments to farmers. However, if the exchange rate between USD and ETB fluctuates, savings backed by technical assistance will be established to provide farmers with access to additional credit. These savings accounts will be closely monitored and reported annually to ensure it supports agricultural and livestock production effectively.

#### Security challenges: The FP notes that security issues in northern Amhara could impact project implementation. To mitigate risks, the project will initiate activities in the more secure areas of the region first before expanding into other areas with local stakeholders to ensure security. The AE also emphasizes that it will work closely with the national designated authority to continuously monitor the situation and will halt disbursements if credible threats are identified.

#### Debt viability (and concessionality): The use of grants (i.e., 100 per cent concessional funding) is a key feature of the project. This includes the need for an estimated USD $16 billion by 2030 to achieve its climate goals, as outlined in the FP. These challenges are compounded by economic constraints like double-digit inflation, an overvalued exchange rate, and liquidity stress in the banking sector, according to the Economist Intelligence Unit operational risk assessment.

Additionally, the private sector’s risk aversion and the lack of suitable financial products limit private investment in critical areas such as

### 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

#### 71. The cross-cutting programme aims to strengthen rural climate resilience in the Amhara and Tigray regions of Ethiopia by increasing access to irrigation water from aquifers using solar PV technology. This intervention is expected to reach 2,038,694 beneficiaries through 172,220 (Co) eq over the 25-year technology lifespan and reach 2,173,221 beneficiaries (53% male and 47% female) by 2040. The project will also improve water management systems.

The methodology has allowed a systematic identification of adaptation benefits and beneficiaries for each adaptation impact in the communities.

# Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 26 January 2018 and became effective on 21 February 2018, which was amended and restated pursuant to a re-endorsement and restatement agreement which was signed on 30 October 2018 and became effective on 29 November 2018 (the "AMA").

The Federal Democratic Republic of Ethiopia has confirmed that it has obtained all internal approvals, and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

The proposed project will be implemented in the Federal Democratic Republic of Ethiopia. The GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, nor does it have any privileges or immunities.

The Boards of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities with respect to projects implemented by the GCF in Ethiopia without such rights and relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP243

## Proposal name:
Climate-resilient community access to safe water powered by renewable energy in drought-vulnerable regions of Ethiopia

## Accredited entity:
Ministry of Finance (MOF)

## Country:
Ethiopia

## Project size:
Small

### I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

1. **Introduction:**
   - Ethiopia is highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change, including more erratic rainfall, increased frequency and intensity of droughts, reduced agricultural productivity, and food and water security.
   - Vulnerable communities are being pushed deeper into poverty.

2. **Project Submission:**
   - The submission under the standard proposal approval process of the funding proposal titled "Climate-resilient community access to safe water powered by renewable energy in drought-vulnerable regions of Ethiopia" was reviewed by the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) based on the funding proposal package presented to the ITAP on 6 August 2024 and with revisions to the funding proposal and budget received on 19 August 2024.
   - The review included a detailed assessment, technical evaluation, and discussion of questions and answers further discussed in an online meeting between the ITAP and the accredited entity.

3. **Project Overview:**
   - The proposed project seeks to enhance climate resilience in the Robo Granna Valley in Amhara and the Borena Zone in Oromia.
   - By way of solar photovoltaic (PV) water pumping systems, the project aims to provide safe drinking water for households, schools, health centers, and livestock rearing.
   - The project has two main components: Component 1 focuses on infrastructure development, while Component 2 focuses on capacity building.

4. **Funding and Collaboration:**
   - Technical and financial assistance is provided by the ADB, the Ministry of Finance (MOF), previously named the Ministry of Finance and Economic Cooperation) and the executing entity, the Ethiopian Water Supply and Sanitation Development Agency.
   - The project also involves the Green Climate Fund (GCF) for climate action in energy, along with rural savings and credit cooperatives (RUSACCOs), all in collaboration with regional governments.

5. **Project Budget:**
   - This seven-year project has a total budget of USD 50 million.
   - It includes a requested grant contribution from GCF amounting to USD 45 million.
   - The USD 15 million co-financing is provided by MOF.

6. **ITAP Assessment:**
   - The ITAP assessed the funding proposal against the six GCF investment criteria.

### 1.1 Impact potential

The exact levels of abstraction will be capped through precise metering, a safeguard system that will be put in place to prevent unsustainable abstraction level.

10. The proposed project makes a solid effort to introduce smart water metering systems to monitor and manage water usage. This is very important but captures only part of the equation. It is recommended that the project also set up a system to monitor trends and fluctuations of the groundwater levels at each borehole. During the course of the preparatory phase and after further deliberations it was learned that the groundwater levels at each borehole will be periodically monitored by the Water Users Associations (WUA) and Irrigation User Associations (IUA). GCF recommends that the results of this monitoring be made part of the proposed project's formal monitoring system. The focus on water use efficiency is a very important long-term assurance or sustainability: the focus is the recommendation 1 in chapter II below.

13. A more immediate matter for the project is ensuring the realization of the intended adaptation benefits. This will require that the project team and its partners take advantage of the activities listed under input 1.3. These activities relate to facilitating the formulation of the Irrigation User Associations (IUA) and Water Users Associations (WUA), as well as providing training, technical assistance, all with a focus on gender analysis. In order for IUAs and WUAs to make good use of the water it is critical that they also be involved in the design and technology selection process. The project should ensure that the institutional capacity and payment systems are equally iteratively developed upon the selected technical solutions.

14. The implementation schedule contained in annex 5 to the funding proposal presently has the entire input 3 activities completed by the end of June 2017, while the completion of the construction and rehabilitation work should already have been completed. In the same way, the activity input 6.1 is expected to be completed at that time as well. It is recommended that the tariff will have to be designed for a water system that has already been constructed: "The IUA/representative committee should be involved in the design of tariffs and construction plans can be - if not co-created - at least vetted by communities" associations before construction begins.

15. The project will establish a small-scale revolving fund facility to enhance financial inclusion and support community members in accessing finance for agriculture, and for irrigation activities. This facility is expected to be established with the assistance of partners who collaborates with RISACOONs to manage this facility, providing returnable grants for the purchase of improved crop equipment and other inputs.

16. RISACOON are a form of community-based, member-based cooperative financial institutions designed to provide savings and credit services in rural areas. Members pool their savings and use them as capital to make loans to each other. The funds can be used for agricultural activities, small businesses or household needs. RISACOONs facilitate access to financial services by pooling resources and providing a collective voice. They are cooperatives and regulated under Ethiopian cooperative laws and operate on democratic principles where each member has one vote, regardless of the amount of their financial contribution. The proposed project will provide training for RISACOON members in financial literacy.

17. To ensure the effective management of the returnable grant, the project provides training to IUA and WUUA members through the RISACOONs in financial literacy and library.


It should be noted that annex 9 to the feasibility study contains an implementation schedule. The activities, subjects and their respective timeframes are contained within this document. It is recommended that the implementation schedule contained in annex 5 to the funding proposal. The partial implementation schedule in the feasibility study suggests that the project will complete all of its activities by June 2017. This is not possible as it does not include a list of assets proposed and includes only one activity (input 3) which is related to technologies that pertain to the adoption of the technology. The project also seeks to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. The mitigation potential has been calculated by comparing the project scenario against a reference case where groundwater is pumped from shallow wells and used for irrigation. The total area of irrigated land under diesel pumps in the target regions is estimated at 17,120 hectares of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2 eq) over the asset lifetime of 20 years (or an annual reduction potential of 6,597 t CO2 eq).

The calculation of greenhouse gas emission reductions is based on an assumption that diesel pumps would have been installed for the expansion of irrigation in the target regions. If this project were not implemented and the area under diesel pump irrigation remained constant, the likelihood of additional expansion based on diesel pumps occurring in parallel with the project can be deemed as uncertain whether or not the anticipated greenhouse gas emission reductions might be counteracted by other increases.

### Sustainable development potential

#### Scale: High

The proposed project seeks to produce a range of co-benefits from the massive application of solar PV pumped irrigation.

**1. Economic co-benefits:** Expected with the shift to modern irrigation are new job opportunities for local technicians and potentially in the upscaling crop and livestock production. Greater predictability of inputs (water) should help to reduce the level of "kittness" sales of water, which can lead to a reduction in transaction costs and greatly enhance productivity, which can lead to an economic upswing of the whole project area.

**2. Environmental co-benefits:** Including improved local air quality and reduced noise pollution from diesel engines, as well as the potential for reducing greenhouse gas emissions on those not installed since there is now solar PV pumping. Depending on the agricultural practices engaged in, there may be a positive impact on biodiversity. The extent to which water productivity and conservation are also enhanced, the risk of groundwater depletion will be lowered.

### Needs of the recipient

35. As highlighted in the funding proposal, Ethiopia’s precarious economic and environmental situation underlines the urgent need for comprehensive support. Ethiopia ranks fifth among countries most vulnerable to climate change (para. 48). The country faces moderate to very low coping and adaptation capacity (para. 248). Pastoral areas, located in deserts and semi-arid regions, are particularly vulnerable to droughts and desertification, land degradation due to overgrazing and poor land and water management (para. 59). Ongoing droughts have led to food insecurity, poverty, and security, and exacerbating problems related to population displacement and food aid dependence.

36. Economic strains are severe, with 70 per cent of the population living on less than USD 2 per day, and a high level of debt servicing (para. [159]). The country is also vulnerable to currency depreciation. It has been calculated that 10 per cent gross domestic profit loss has resulted from the economic impacts of climate change.


---
**Page 153**
---

## Water-related conflicts are on the rise, with different users competing for increasingly scarce water resources. Water access is also problematic for a great proportion of the population.

The funding proposal (para. [24]) highlights that access to clean drinking water varies between regions and communities. In some areas, women and children spend 30 minutes or longer to fetch drinking water (round trip). The feasibility study found that while in the Gobora Zone, there is a high level of access to piped water, in the Borena Zone, dominated more and more by Borana is strongly in need of expanding drinking water provision. In both zones, the majority of households rely on rainwater harvesting over reliance on exposed surface waters source due to more resilient resources is a key priority that benefits the most vulnerable populations.

### The Gobora Zone

The livelihoods of farmers in the Gobura Zone is primarily based on mixed farming system, including crop cultivation and animal rearing. Traditional farming systems rely heavily on rain-fed agriculture, which is highly susceptible to climate change impacts such as erratic rainfall patterns, temperature variations and crop pest infestations exacerbating the challenges faced by these farmers, leading to low agricultural productivity. Here, the project seeks to enhance resilience of livelihoods through adaptation strategies.

### The Borena Zone

In the Borena zone, communities rely largely on livestock production for their livelihoods, with a limited crop-livestock mixed farming system. Goats, sheep, cattle, donkeys and camels are the main livestock species in this zone. In addition to pastoralists who cultivate cereal and pulse crops, owing to erratic rainfall, not all cultivated land is currently productive. Livestock production systems have also been negatively affected by poor condition due to the impact of consecutive droughts in the previous years. Adaptation strategies include improving pasture feeding, moving herds to other/remote areas, and seeking other off-farm employment.

## 1.5 Country ownership

### Ethiopia submitted its first nationally determined contribution to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change in 2017, with a revised version in 2021. As explained at the first National Communication, Ethiopia has set itself an ambitious target of achieving a significant reduction in greenhouse gas emissions by 2030, aiming for carbon neutrality by that year. This ambitious target, contingent on international support and conducive investment environment, is supported by the Climate Resilient Green Economy (CRGE) strategy launched in 2011, focusing on mitigating emissions and enhancing resilience to climate change impacts. The CRGE strategy has identified the sectors of agriculture, energy, industry, water and energy and these sectors' pivotal roles in achieving both climate resilience and broader development goals.

### The proposed project is very clearly aligned with the climate policies of Ethiopia. This alignment should also be continuously ensured by the CRGE Facility Management Committee, chaired by the Ministry of Environment, Forests and Climate Change (MoEFCC) as a key impact.

### 42. The solar-based irrigation approach also constitutes a direct contribution to the Government of Ethiopia's 2030 target of scaling modern irrigation from 2 to 20 per cent of agricultural area by 2030.

### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium to High

Ethiopia is a highly indebted least developed country with limited budgeted resources.
The Government of Ethiopia, through MoF, made a credible contribution towards the proposed project, including the capitalization of the revolving fund. The USD $5 million contributed by the MoF will be used for the initial capitalization of the revolving fund budget.

1. The initial capitalization of USD 25,000 for the revolving fund will be allocated by the CIFD Facility within MoF. This amount will be distributed to the regional Bureaus of Finance in Addis Ababa and Dire Dawa to facilitate the establishment of revolving funds at the GCFDs and support the establishment of the revolving fund facility.
2. The grant facility is available to member countries for the establishment of a revolving fund facility, which ensures that each beneficiary can access up to USD 350 but does not clearly detail the repayment of the project or whether there are limits on how often beneficiaries can access grants within the grant period.

The MoF has clarified to the TAP that these details are to be developed through GCF-supervised technical assistance and will be included in the implementation plan and capacity-building of the revolving fund. The TAP also notes from the AE responses that the operation of the revolving fund is consistent with the principles of Islamic banking, intended to emphasize that the revolving fund facility is not a loan but rather an investment.
3. The MoF has assured that the financial products offered by the revolving fund are accessible to all beneficiaries, including those who adhere to Islamic banking principles.

The TAP also notes from the AE responses that the MoF will ensure that there are sufficient funding sources for the revolving fund, including contributions from local governments, private sector partnerships, and other donors. The TAP has requested further clarification on how this will be done to ensure sustainability of the facility in the long term.
4. Most of the project funding is destined towards the adaptation results area, as indicated in Appendix 1. Table 2 provides an estimate of the effectiveness of the proposed project's efficiency ratio. Still, the numbers seem to indicate a relatively high level of co-financing for the interventions for rehabilitated wells and beneficiary household or individual.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The ITAP finds that the proposed project "Climate-resilient community access to safe water powered by renewable energy in drought-vulnerable regions of Ethiopia", aligns with GCF funding priorities. The project is expected to contribute significantly towards achieving the goals towards enhancing livelihoods and climate resilience of vulnerable communities in the country.

## (a) The TREP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

### (b) To ensure the sustainability of the proposed initiative, the TREP recommends that the AE carry out the following:

#### Recommendation 1: monitor groundwater levels in both intervention areas and include these data as part of the project’s monitoring and evaluation framework. The development of the size of the resource is a critical piece of information that is completely missing from the current proposal.

#### Recommendation 2: re-sequence the order of outputs and activities in the implementation plan to ensure that the rehabilitation and construction activities (outputs under output 1.3) precedes and informs the rehabilitation and construction activities under output 1.4. This will allow for a more informed choice of technology and borehole siting, thereby aligning with the aspirations of farmer/pastoralist communities to guide the choice of technology and borehole siting. This way, tariffs and payment systems would be developed iteratively with both communities.

#### Recommendation 3: for the revolving fund, develop (i) an operational manual setting out the rules and procedures for the operation of the revolving fund; (ii) a strategy to leverage contributions from other sources to expand the revolving fund and ensure its sustainability beyond the project implementation period.

#### Recommendation 4: conduct a comparative analysis of the two pilot areas to facilitate the replication and uptake of successful elements to neighbouring areas in the future. The TREP recommends that particular and additional attention be placed on the demonstration and learning by doing, marketing processes and the testing of water pricing with regard to agricultural irrigation, livestock watering and domestic use.

#### Recommendation 5: review the process of support, training and awarding of certificates to the technicians to be trained in the operation and maintenance of solar PV water pumping systems. The TREP recommends that a specific gender balance target be set for a proportion or number of women to be included in the 100 or more technicians to be trained by the project.

Part I: Gender Assessment

## Background and National Context

Located in the Horn of Africa, Ethiopia is home to 105 million people (World Bank, 2018). Over 83% of the population are smallholder farmers, of whom 29% are female-headed households (MoF, 2019). Smallholder agriculture contributes over 85% of total employment, over 90% of foreign exchange earnings, and approximately 76% of GDP. The agricultural sector is dominated by subsistence farming, which accounts for nearly 90% of the total area under agriculture and these farmers provide more than 90% of total agricultural output (MoF, D, 2018).

The country’s economy has grown and development, as well as the livelihoods of the rural population, are highly dependent on the utilization of natural resources. As a result, growth in the agricultural sector can play a critical role in poverty reduction – i.e., a 1% increase in agricultural output leads to a decrease in poverty nearly 1% (World Bank, 2016a).

Eighty percent of the Ethiopian population currently live in rural areas. Recent rapid economic growth, however, signals the beginning of a demographic shift as urban services and industry are expanding rapidly (MoF, Bank, D, 2018). In this decade, Ethiopia’s economy grew at an average annual rate with slightly over 15%, exceeding the regional average of 5%. In this period, services grew by 12%, industry by 21% and agriculture by 9.7%.

Public investment, which increased from 5% of GDP in the early 1990s (Rudis, D., 2016) to 15-23% in 2020 (Terry M et al., 2022), plays a significant part in Ethiopia’s growth. More recently, foreign direct investment (FDI) has been increasing at an average annual rate of over 40%, with the largest inflows coming from China (CA, D, 2021). The sustained economic growth Ethiopia maintained over the past decade reduced the poverty rate twice as fast compared to the previous two decades.

However, in the past two years, the COVID-19 pandemic, desert locust invasions, erratic rainfall that disrupted the country’s dominant rain-fed agricultural sector, civil unrest, as well as cholera, measles and yellow fever outbreaks have all had a significant impact on Ethiopia’s economy, agriculture, health care, and livelihoods. As a result, Ethiopia’s economy grew at 6.1% in 2020, compared to 9% in 2019. Remittances declined by 10% in 2020, and Foreign Direct Investment inflows were 25% lower (DORF, D, 2021).

Ethiopia is among the poorest countries in the world. The Human Development Index (HDI), which measures a country’s average achievements across health, education, and standard of living, ranks Ethiopia low in the human development category: it ranks 173 out of 189 countries, with a value of 0.485. Almost half (49.6%) of the population is multidimensionally poor (UNDP, 2020).

Despite its very low global greenhouse gas emissions contribution (0.4% of global emissions), Ethiopia (MoF, D., 2018) has experienced significant climate change impacts over the past decade and in the last ten years. Evidence of extreme climate change impacts has become clear in Ethiopia. Temperatures have increased by an average of around 0.5°C per decade since 1973. The frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as droughts and floods have increased in the last ten years. These conditions are expected to continue, with significant implications for food security, access to water resources, natural resources, put infrastructure at risk and exacerbate environmental degradation. Therefore, sustainable adoption and resilience measures are crucial to manage vulnerability to climate risks and hazards (DORF, D, 2021).

Women constitute half of the Ethiopian population (49.87%) (World Bank, 2020) and 22.1% of the total head of households in Ethiopia (MoF, 2019). Almost all women are involved in agriculture to produce and environmental resources for their livelihoods and are engaged in productive activities (including crop farming and livestock herding) and the management of natural resources and household assets (AU, D, 2012).


3


---
**Page 162**
---

The major responsibility for household water supply, energy for cooking and heating, and food security falls on rural women, although they are often excluded by law (UNDP, universal rights), and discrimination (AU, 2012). Despite their significance, women have been marginalized or even ignored in major decision-making processes at all levels (MOPF, 2019).

Ethiopia Gender Profile

With a population of 105 million and a population growth rate of 2.86%, Ethiopia is the second-largest country in Africa (World Bank, 2019a). Of the total population, 50% are women; 44% are under the age of 15 and 4% are above the age of 65 (EPHII, 2021). Average household size is 5.2 persons in rural areas and 5.5 in urban areas (CSA, 2020).

Health

Health problems in Ethiopia are largely attributable to preventable infectious ailments and nutritional deficiencies. The health status of people, particularly women, is poor, mainly due to the higher rate of illiteracy and poverty among women, which has hindered their access to services, information, and decision-making on health matters (ECA, 2019).

The age at which childbirth commences is an important determinant of the health and well-being of a mother and her child. The average age for first birth in Ethiopia was 18 years old in 2015 (EPHII, 2021). The 2020 gender inequality index shows that, in Ethiopia, there are 6.87 births per 1,000 women aged 15-19.

Family planning is essential for women to avoid unplanned or unwanted pregnancies and prevent unsafe abortions. Additionally, contraceptive use enables women to space the births of their children, which benefits both motherhood and childbearing. The contraceptive prevalence rate in Ethiopia was reported that 96% of married women aged 15-49 know at least one method of contraception. The contraceptive prevalence rate in 2015 was 41%, and has increased recently from 34% to 48% (EPHII, 2021).

Health care services are essential for the survival and well-being of both the mother and the infant. Skilled care during pregnancy, childbirth, and the postpartum period is critical in reducing maternal mortality rates. The data shows that 74% of women aged 15-49 received antenatal care from a skilled provider, which has increased from 62% in 2015 to 83% in 2020. Similarly, the rate for delivery by skilled personnel has increased from 26% in 2016 and just 5% in 2005. The gender inequality index of 2020 shows that the mortality ratio for Ethiopia is 47 deaths per 100,000 live births (EPHII, 2021). This is below the SDG target of 70/100,000 by 2030. Although institutional delivery has been promoted in Ethiopia, home delivery is still common, primarily due to distance, scarce transport, and lack of appropriate facilities (EPHII, 2021).

Twenty percent of women aged 15-49 and 38% of men aged 15-49 have comprehensive knowledge of HIV. The prevalence rate for HIV among pregnant women in Ethiopia is 6%. This is a significant drop compared to female genital mutilation (FGM), 65% of women aged 15-49 (a decrease from 74% in 2003 and 80% in 2000) are circumcised. Among women who have heard of female circumcision, 24% believe that the


---
**Page 163**
---

practice is required by their religion and 18% believe that the practice should be continued (CSA 2017a). Though efforts have been observed since a national strategy and action plan were developed in Ethiopia in 2013 to address harmful traditional practices, it is evident that more work is needed in raising awareness and taking actions to eliminate the practice of FGM (MAGWYA 2012; CSA, 2017a).

The 2019 Ethiopian Demographic Health Survey shows that women's health indicators are higher for women in urban areas and for those that have at least a secondary education. This is a clear indication that a focus on education can significantly improve the health of both women and men in Ethiopia.

## Education

Literacy and skills in equipping the workforce with skills. Participation in a broader range of business, including more profitable and high-value-added sectors, depends on technical and vocational skill development (Hollward-Diemmerer, M., 2013). Education is strongly linked with socioeconomic variables such as lifestyle, income, and health.

The gender development index of the 2020 Human Development Report indicates that the mean year of schooling in Ethiopia is 1.7 years for females and 4.3 for males, while the 2020 gender inequality index shows that women have a median of 58% and 62.9% of males above the age of 15 with at least secondary educations (UNDP, 2020).

A survey conducted in 2018 shows that men are better educated than women in Ethiopia. About half of women (48%) and two-thirds of men (73%) have completed primary education. Urban women complete a median of 6 years of schooling while the median among rural women is zero. The corresponding figures for urban men are 9.3 and 2.9 years, respectively. Additionally, 48% of women aged 15–24 had no education compared to 10% of men (CSA, 2017a; EPHI, 2021).

In 2019, 35% of females attended some primary schooling, 6% completed primary schooling, and 8% completed secondary schooling and 7% completed secondary education. The percentage of women with no education fell from 75% in 2000 to 48% in 2016 and 40% in 2019 (FPeI, 2021).

Most individuals learn literacy skills through the formal education system, for which attendance has increased in the past decade, especially for girls and women (CSA, 2017a). Between 2000 and 2016, the percentage of children aged 5–14 years who attended school rose from 38% to 99%. Age-specific attendance rates for the population aged 6-24 show that 70% of children attend school by age 6. The rate increases to 90% at age 10 and 99% at age 15 (CSA, 2017a). The literacy rate among females aged 14–24 during these years attendance is higher for males than females (CSA, 2017a).

The median age for a mother's first birth in Ethiopia was 18.7 years (EPHI, 2021). Women who give birth in their teens are more likely to have another child within two years of the first birth and are more likely to continue to grapple with decisions related to fertility, motherhood, and the labor market (CSA, 2017a).

A report on adult learning shows that due to a persistent share of older, illiterate cohorts, overall gender literacy gaps remain. The government has made efforts to increase access to education for women through national education programs but are being made available closer to this generation of women. Children and adults aged 15 and up may enroll in Adult Literacy training to learn literacy skills, while any adult may enroll in

| 0% | 28% |
| 36% | 49% |

---
**Page 164**
---

community skills training centers to learn basic literacy, numeracy and other entrepreneurship and trade-related skills via regionally governed centers (MGE, 2008).

The gender gap in adult education is wide: while 70% of literate men are enrolled in adult education programs, only about half as many women are. Women who participate in such programs, since women are likely to experience more time and social constraints with age relative to men. In order to facilitate women's involvement in adult education, it may be necessary to provide child care services or offer financial incentives to offset time costs associated with attendance and travel (UN Women, 2014).

Buhenen, N., & Salisbury, T.V., 2017 find that education facilities may fail to offer girls women a safe, accessible environment. To address this issue, they recommend the establishment of community learning centers, provide safe transportation, provide female instructors or those that are attuned to the needs of female students, and organize regular parent-teacher meetings. Open discussions on cultural and social practices are essential to increase the participation of girls and women.

A study undertaken in Malawi and Uganda shows that when women's schooling increases, women's ideal family size decreases by 0.5 children. This is because education empowers women to make decisions about their ideal family size. As such, women's education can influence women's economic activity by improving skills development, increasing fertility rate-and increasing the amount of time women can contribute to the labor market (Beltman, 2013).

Participation in the formal and informal economy

According to the Ethiopia Socioeconomic Survey (ESS) of 2015-16, gender serves as a strong predictor of working hours. The survey found that women work an average of 49 hours per week while men work 58 hours per week. This is because women are 17% less likely than men to participate in the labor force. This difference widens to 23% when considering other factors such as age, education and marital status. Women who have completed secondary school or higher education are more likely to be in the workforce; while men work 31 hours per week, on average, women work only 27 (World Bank, 2019a).

Women in Politics

According to the World Bank report on women in politics data, Ethiopia ranks 25 (out of 182) for women in ministerial positions. The country ranks 34th out of 196 countries, while the country ranks 31st for women in parliament. Of the 700 members of the Ethiopian Federal Parliament Assembly (House of Peoples' Representatives and House of Federation), only 58 are women. This is higher than the sub-Saharan African average of 24.9% and the world average of 25.5%. Ethiopia ranks among the top 3 countries in Africa globally that have a woman president (World Bank, 2021).

Access to Resources

Asset Ownership

Assets such as land and business equipment serve not only as essential inputs, but also as potential collateral for credit. Half of all women in Ethiopia own it their name or on behalf of the household. However, only one-third of women who own land report having their name on a title deed (CA, 2017a). However, relative to male and male-headed households, women and female-headed households fare worse in land and asset ownership. Compared to female-headed households, men are more likely to be the owner of land or business equipment, have registered land and a larger fraction of registered land. Women in male-headed households are very rarely primary land managers; though the reverse is not the case for men in female-headed households (World Bank, 2017a).
