### Land as productive resource

According to the latest Economic Survey (ESS) of 2015-16, 74% of female farmers are widowed, divorced, or separated. In addition, on average, they have smaller household size, are five years and more are likely to be literate — 89% for boys vs. 59% for mothers (World Bank, 2019a).

Rural households, on average, own c.1 hectare of land; while, on average, male-headed households own 1.13 ha. Female farmers are more likely to be landless or have small plots of land. Women make up more than 40% of the agricultural labor force and head approximately 25% of all farming households, they have less access to productive resources such as land, inputs, credit, extension services, and markets.

In terms of gross value of output, female farmers produce 37 less hectare than male farmers. In addition, women face greater constraints in accessing agricultural inputs, credit, extension services, and the use of fertilizers and even compared to their male counterparts (O'Sullivan M. et al., 2014). The fact that female farmers are more likely to be landless or have small plots of land is a major constraint for them (World Bank).

These lower returns point to broader social norms, market failures and institutional constraints that prevent women's resources from translating into the same levels of agricultural productivity as they would for men (World Bank, 2019a). The government has recognized this challenge in its national development plan — so those in the national Ten Year Development Plan – including securing the rights of the 60% of women who are not given land rights (FODE, 2020).

### Services and Inputs

#### Extension services

Smallholder farmers access information about new technologies and other farm-related information through agricultural extension programs. In 2015-16, 38% of male farmers attended extension programs compared to 34% of female farmers (World Bank, 2019a). This gender gap is a major challenge in improving the adoption of new knowledge and management practices. Though policies have recognized the need to close the gender gap, identifying and addressing underlying constraints still remains a challenge (World Bank, 2019a).

#### Formal credit

Credit and other financial services can provide small-scale farmers with the opportunity to improve farm productivity by increasing their access to inputs such as fertilizers, pesticides, herbicides, and fungicides. Credit can, in the short term, help farmers increase their purchasing power to acquire necessary production inputs and reduce their vulnerability to shocks (Mukasa A.N., 2017). In the long run it can help farmers to make profitable investments that will improve farm productivity. However, women are less likely to live in a household with access to credit than male farmers (Mukasa A.N., 2017). Reasons include the fact that they have lower levels of education and training, which limits their ability to understand financial concepts; lower levels of human and social capital which, in turn, can reduce their eligibility for formal credit. When credit is available it may be used sub-optimal levels of productive inputs, merely limiting their productive capacity (Mukasa A.N., 2017).

#### Production inputs

Modern agricultural practices such as fertilizers, pesticides, herbicides, and fungicides, are used to increase agricultural productivity and protect farmers against harvest fluctuations linked to pests, adverse weather, and soil degradation. However, women farmers have less access to these inputs than their male counterparts at all levels of these agricultural inputs than their male counterparts, which limits productivity and may imply greater vulnerabilities to shock-induced variations in production. Reasons vary from these products are typically sold in larger quantities or require specialized knowledge for application (World Bank, 2019a). Women tend to mobility where limited transport options are available that affect access to both inputs and markets (World Bank, 2008b).


---
**Page 166**
---

Access to Irrigation

Although studies show that benefits target both women and men farmers, women still benefit much less from irrigation investments due to lower access to information, including training (Likhnyshk, N., et al., 2017; FDRE, 2007). Men mostly control the use of irrigation technologies and have more control over income from their land than women (Amanyesh, N. et al., 2017).

Level of income and savings

In 2009, the average wage in Ethiopia was only one-third of the Sub-Saharan African average and less than one-fifth of the global average (World Bank, 2016). In 2012, the monthly average real income was ETB 421.70 (USD 23.40), less than USD 1.25 per day (Tadesse, F. and Kefalehi, K., 2015). Low levels of productivity and investment limits only control about one-third wage growth.

Both formal and informal sectors indicate that farmers earn 44% less per hour than their male counterparts. This disparity drops to 36% when individuals, households, and job-level characteristics are taken into consideration (World Bank, 2016a).

The gender wage gap is partly explained by gender differences in education, experience, and training (Arbache J.S., et al., 2010). Women with secondary education help individuals to develop more advanced skills to garner higher wages. Data show that employees who hold a bachelor's or postgraduate degree earn about 35% less than men holding the same degree; those with only primary-secondary education, and a 20% higher wage than those who only completed their primary education (World Bank, 2016a).

Furthermore, women’s limited labor market skills pigeonhole them into jobs concentrated in low-profitability sectors, with more women working in informal wage employment than men (Arbache J.S. et al., 2010). 37% of women work in agriculture compared to only 25% for men; this is due to the nature of their tasks and trends of irregular employment contribute to women’s limited on-the-job training, lower professional development opportunities, and a perpetuation of disparities in skill sets, job opportunities and wages (World Bank, 2016a).

Norms and Practices

Shared beliefs or ideas about which behaviors are appropriate, typical, or desirable in a particular social group are referred to as social norms (Padlock E.L. and Bell L., 2010). Although norms do not dictate behavior, they influence the likelihood of particular behaviors by establishing expectations of rewards and approval for certain actions and sanctions against others. Social norms can be seen as a reflection of what men and women are, and should be, different in behavior, aspirations, status, and economic activity (Gibson G., 2013). These social norms are often reinforced through the family and community and play an important role in life such as the timing and dynamics of marriage, childbearing, household dynamics, asset ownership and resource allocation.

Marriage and Childbearing

Marriage in Ethiopia occurs early in life, with the median age at first marriage for women standing as the lowest in Eastern Africa at 17 years (Clark S. et al., 2017). Both social norms and economic pressures may result in young girls being married before they are ready to bear children or complete their education. Girls may be motivated into moving into adulthood through marriage and motherhood rather than through education and employment opportunities, which can lead to a lack of investment in human capital (World Bank, 2016a). Economic pressures also motivate marriages, leading parents to arrange their daughters’ marriages in order to secure dowry payments. Parents may also marry off their daughters early so that they are less likely to spend time acquiring valuable skills for economic success. Therefore, delaying marriage may result in better educational and economic outcomes for women in Ethiopia (World Bank, 2016a).


---
**Page 167**
---

# Career and Family

Women may be forced to avoid job opportunities that will minimize the time they can give to caring for family members and the household. Such choices will impact lifetime earnings and contribute to the gender gaps in wages and profits (World Bank, 2018).

Further evidence shows increasing trade-off between career and family as they enter roles with higher pay and responsibility, in part discouraging women from aspiring to particular occupations or positions. In a study of large companies, including Ethiopian Airlines, Etihad Telecom and Wells International Bank, found that women were more likely than men to leave their jobs due to their "inability to say no", the nature of their company and their work, and the imbalance of their responsibility and their family's demands (World Bank, 2018).

## Intra-household Dynamics

In Ethiopia, the majority of domestic work is delegated to women, including child rearing, cleaning, food preparation, wood and water collection, and food production. Ethiopian women aged 18-19 spend 4.1 hours per day on these tasks compared with only 2.5 hours by men (World Bank, 2016). Women are much more likely than men to spend time collecting water and fuel wood; about 45% of female household members engage in this activity daily, compared with only 25% of male members (CSA, 2020).

In addition, many studies document the large amounts of time women devote to livestock production. In Oromia, Amhara and Tigray regions, women divide their time between agricultural and domestic activities for about 14 hours a day on both productive and non-productive tasks compared with an average of 10 hours spent by men (Agboola et al., 2011). According to UN Women, women in Ethiopia spend 75% of on-farm labor in post-harvest activities that account for about 60% of farming activities.

These responsibilities hinder women's opportunities to study, develop professional experience and skills, run a business or engage in paid work. 10% of girls drop out of school to look after siblings and 12% of boys drop out of school due to family issues (Finn M. & Robertson C., 2013).

## Internalized Beliefs

Women's and men's subjective self-assessment capabilities contribute to gender gaps. Ethiopian gender gap in agricultural productivity is partly explained by women's lower self-assessment of their ability than for which either men or women have a perceived natural advantage. On the other hand, when gender is said to be irrelevant to the task, men and women show no difference in self-perceived competence (World Bank, 2018).

Violence against women affects a woman's physical and mental health, as well as her ability to engage in daily activities. Fear of violence can also reduce women's willingness to pursue economic activities, especially when they are married. The CSA (2017a) found that 63% of Ethiopian women have experienced sexual violence while one-third of ever-married women have experienced spousal violence (CSA (2017a), 63% of

## II. Gender and Climate Change

Although climate change is global in its extent and impacts, Africa has been identified as highly vulnerable to climate change due to low adaptive capacity and high reliance on climate-sensitive sectors such as rainfed agriculture, livestock production, fisheries, and forestry. Among the various climatic variables, temperature extremes are the two most important variables of climate change, imposing a negative effect on the productivity of crops and livestock in sub-Saharan Africa (Sardesey, O., et al., 2017; Abena, K. et al., 2018; Ataae, A. et al., 2018; Gakossoh, M. et al., 2019).

Despite its very low global greenhouse gas emission (0.4%) contribution (Crippa, M. et al., 2019), Ethiopia is one of the sub-Saharan African countries that is highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change and variability.

In the past 50 years, evidence of climate change impacts has become apparent in Ethiopia. Recurrent droughts, combined with changes in the amount and spatial distribution of seasonal and annual rainfall, are among the most climate-related developments evident in Ethiopia (Zeleke et al., 2017; Woldesenay, Z.K. and Teda, D.G., 2018).

Agriculture is a major sector in Ethiopia, contributing significantly to the country's economy through its production, distribution and trends will have a direct impact on agricultural production and thus significantly affect the lives of rural smallholders who depend largely on agriculture as their main source of income (Debalasa, A. and Bhattacharjee, H.G., 2021).

A study by Stoltenberg, R. et al. (2021) on agricultural productivity change induced by climate change up to the year 2050 in Ethiopia shows that there will be a significant increase in temperature over the next few decades, with increased severity over the time period. As a result, food prices will be higher and this will lower Ethiopian GDP growth, reduce real household incomes, and increase poverty rates. Overall, the study projected an average decline of 10% to 25% in agricultural GDP by 2050. Poor rural households will be more affected than urban and non-rural farming households. Since agriculture has long been a major source of income for poor rural households, the impact on these households is likely to be severe.

The study also projected that the share of agriculture in total employment would decline from about 41% in 2015 to between 36% and 37% by 2050. The study further suggested that the overall performance of the economy is of paramount importance. The key recommendations of the study are increasing agricultural productivity, improving access to markets for farmers, providing better services to farmers, and integrated policy options, including changes in modern technology and enhanced awareness.

Another study done in the Rabi Watershed, northwestern highlands of Ethiopia, indicates that both seasonal and annual rainfall patterns across the watershed vary extremely and exhibit high temporal and spatial variability. The study also showed that there has been a significant increase in temperature over the last few decades, with higher variability or less reliability of rainfall over the last few decades, notably with higher variability of Belo – July period precipitation (1970-2018) compared to the 1950s and 1960s. Projections by the study suggested that the Kiremt rainfall will probably increase by 20–25% in 2050 relative to its baseline value, while the Belo-July period precipitation is projected to decrease.

A greater warming trend for both current and future scenarios was observed in the Rabi watershed. The mean annual temperature in the study area has increased by 1.7°C over the last four and a half decades, with an increase of about 0.35°C per decade (Abena et al., 2018). Similarly, significant warming trends were found in Lake Tana sub-basin (Abera, K. et al., 2018) and Tekezé basin (Fikru, F. et al., 2018).


---
**Page 169**
---

A high concentration or rainfall over a few months—particularly in July and August—which accounts for more than 50% of total Kiremt rainfall—results in severe flooding events and soil erosion, thereby posing a threat to agricultural production (Desalew, M.M., and Bhat, H.G., 2011). Flooding and waterlogging result in anaerobic stress in roots, which significantly reduce crop yield (Fries, L. et al., 2014), while at the same time, it increases soil erosion (Desalew, M.M., and Bhat, H.G., 2011). This phenomenon is also linked to climate change (Mahajan and Joshi, 2013). Excessive rainfall also leads to a high rate of surface runoff and soil erosion that causes the loss of fertile topsoil. The effects are more pronounced in areas with no vegetation cover or in the absence of proper soil conservation structures (Fries, L. et al., 2014). Loss of fertility consequently leads to losses in crop yields.

Increases in growing season temperatures may also adversely affect crop production, farm income and food security in many ways, especially when combined with high inter-annual and intra-seasonal variability of rainfall. An increase in temperature is expected to reduce the yield of crops such as wheat, barley, sorghum, maize, and millet (Mahajan and Joshi, 2013). Heat stress also reduces grain yield by increasing evaporation and reducing water availability (Hatifness and Kassam, 2005), especially in rain-receiving downstream areas.

The challenges of having a high concentration of rainfall are Kiremt season while, other months remain dry or receive only light precipitation. As such, the wet weather is unable to provide sufficient moisture for their small parcel of plots in a given year, which leads to food insecurity and poverty (Desalew, M.M. and Bhat, H.G., 2011).

A projected increase in annual and seasonal rainfall would have mixed implications for future agriculture. On the one hand, it could contribute to future agriculture under proper soil conservation practices to control associated risks of flooding and erosion. However, if not managed properly or if rainwater is poorly managed, the projected increase in Kiremt rainfall will lead to excessive flooding, runoff, and soil loss, thus contributing to food insecurity (Desalew, M.M., and Bhat, H.G., 2011).

Livestock production is also very important in supporting rural livelihoods. It contributes about 17% of the gross domestic product (GDP) of Ethiopia and 39% of the agricultural GDP (Shiferaw, B. et al., 2017). However, livestock production has been declining over time due to a number of factors such as lack of land for livestock rearing, poor feed availability, and high mortality rates among animals that have many livestock-producing households in poverty (Retterberg, S. et al., 2017).

Approximately 65% of the total population is engaged in agriculture (Shiferaw, B. et al., 2017) while estimate that 60 million ha of rangelands are grazed in Ethiopia and that livestock consumes 120% of the annual forage production in average weather years. The forage densities are higher in drought years and have been aggravated by climate change (Retterberg, S. et al., 2017). However, the quality of forage has declined.

Retterberg, S. et al. (2017) report that most of the rangelands in Ethiopia is already degraded and has sparse vegetation cover with low productivity. The livestock population in Ethiopia is also increasing rapidly. However, the ability to move livestock to different areas is diminishing and there is a pressing need to develop other approaches to reduce animal mortality rates and support production during droughts. The Government of Ethiopia (GoE) has been implementing various strategies for rangeland management whereby are being increasingly enclosed and privatised. As a result, the future productivity of livestock and crop production will be affected by climate change. This is because the livestock sector in Ethiopia is also at risk from climate variability and change (Ng'ang'a, B., et al., 2020).

A study done on the health impacts of climate change reveals that the biggest climate challenges they are facing are reduced rainfall, erratic rainfall, recurrent droughts, and loss of seasonality (Samueli, L. et al., 2021). Communities are traveling greater distances to access sufficient grazing lands, and this is leading to vegetation degradation in rangelands. This has also led to increased livestock mortality rates as well as an increase in disease outbreaks in animals due to increased mixing of different herds.

The reduction in milk production from dairy cows and the uncertain crop harvests are having impacts on the community's health. Reduced availability of animal milk, utilization of unsafe water


---
**Page 170**
---

sources and seasonal shifts in climate-sensitive diseases such as malaria are among the many challenges they are facing (Santos, L., et al., 2021).

Climate change is also having a significant importance of pastoralism versus sedentary crop and livestock farming in arid and semi-arid areas of Ethiopia since traditional cattle varieties grazed in Ethiopia may be more adapted to climate change than the introduced breeds that are used for dairy and meat production (Rettberg, S. et al., 2017). Improved production practices, such as reduction of degraded rangelands, can reduce GHG emissions per unit of livestock product by up to 50% over a period of years (Ng'ang'a, S. et al., 2020) and can reduce the GHG emissions per unit of animal product by up to 30% in some cases (Vetereans sans Frontières, 2016). However, if human populations increase, total GHG emissions may increase despite these improvements.

Improved livestock production practices can thus have positive and negative impacts on GHG emissions. The positive effects include increased productivity of livestock products which can improve food security and provide more carbon sequestration and storage in the soil, while the potential negative effects stem from increases in the ruminant livestock population, which increases enteric emissions of methane. Increases in livestock product yield per unit area may also increase land use for livestock production (Ng'ang'a, S., et al., 2020) and thus where intensive production methods are raising livestock intensively (Ng'ang'a, S., et al., 2020).

The impacts of climate change on pastoralists in Ethiopia have been well documented. There is a general understanding that since climate change has gender-differentiated impacts, policies, programs, and interventions need to address the impact of climate change and adaptation responses differently for men and women (Alemayehu, S., et al., 2013). Women are more vulnerable than men in terms of food security and access to resources. They also have a greater responsibility to avoid further increase in their vulnerability gap (MIFR, 2019).

A study by Tesfamicheal, W. (2016) shows that although all rural Ethiopian households are vulnerable to climate change, the magnitude of the effect differs, and female headed households are more vulnerable than male-headed households. The study found that women's vulnerability is higher in terms of climate variability, while income declined by 57% in male headed households. Given that the study exposed both types of households to similar climatic conditions, it can be concluded that women have less control over resources and endowments and adaptability. It is expected, therefore, that female-headed households will become absolutely more vulnerable than male-headed households.

Women and female-headed households are the most vulnerable, least prepared, and likely to be worst affected by climate change. Their limited access to, and control over, resources and information, and their limited ability to cope with shocks and stresses make them particularly vulnerable in times of natural change (Akiu., A. et al., 2013; Alebachew., S. I., 2011; Tesfamicheal., W., 2016).

Securing food, water, and energy for the household, as well as maintaining overall household well-being, is often a woman's responsibility in many rural communities (Alemayehu et al., 2013). As droughts and rising temperatures place greater pressures on women. Furthermore, duremances emergencies are also usually formed by women who are responsible for feeding their families and taking care of the children without necessarily having the right skills and knowledge (MoNATR, 2017). A study by Alebachew, A. (2011) shows that some female-headed households have migrated to urban areas in search of better livelihoods but never return, as they establish new lives at their destinations. Migration can thus increase the level of malnutrition due to increased scarcity of food and leading to health status of the communities left behind.

Women often engage in a greater number of gendered or pursuing processing, and managing food and other resources for household consumption, and are also often responsible for livestock production that is responsible for larger livestock farming and cropping. In these contexts, responsibility for adaptation to climatic and biological changes falls on women (Alemayehu et al., 2013). Women have limited access to alternative ways to feed their household members (ALU, 2012). This is more pronounced for female-headed households where the lack of resources makes it difficult for them to adapt to climate change. A case study in different parts of the country reveals that the extent of involvement of female-headed households in adaptation and mitigation activities varies, while the proportion is higher in women-owned male-headed households (Leuleleged, K., et al., 2015).


---
**Page 171**
---

Adaptation preferences in male-headed households tend to focus on on-farm adaptation measures, such as cropping time adjustment, crop diversification, planning for water (such as wind and touchwood) and soil conservation, while off-farm and non-farm diversification adaptation measures are preferred by female-headed households (Akeb, A. and Van Laarhoven, F., 2016). Male-headed households are more mobile, which makes them better suited to migrate in search of water or other resources during labor migration during bad harvest times – which is usually not an option for female-headed household heads as they have a greater responsibility towards the elderly and the sick, as well as the cattle (Akeb, A. and Van Laarhoven, F., 2016; Akiliu, A. et al., 2013).

Increased water stress, increased poverty, and delay or failure of rains are additional climatic impacts of climate change. Women use water for domestic purposes while men usually use it for agriculture-related functions: women and men therefore often have different needs and priorities when it comes to water access.

In drought-prone areas affected by desertification, for example, the time required for water collection increases significantly (Akeb, A., Van Laarhoven, F. and Van Laarhoven, F., 2016). As a result, they are forced to spend more hours fetching water, which significantly increases their workload and potentially exposes them to harassment, especially in areas and times of the day when women are most vulnerable (Akeb, A., Van Laarhoven, F. and Van Laarhoven, F., 2016). In such a context, and during chronic drought and famine years, the daily work schedule may extend to 18-18 hours and beyond.

According to a time-use survey by CSA (2014), rural households in Ethiopia obtain water mostly from wells or from public/private taps outside their homes. Thirty percent of rural households have access to protected wells (boreholes) for more than 95% of the time, while 38% get most of their water from a shared/ community tap, and 25% from natural sources (rivers, springs, etc.). About 22% obtain water from a shared /community tap, and less than 1% of households have access to protected wells for more than 95% of the time. This implies that they spend an appreciable amount of time collecting water. About 56% of rural households have to travel less than 1 hour to get water, while 32% need between 1 and 4 hours, and only about 10% need more than 4 hours to collect water for both water. Poor access to safe drinking water, coupled with literacy (73%) and water-borne disease prevalence (65%), are major constraints on women’s education and training in education, agriculture production, and other development activities (Gedachew, D., 2016).

**Page 172**

Therefore, easy access to water mostly benefits women and girls as it reduces the burden of water collection and fetching which falls on them and makes time available for education and economically productive activities. It also reduces the physical challenges they face (i.e., exposure to physical hardship, sexual and physical violence), when they travel long distances to fetch water (UN Women, 2014). Yet, achieving this goal is not easy as it requires a gender-sensitive approach that takes into account the sustainability of groundwater use (Likimiyekh, N. et al., 2017).

An assessment made by a project implemented in Oromia and SNIPR that provided women and men farmers with water-based technologies showed that the technologies near households enabled multiple uses in addition to irrigation (Likimiyekh, N. et al., 2017). The assessment showed that both women and men use these technologies for different purposes. Women use water from these technologies mainly for irrigation, women and children collect water for domestic use while men use it for livestock watering and other productive uses. For these reasons, a water-based project should give special attention to gender-based needs and concerns in order to maximize opportunities for water access and governance or social norms against women (World Bank, 2016b).

Access to resources, including information, affects the likelihood of technologies improving the livelihoods of farmers. Likimiyekh, N., et al. (2017) found that, in their study area, men have greater access to information and are more likely to participate in group meetings than women. This is partly due to male dominance, cultural influence and women simply not being invited to meetings, as well as inability of women to attend meetings due to domestic workload. Due to the same reasons, women are reluctant to participate in groundwater monitoring.

Therefore, projects must invest more effort in reaching and informing women, including understanding the issues and challenges they face when accessing information and participating in group meetings or for information-sharing events and meetings, and not rely on spouses or men in the community to inform women. In addition, it is important that women are included as active participants in programs to be built upon gender mainstreaming approaches; the result is often nothing more than a satisfied quota (i.e., a token of inclusion) rather than an actual change in power relations and women's actual participation or influence in decision-making (Letore, N. et al., 2017). Therefore, it is imperative that steps are taken to ensure that women have the opportunity to participate as active participants in programs due to domestic responsibilities, and social norms that discriminate against them (Likimiyekh, N. et al., 2017; Letore, N. et al., 2017). This is because women often bear a disproportionate share of the workload due to their role as caregivers and homemakers, which limits their ability to engage in productive activities and diversifying capacity and diversifying their narrow livelihoods and income sources are put in place, climate variability and extreme weather events can have devastating effects on the well-being of smallholder farmers and pastoralists, with women bearing the brunt of the impact due to their responsibilities for household management and food security (ALU, 2012).

The development of the Climate Resilient Green Economy (CRGE) strategy in 2011 has provided a strong basis for climate change adaptation strategies. The CRGE was developed as part of the Ethiopia Climate Change Strategy (FDRE, 2011; MoF, 2011a). In 2012, the Ethiopian government established the CRGE Facility as a financial mechanism to support the implementation of the CRGE strategy and other climate change adaptation strategies (MoF, 2016b). Although studies indicate that the CRGE Strategy, as well as the Climate Resilient Strategies for Agriculture and Forests, have been effective in improving the livelihoods of smallholder farmers and pastoralists (Alemu and Van Laethem, 2019; MoF, 2019), recent efforts by the CRGE Facility have tried to ensure gender is taken into account in the implementation of programs and projects managed through the Facility (MoF, 2021b).

### Gender and climate: institutional, legal and policy frameworks

#### Legal and policy frameworks

The Government of Ethiopia has demonstrated strong policy commitments to bringing about gender equality and women's empowerment, including in the socio-economic and political arenas. It has signed and domesticated a number of international instruments related to gender equality and human rights, including the Convention on the Elimination of all forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), the Beijing Platform for Action, the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs), the Addis Ababa Protocol, the Maputo Plan of Action, and the Malabo Declaration.

Further, the Ethiopian Constitution guarantees equality before the law: equal rights to land, property, employment, education, and inheritance. The law also prohibits discrimination on grounds of gender in marriage. Provision is also made for affirmative action to address the historical legacy of discrimination.

The 1993 National Ethnographic Women's Policy and the 2006 National Action Plan for Gender Equality, accompanied by the Government's Development Strategy and Change Package, have served to mainstream gender issues into the country’s social, economic and political development agenda.

Furthermore, the Ten-Year Development Plan (2021-2030), which is the country's overarching development strategy, includes women's rights, representation, and access to resources as one of the key areas of focus. The National Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (NCCSAP) 2017-2025 is expected to be implemented across all sectoral ministries, guided by the National Gender Responsive Budgeting Framework (NGRB).

The Women's Affairs Office was upgraded to a Ministry in 2005 and was restructured as the Ministry of Women and Social Affairs in 2021. Proclamation no 691/2010 expanded the Ministry’s mandate to render comprehensive services for women, including addressing the effects of the Women’s Affairs Directorate (WADs) established in the sectoral ministries (March 2018).

In a similar manner, several legislative and policy frameworks have been established to provide directions on how to mainstream gender equality into climate change policies. These include provisions from early climate alone change mitigation and adaptation processes to the mainstreaming of climate change into decision-making at all levels. The following table provides an overview of different policy documents that present such provisions.

| National Policy on Ethiopian Women, 1993 | The policy outlines the major economic, social, and political concerns of Ethiopian women and includes broad themes related to gender equality (Government of Ethiopia, 1993). Since then, major programs have been designed to be gender-sensitive or to have gender components, and women's affairs have often been included in national development plans. |
| --- | --- |
| The Revised Family Code, 2006 | The earlier Family Code granted permission to married women to control assets or pursue a profession, but it failed to offer protection to unmarried or widowed women. The 2000 Revised Family Code better protects the rights of women and children in marriage and divorce by providing for the dissolution of marriage, requiring equal asset division between the husband and wife upon divorce (FDOE, 2006). |
| In an attempt to improve women's ability to earn, work and thrive outside of the home, the 2000 Revised Family Code lowered the minimum age of marriage. A total of five regions in Ethiopia have done this; two regions and two charter cities had implemented the change. The decreased marital age has increased participation in the labor market, particularly for young women. In the five regions, labor force participation rose by 15-20% more than regions that had not yet implemented the change (Haldane-Dimmerle, M., & Gagio, G.C., 2013). |

# Water Resources Management Proclamation: 2009

The Ethiopian Water Resources Management Proclamation (WRMP) is the main living policy governing the water resources sector. The theme of the WRMP revolves around the sustainability and equability of water uses; and crossing-cutting issues in general. The WMP indicates that management of water supply and sanitation services should be based on a participatory approach, which will ensure efficient use of water for the full participation of the users for effective decision-making (FSE, 2006). The main constraint surrounding the WRMP is the lack of capacity at all levels to implement it. In Ethiopia, most of the major river basins: 80-90% of the water resource is found in four river basins; namely, Abbay (Blue Nile), Tekezé, Omo and Awash. The majority of the population lives in these river basins. However, about 30-40% of the national population is found (Israel K. and Merkin M., 2020).

## The Ethiopian Water Resources Management Policy and Water Sector Strategy: 2001

The Policy and Strategy document was developed to promote the full involvement of women in planning, implementation, decision making and training, as well as empower them to play a leading role in water-related activities. The Strategy (MOWR, 2001) emphasizes the importance of women with the aim to:

- **Pay special attention to the role of women establishing inclusive gender-based structures for the management of localized water supply and sanitation (WSS) and small-scale irrigation systems. Allocate a specific number of seats for women in these community-based structures, depending upon their needs.**
- **Enhance the active involvement of women for the success of water projects and programs, and for the sustainability of water resources. Launch campaigns to encourage women to contribute to improved management of water schemes.**
- **Take measures to ensure that women have access to clean drinking and cooking water for the family by empowering them in decision-making in water projects.**

## Land Registration Act: (FDRE, 2003)

The Act sought to grant equal inheritance and property rights to women. It facilitated land registration of households and provided a legal basis for issuing certificates. To ensure transparency, land certificates were issued after public registration. Furthermore, the Ethiopian land certification scheme required that land administrators should be accompanied by at least one female member (Höldén et al., 2011). The presence of female members in the land administration committees is also a reflection of women’s empowerment and their role in land management. Ethiopia’s land registration process increased tenure security for women (World Bank, 2019a). A study across 15 villages found that women were more likely to be involved in land-related activities under the Land Act shift perceptions and social norms related to the division of assets upon divorce (World Bank, 2019b).

## Climate Resilient Green Economy (CRGE) Strategy: 2011

The CRGE Strategy is a national strategy for climate adaptation and resilience building measures into the country’s development planning objectives. It has recognized agriculture, health, water and energy, buildings and infrastructure as key sectors that are vulnerable to climate change impacts. More than 150 potential green growth opportunities were identified, of which 60 were prioritized for inclusion.

Studies show that the CRGE Strategy is weak in terms of identifying gender equality issues and in elaborating on how women can benefit from the implementation of the strategy (MOP, 2019). The initiative that mentions the potential positive impact on women is the Rural Energy and Efficient Stoves Initiative, where the potential to contribute to gender equality is indicated without any specific detail (MOP., 2019).

Despite the Strategy recognizing the effects of climate change on people’s livelihoods and social well-being in a variety of sectors, it does not provide any information about how women are affected by these changes. The Strategy provides no explanation for how the gendered nature of climate change problems and their solutions can be addressed.

Following the creation of the CRGE Facility in 2012 and the development of sector climate-resilient strategies in 2015, the Facility has recognized the need to have deeper gender integration across its programs.


---
**Page 175**
---

priority sectors, the institutional architecture and program implementation, and has taken some steps towards this in recent years (MoF, 2021a).

Climate-Resilient Strategy for Agriculture and Forestry (FDERE, 2015a). The Climate Resilient Strategy for Agriculture and Forestry sets out to ensure climate-resilient economic growth in Ethiopia. It focuses on three sub-sectors identified as being the most vulnerable to the impacts of climate change: agriculture, forestry, and fisheries.

As indicated in previous sections, agriculture is a sector where the role of women is very significant and there is, therefore, a need to identify potentials for equitable participation and benefit from investments generated by climate-resilient agricultural practices. The Climate Resilient Strategy for Agriculture rarely mentions the terms gender, women, or females, and when it mentions any of these terms hardly any explanation is provided on how this will be addressed in practice. It is important that such sections should be in the instances where the terms are used. It portrays women as being impacted by climate change but does not specify particular mechanisms to address their vulnerability (Arias, A., and Van Laarhoven, F., 2019).

Further, the Strategy defines its actions into themes, which are further categorized under nine themes. One of these themes is a sectoral theme for high-profile action areas. However, the remaining themes (capacity building and institutional coordination, information and awareness, crop and water management, livestock development, market development, sustainable agriculture and land management, natural resources conservation and management, disaster risk reduction) include no references to gender and women’s issue.

Climate-Resilient Strategy for Water and Energy (FDERE, 2015b). The Climate Resilient Strategy for Water and Energy aims at economic growth and poverty reduction by analyzing the vulnerability of Ethiopia's water resources and energy sector to climate change. It is aimed for the impacts of future climate change in the water and energy sectors to build climate resilience in Ethiopia.

A review of the Strategy indicates that the Strategy includes few references to the impact of climate change on women. For instance, even though there is an overall statement that identifies the positive contribution of improved access to clean water and sanitation to women’s empowerment, it does not specify what will be done and how the changes will come about. Further, the vulnerability assessment does not consider gender as a factor in its analysis. The Strategy includes several action areas under strategic priorities; only one (the development of the gender action plan) refers to women's issues (MoF, 2019).

On a positive note, the Strategy does recognize a few gender-based issues, including the impacts of lack of access to modern energy and water on women’s health and well-being. These include issues such as education and employment, their health and lack of access to clean water and sanitation. Moreover, although it has yet to materialize, the Strategy has committed to developing a gender action plan (MoF 2019).

Climate-Resilient Strategy for Transport (FDERE, 2015c). The Climate Resilient Strategy for Transport sets the framework to deliver an integrated, modern transport system that is safe, reliable and affordable with high customer service. A review by MoF (2019) indicates that this Strategy is completely gender-blind with regard to its contents.

Gender Equality Strategy for the Agriculture Sector (MoA-NRIT, 2017). The Gender Equality Strategy for the Agriculture Sector aims at addressing these through capacity building of staff on gender-sensitive planning, programming, and service delivery. It also emphasizes the need to strengthen the institutional framework that supports gender equality in agricultural national structures and systems in Ethiopia to increase the profitability and productivity of women in the agriculture sector. Furthermore, it recognizes the importance of collaboration and partnership with other relevant ministries to promote gender equality are also among its strategic objectives.

---

# Women's Development and Change Package and Strategy (MoWCA, 2017a,b)

The Women's Development and Change Package recognizes that women farmers have limited access to extension services and highlights services that would benefit women, including input use, labor-saving technologies, and training in post-harvest handling, storage, processing, marketing, and agro-processing.

The Women's Development and Change Strategy lists a set of interventions related to ownership, access, and use rights. Among these are: encouraging sharecropping where women lack the required labor to cultivate their land, ensuring women obtain fair sharecropping agreements, assigning plots to landless women, and providing training on land tenure rights.

# Ethiopia's National Adaptation Plan (NAP-ETH), 2019

NAP-ETH is a key element of the country’s response to climate change. Under the framework of the CRGE Strategy, the NAP-ETH aims at contributing to achieving the goal of reducing vulnerability to climate change by building adaptive capacity and resilience (FIRE, 2019). A gendered analysis report to inform the development of the NAP-ETH was commissioned in 2018. The analysis identified three main issues that need attention: (i) limited access to information on adaptation needs, opportunities, and costs; (ii) limited needs, opportunities, and cost; (iii) equitable participation and influence in adaptation decision-making processes; and (iv) lack of gender-sensitive climate change adaptation investments.

The document further elaborates on actions to be taken to address these issues, with the aim of providing a basis for the development of an NAP-ETH that is gender-responsive and inclusive (FIRE, 2019). The NAP implementation roadmap was developed in 2020 and identifies 5 implementational strategies which focus on agriculture and water natural resources management; health, livelihoods, and social protection; infrastructure and urban development; energy and environment; and disaster risk reduction. It identifies adaptation options with corresponding key activities and gender considerations (FIRE, 2020).

# Gender Mainstreaming Strategy 2020-2025, CRGE Facility

The CRGE Facility Gender Mainstreaming Strategy aims to address gender gaps and opportunities relating to its climate finance mandate. The overall goal of the Strategy is to enable vulnerable women and men in Ethiopia to access and benefit from climate finance and other resources that strengthen their resilience to climate change. The Strategy aims to achieve this by creating equitable and fair opportunities for women and men, including through the development of gender-responsive project design and development.

The Strategy has four strategic outcomes, with associated outputs and activities, as well as a gender action plan. The four strategic outcomes are: 1) increased access to climate finance; 2) synergistic outcomes identified are:

- Transformed policies, institutions, and processes within the CRGE Facility and Executing Entities on the promotion of gender equality.
- Enhanced coordination mechanisms and strategy delivery within the CRGE Facility and Executing Entities.
- Increased implementation of gender responsive projects and programs in the CRGE Facility.
- Increased participation of women in climate action decision-making.

The Strategy builds upon the findings and recommendations of the Gender Framework developed for the Facility in 2019. A scoping study conducted in 2019, the National Women’s Policy and the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Gender Action Plan were used to inform the development of the Strategy. The CRGE aims at building adaptive capacity to address gender concerns in the climate change space. It is expected to strengthen key interventions that will contribute to achieving the overall goal of the CRGE Facility, including through core operations of the CRGE Facility and its implementing and executing partners at various levels (MoF, 2020).

# Updated Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC), 2021

The updated NDC was submitted by Ethiopia in June 2015. The updated NDC includes updated greenhouse gas emission projections and is aligned with the national Ten-Year Development Plan. In the updated NDC, Ethiopia commits to increased mitigation: to reduce economy-wide emissions by at least


---
**Page 177**
---

68.9% by 2030 against the business-as-usual projection. The NDC also specifies 40 adaptation interventions (FCCR, 2021).

A gender analysis undertaken on the updated NDC shows that there are neither gender-specific interventions nor gender-disaggregated results and indicators to ensure gender mainstreaming in the mitigation interventions. Although there is improvement in the updated adaptation actions, the analysis indicates that there has been no significant change from the previous version of the NDC as far as A4 gender disaggregation is concerned. The analysis acknowledges that areas identified for GHG emission reductions, particularly those related to land use and forestry, are predominantly male-dominated sectors (A2019). However, very few gender-specific interventions are included.

The analysis concludes that there is a lot to be done during the preparation of detailed implementation plans at all levels. It also highlights that the sectoral working in the sectors should focus on specific objectives rather than general mainstreaming activities, which usually do not result in tangible outcomes. Financial and human resources for gender-responsive adaptation interventions are limited. In the mitigation interventions, the analysis calls for an earmarked budget to implement gender-responsive activities (Bertelsen, 2019).

The Ten-Year Development Plan (2020/21 – 2030/31) Ethiopia’s ten-year development plan sets the government development mission from 2020/21-2030/31. In the sectoral strategies, it is clearly stated that attention will be given to women’s rights, representation and equitable access and ownership to resources. This includes completely addressing gender inequality in land tenure by increasing the proportion of women who own or have secure title over at least 50% of all agricultural land; providing equal access for men and women to credit and other financial services; ensuring that women are included as decision-makers in the green economy through development and conservation of the environment, forest, wildlife, and biodiversity (Plan and Development Commission, 2021).

Institutional Arrangements The 2001 water sector strategy puts provision for the promotion of establishment of water users’ association and irrigation cooperatives at local levels, while promoting the role of women in these community-based structures. Women are also involved as service providers and managers in water supply and sanitation management.

The establishment of the Ministry of Women’s Affairs in 2005, with structures at regional, woreda and sector departments, symbolizes the government’s commitment to gender equality. The Ministry is charged with the responsibility of overseeing and coordinating the work of sectoral ministries in their efforts to address gender issues. It has been involved in developing policies, including the Women’s Development and Change Package, that identify gender issues relevant to climate change adaptation and mitigation (A2019). In 2013, it was restructured as the Ministry of Women and Social Affairs.

A number of reports on the integration of gender and climate change shows that there has been limited success in this regard. A report by the CRGE Framework for Gender Equality in Agriculture found that the Women’s Affairs Departments within sectors were not actively contributing towards integration of gender in the context of the CRGE Framework (CRGE, 2013). Another report by the CGIAR Research Program on Climate Change, Agriculture and Food Security found that women’s limited role in decision-making and planning processes (AU, 2012).

A more recent study (2017) showed that the involvement of the Women’s Affairs Departments in policy development was very low. The study also found that there were no gender roles at A4 level (A2019). Within the Ministry of Agriculture, where a gender mainstreaming manual was developed, at the zone and woreda levels (where actual implementation takes place) the assigned gender focal points were found to have little or very limited knowledge of gender issues (A2019).


---
**Page 178**
---

A scoping study conducted in 2020 on the Climate and Gender Directorate of the Ministry of Finance and the Environment Forest and Climate Change Commission concluded that there is consensus amongst stakeholders that gender-responsive climate change policy and program are vital for addressing climate change issues. However, the understanding of which gender issues need to be incorporated into the day-to-day operations of the government, as well as the capacity of government staff at all levels, including officials, indicates the need to create a common understanding. Moreover, despite the interest in integrating gender perspectives and promoting gender equality within the climate change agenda, there is limited gender-responsive planning and implementation of program, accountability and monitoring, intra- and inter- sectoral coordination, and institutional strengthening (see also Mekonnen, 2019; Mekonnen, Tadesse, and Lealem, M., 2020).

In December 2020, the GRGE Facilitator and the then Ministry of Women, Children and Youth (MoWCY) launched the National Committee for Practice for Gender Equality and Social Inclusion in Climate Change. It was agreed that the Women's Affairs Directorates of the Ministry of Finance and the Environment Forest and Climate Change Commission would be responsible for coordinating this initiative (MoWCY). While MoWCY would provide overall guidance, Members include sector ministers, non-governmental organizations, civil society organizations, and other stakeholders in the climate change sector. The committee will work with the MoWCY to identify gender-responsive actions within the climate change agenda, as well as to ensure that all relevant sectoral experts worked on and approved the terms of reference and the 2021/2022 workplan. The committee will also be responsible for ensuring that the activities are coordinated and aligned investment programming; capacity development and implementation; advocacy and resource mobilization; and monitoring, evaluation, reporting and knowledge management (MoWCY, 2021c).

### IV. Key Gender and Climate Change Issues at Project Sites

The project sites to potentially extract groundwater from deep aquifers for agricultural production and drinking and increase the resilience of communities in the Amhara region are identified as:

- The Amhara region's Raya-Kobo Girana Valley (for agricultural production); and
- The southern rangelands in Oromia region's Borana zone (for agricultural production and drinking water).

**Raya-Kobo Girana Valley, Amhara Region**

Background

The Amhara region is situated in the northwestern and north-central parts of Ethiopia. It has a total area of 105,324 km² with a population of 21.1 million. 84% of the population live in rural areas and are engaged in agricultural production (MoFED, 2019). The Amhara region include teft, barley, wheat, oil seeds, sorghum, millet, maize, and beans as major crops. The total number of livestock is 8,314,200 (27% of the national total), with a large proportion being sheep and goats. The Amhara region has various water resources, including Lake Tana, and several rivers that have potential for irrigation development (UNICEF, 2019a).

Although there has been consistent decline in monetary poverty over time, the Amhara region is still lagging behind other regions of Ethiopia with respect to access to social services. The Amhara region benefits from a number of government programs such as the Productive Safety Net, there is still a significant proportion of people living below the national poverty line (the SDG target being 13%) and almost one-third (31%) live below the food poverty line (SDG target being 16%).

Fig.5: Location map of Amhara region and Kobo-Girana Valley

| Source: Gebreses, M. and Müller-Mahn, D., G. 2019. |


---
**Page 179**
---

The median age of 18.2 years for first marriage among women aged 20-49 years is the lowest in the country.

The rationale behind early marriage and childbearing is poverty, which increases the risk that daughters engage in pre-marital sex, exposing them to sexually transmitted diseases and pregnancy while still unmarried, which would lead to family disgrace and social stigmatization (UNICEF, 2019a).

As in most other regions of Ethiopia, Amhara women and girls are traditionally labelled as nurturers and caregivers. The decision on when a girl should get married is usually made by her parents or guardians. In some cases, the decision to marry off their daughters is decided by parents and 64% of women stop attending school after marriage, with the main reason being that they are too busy taking care of household chores (UNICEF, 2019a).

As in other regions, Amhara women are often denied their share of inheritance when their parents or husbands die. It is also common for women to be excluded from decisions on common property in marriage and to be forced into early marriages.

Gender-based violence is high in Amhara region, with women aged 15-49 reporting psychological (26%), physical (22%) and sexual (17%) violence. Further, 65% of women and 48% of men believe that it is justified for a husband to beat his wife in certain circumstances (UNICEF, 2017).

The climate in Amhara region affects significantly by weather patterns. Farmers face droughts, frost, hailstorms, flooding, and landslides. Localized flooding of fields by rainfall run-off is a frequent problem. It was estimated that more than 35,000 people were at risk of flooding and more than 25,000 people were likely displaced in 2016 (UNICEF, 2019a).

According to the 2016 Ethiopia Demographic and Health Survey (EDHS), 64% of households use improved drinking water sources. This is lower than with only about one-third of water sources being piped. The Ethiopia Socioeconomic Survey (ESS) in 2017 found that women spend an average of 3 hours per day collecting water from the nearest water source, fetching water, and returning to their dwelling. As in other parts of the country, women and girls are more likely than men to be responsible for this task. The proportion is highest among women aged 45% and 75%, respectively.

A study on gender mainstreaming in selected sectors in the Amhara region shows that, despite the existence of legal and policy frameworks, in practice gender mainstreaming is not being implemented. It is also not taken into consideration in the region's plans, implementation monitoring, and evaluation and budgeting processes (UNICEF, 2019a).

Rayo-Kobo Girra Valley

The Rayo-Kobo Girra valley is found in North Wele zone of Amhara region and has diverse agro-ecological zones. The valley has a bimodal rainfall pattern. On the one hand, dry and moist kiroo agro-ecological zones, which are characterized by relatively sterile alluvial soils, an erratic rain regime that is drought prone, and frequent drought and crop failure (FDER, 2007). On the other hand, wetter areas with fertile soils, high rainfall, and low soil fertility but suffer from heavy pressure population, land degradation and significant levels of poverty (FDER, 2007).

The valley has a bimodal rainfall pattern. The short rainy season (the Bellig season), which extends from March to May, accounts for about one-third of the total annual rainfall in the valley and is characterized by dry time. Production in this season accounts for 26% of the area farmed and 35% of rain-fed crop production. This season is characterized by low yields and poor quality crops that are mainly marketed areas in the lowlands.

The main rainy season (the Kiremt season) extends from June to September. Rainfall amounts vary between 1,000 mm and 2,000 mm per year with a mean of about 1500 mm. Production during this time accounts for 74% of the farmed area and 65% of annual rain-fed crop production in the valley (FDER, 2007). The Kiremt season is characterized by high yields but suffer from significant levels of poverty.

The months extending from October to February are the dry season, characterized by no or little rainfall, high daily temperatures, and low humidity. This period accounts for about one-third of the total annual rainfall in the valley (FDER, 2007).

In a survey undertaken in 2007, the average household size is about 4.9. Female-headed households tend to be smaller - the average family size of female-headed households is almost 80% that of male-headed


---
**Page 180**
---

households. The net annual population growth rate is estimated to be 3.5%, which means the population of the area would double in about 20 years. This means that for marriage or temporary migration, the age is about 17 years while that of males is 24 years. In general, 58% of the ever-married population is married at an age of less than 20 years (FDER, 2007).

High population pressure has led to increased environmental degradation, as well as increasing number of land deficit households. In 2007, the number of people subsisting on a hectare of land was about 4:2. Traditional irrigation systems are not sustainable and have been replaced by modern irrigation technology of the majority of the population in the area, making them vulnerable to climate shocks. Small-scale traders and informal sector workers are also affected by environmental degradation due to deforestation or environmental degradation; changing patterns of rainfall, low level of input use and poor cultural practices.

annual crop yields are very low. irrigated crop production accounts for about 38% of annual cultivated land and 40% of total food production in the project area (FDER, 2007). In the highlands, 65% of the households are traditional river diversions (70%) and; for a few households (30%), modern small-scale irrigation systems are used. The majority of the farmers use the same land year after year without any fallow in the last ten years. Irrigated crop production is only practiced once per year, mainly during the dry season irrigation period from March to May. In 2007, most households (85%) had no access to credit and only a few households (FDER, 2007).

The problem of water management is critical in areas that are dependent on traditional river diversions. For those irrigating crops using the river diversions, the main problems are lack of adequate technical expertise to manage pumps, water and supplementary logistics and materials, as well as lack of frequent maintenance of irrigation systems. The majority of farmers (80%) have no access to credit for purchasing inputs, tools and equipment and capacity building and training for the farmers, and weak water users associations and cooperatives. In addition, there is a lack of adequate infrastructure in terms of roads, bridges and other major constraints on the marketing and distribution of production in the project area (FDER, 2007).

The main crops produced are cereals, pulses, oil seeds, vegetables, and fruit trees. Crop production is low yielding due to poor soil fertility, lack of adequate inputs, and inadequate irrigation systems. The majority of crop production is subsistence-based, with no or small market surplus. However, irrigated crops are mostly sold in the markets during the dry season (March to May). In 2007, only a few households had access to scale irrigation. Of the total area under horticultural crops (including sugarcane), 48% is under traditional and modern small-scale irrigation systems while the rest of the land is rainfed. The area under irrigated vegetable production is rapidly increasing due to the expansion of drip irrigation and market opportunities (FDER, 2007).

Farm practices such as intercropping, crop rotation and fallowing are not common. The shortage of land generally limits such fertility-improving practices in the highlands; the average land holding per capita (cropped area) is about 1 ha. In the lowlands, the average land holding per capita (cropped area) is about 0.5 ha. However, the proportion of land under rice cultivation has been reduced as well as the proportion of land under rice each year is very low – due, again, to the shortage of land, as well as lack of irrigation facilities.

Although access to irrigation land is determined mainly by proximity of land holdings to irrigation water and facilities, significant disparities are evident in access to irrigation, training, and extension services. The majority of households (80%) have no access to irrigation land. In addition, only 15% of the women over age twenty to productive assets leads that to less income, decision making power and low access to basic services such as education and health care. Women are also underrepresented in the area of irrigated agriculture; only about 22%, or female-headed (25% of the total female-headed households). Of these, only 41% have developed adequate experience to use irrigation, compared with two-thirds of male-headed households. Only a few women (3%) received training on irrigation techniques and management.

Of those females who have received training on irrigated agriculture, only 8% report that the training was adequate in terms of content and duration. In general, female-headed households are less likely than male heads have access to irrigation extension and support services, compared with 80% of male heads. Furthermore, the proportion of irrigated land is only less than 0.4 ha for female heads compared to 0.7 ha for males (FDER, 2007).


---
**Page 181**
---

Most women in the valley are engaged in family and household chores in addition to supporting agricultural activities such as planting, weeding, and transporting. In some parts of the highlands, women are also engaged in plowing, phlothing, and threshing. Decisions on planting time, the sale of animals and crops and other matters that affect the household are predominantly made by males. However, this applies to mainly male-headed households only. Female-headed households have more say over their own land and their households. Equal decision-making is, however, common with regards to some issues, particularly migration of family members for work, purchase of livestock and contracting and also, sometimes, marketing of large animals such as cows and oxen (FIRE, 2007).

Although men and women have equal legal rights in inheriting and possessing land, the majority of female-headed households actually own smaller plots of land compared to male-headed households. Women in general own land that is less than one hectare per household on average, while males own more than two hectares per person separation: very few of them own land through inheritance. The proportion of male-headed households with less than half a hectare of land by far, is 18%, whereas that of female-headed households is approximately 30%. Female-headed households are also more likely to have no land at all (less than 2.5 times than that of females). In addition to the relatively small plots of land they cultivate, women-headed households own fewer livestock and other assets compared with male-headed households. While most male heads own fertile land, while for males is about 37% (FIRE, 2007).

The efficiency of use of farm owned is more critical for the female headed than those of males. Most of the female headed households are engaged in renting and contracting the land to cope with shortages of farm oxen and tools. These households usually suffer from low productivity, low cash incomes and food insecurity (FIRE, 2007). Female-headed households also have less access to credit facilities compared to male-headed households. The proportion of females with an own is also twice that of males (FIRE, 2007).

Female headed households are more likely to be poor than their male counterparts. The average annual production of female-headed households is only 1,211 kg, compared to 2,865 kg for males. Female-headed households depend more on purchases from markets to fill their food gap. About one third (30%) of their food needs are met through purchases in the market while male headed households rely less on this source (20%). For males, market purchases account for 24% of annual food consumption (FIRE, 2007).

The investment expenditure accounts for about 15% and 8% of total cash income for female-headed and male-headed households respectively. Over 91% of the income of the female-headed households is obtained from crop sales. Non-agricultural income accounts for less than 5% of their total cash income. Of their annual cash expenditure, about 49% for immediate consumption while only 30% goes to investment and savings (FIRE, 2007). For male headed households, however, consumption and investment expenditure account for 87% and 13%, respectively.

Although rural credit schemes are said to provide loans to the most vulnerable, only 23% of female-headed households report having access to rural credit in recent years. The proportion is even lower (9%) among male headed households. Only 40% of female-headed households have savings accounts while this is 71% that of male-headed households (FIRE, 2007).

The water availability ratio of urban and rural areas in the region is about 87% and 41%, respectively. The sources of potable water in the project area includes developed springs, deep, shallow, hand dug wells. However, due to the inadequacy of these sources, a considerable proportion (30%) of the river water and unprotected springs are used for drinking purposes. These are found to be protected except some springs which are sometimes found not to be good due to high levels of salinity. Access to safe water is limited in the project area as only 25% of households have access to piped water while 30% use boreholes in the area. The majority of the protected and unprotected sources (except tap water) are not adequate for drinking purposes, especially during dry seasons when there is a high prevalence of some diseases in the area, which are the cause for over 50% morbidity, especially for children under the age of five years old.


23


---
**Page 182**
---

The frequency of fetching water depends on the banner size of the household. Over half of households fetch water two or more times a day and spend a minimum of five hours per day doing so. The burden of fetching water from different sources is the responsibility of the wife, followed by adult females and then girls. This has an adverse impact on the ability of girls to attend school and hinders women from effectively participating in income-generating activities.

Considering this, the Kobo-Girara Valley Development Program (KGVDP), a government-led large-scale ground water-based irrigation program, has installed three water supply points in low-lying villages of Raya-Kobo and Girara valleys.

About two-thirds of households in the region are illiterate. The main reasons for illiteracy are the need for labor, lack of awareness, poverty, and shortage of access to school (primarily because it is too far away). In terms of gender disparity, girls have a higher rate of illiteracy than boys. In 2017, only one in four girls were changing and girls have almost equal access to education. As a result, about 34% of the female population is now literate compared with 56% for males. The proportion of girls who are literate increases from grade two to grade four; with increasing grade level, the proportion of girls declines from 29% in grades 5-6 to 1.5% in grade 10 and above.

The proportion of boys in grade 10 and above is almost 2.5 times less than that of boys (FDRE, 2007).

Though slowly changing, the role of girls and women is still mainly limited to household chores. Poverty, lack of awareness, cultural norms, and gender inequality are some of the factors that limit women from improved stoves, grinding mills, energy sources, etc.) and lack of nearby secondary schools contribute not only to low school enrollment but also to high dropout rates among girls. In addition, poor families and female-headed households have relatively lower access to education services (FDRE, 2007).

In times of stress, cultural norms affect women. Mothers and adult women usually eat after they feed their husbands and children. Women are more likely than men to be the primary caregivers for sick family members, including children. Women also suffer more from maternal mortality. Maternal malnutrition has a synergistic effect on the family and the child. Women are more vulnerable to diseases such as malaria, HIV/AIDS, and tuberculosis (FDRE, 2017). Women have higher rates of pre- and post-natal complications. Only 46% of women visit health services during pregnancy – and, for female heads, it is only 34%. Furthermore, for the majority, delivery is done in their own home, with only 4% delivering at a health facility (FDRE, 2017).

Conflict in the area where the KGVDP office is located has resulted in difficulty to access latest data for this gender analysis. The baseline survey and the feasibility study that will be conducted at the beginning of the project will include a gender baseline assessment in the Raya-Kobo Girara Valley.

Borana Zone, Oromia Region

Background
Oromia is the largest region in Ethiopia, occupying approximately 34% of its total land area. It accounts for 37% of the population. The total population of the region is about 18m and account for 54% of the population (CSA, 2019). The fertility rate in Oromia is higher than the national average at 6.4 compared to the national rate of 4.6 (CSA, 2017a). The region’s urbanization level is also larger, at 5.2 people per household as opposed to a national average of 4.8 people per household (CSA, 2017c).
