---
**Page 183**
---

Oromia has a diverse range of agro-ecological zones. Sedentary rain-fed agriculture and livestock production dominate in the highland areas while the lowlands are characterized by pastoral communities who depend on livestock production (UNICEF, 2016). The region is divided into 20 administrative zones with 84% of the population living in rural areas (CSA, 2019). Oromia has experienced high and sustainable economic growth over the past decade. This has translated to improved access to education and job opportunities in the region, especially for youth (UNICEF, 2016).

Strong government commitment to the Comprehensive School Health and Safety Net Program (CSHSP); and implementation of pro-poor economic and social development policies and strategies have all contributed to an increase per capita income from $358.94 in 2000/05 to $700.16 in 2015/16 (FIRET, 2016). A poverty analysis study conducted by the CSA in 2015/16 found that the poverty headcount ratio in Oromia was 23.9%, just above the national average of 23.1 percent.

Oromia region has the most repeated beneficiaries of relief food in Ethiopia, especially between 2016 and 2018 due to droughts and conflicts (UNICEF, 2022). The region had 792,886 internally displaced persons due to conflict and climate shocks (IOM, 2018).

The proportion of pregnant women who gave birth in the five years and who received antenatal care from a skilled health provider was 54.3% (CSA, 2019). The proportion of births attended by a skilled birth attendant or midwife is 67%. Of these births are assisted by a skilled attendant (doctor or midwife) and 56% of women give birth without any assistance during delivery.

There is a high prevalence of malnutrition, with serious implications for social and economic development. In Oromia, the child deaths are associated with under-nutrition (CSA, 2016). With 96% of children under 5 years old, 35% wasted and 16% underweight (EPHIS, 2018). Stunting is associated with low socio-economic status and poor access to health services. Children from the lowest quintile are two times more likely to be stunted than those whose mothers have completed secondary or higher education (UNICEF, 2017).

The gross enrollment ratio (GER) and the net enrollment ratio (NER) for pre-primary education in Oromia are low (29.4% and 6.4%, respectively) and far below the national average of 40.3% and 23.9, respectively.

Only 17% of children aged between six months to five years old have been vaccinated against measles without primary school is 20%, higher than the national average of 17.5%. Some of the reasons for high dropout rates are poor infrastructure, lack of access to water and sanitation facilities, early marriage, abduction of girls, long distances to schools, internal migration due to climate change, drought, and conflicts (UNICEF, 2016).

About 17% of water sources in Oromia are piped and 63% of households use improved drinking water sources, marginally fewer than the national average of 65% (CSA, 2018). 28% of households spend more than 30 minutes collecting water from a source. The region has made some progress in improving progress in water infrastructure and the availability of water sources. As elsewhere in the country, women and girls are mostly responsible for fetching water (UNICEF, 2017).

Lack of water supply and poor facilities, as well as hygiene practices at schools, are major challenges for girls, leading to high rates of school absenteeism. The problem is compounded due to menstruation: 95% of schools never have water available and 100% of schools never have soap available. There is a clear need for a gender-sensitive approach in the design and implementation of water supply systems at schools, in order to address school absenteeism, performance and completion (UNICEF, 2017).

Dependency on land and weather for agricultural and livestock production is a key vulnerability for many households in Oromia. This has led to increased inter-communal conflicts because of trans-boundary competition over resources, such as grazing land, arable land, and water (UNICEF, 2016).

There was an increase in the average median age of marriage in Oromia between 2000 and 2011; however, progress has since stagnated and currently stands at 17.4 years. There has also been a decline in child


---
**Page 184**
---

marriage rates, from 58% in 1991 to 48% in 2016 – but still well above the national average of 40% (CSA, 2016).

In coming decades, rising temperatures, extraordinary rainfall events and more intense and prolonged droughts and floods are projected (World Bank, 2010). The high prevalence of poverty, high rates of malnutrition, high population growth and low climate adaptive capacities increase vulnerability to climate change. Women and girls are disproportionately affected by the impacts of climate change and climate change, as emergencies exacerbate existing gender inequalities (CEDAW, 2018). During climate change-induced disasters, women and girls are more likely to be displaced from their homes and livelihoods increasing in increased gender-based violence that affects women and girls disproportionately (UNICEF, 2016).

As in most other regions of Ethiopia, Oromia Regional State has a patriarchal society in which men hold primary power in private and public life. Women and girls have traditionally performed their roles in the domestic sphere, while men are responsible for income generation activities. However, women are increasingly taking on more responsibilities as heads of households, especially as male breadwinners become less available due to migration or illness, leaving women as the main providers for their families and carers, with the result that childcare responsibilities often fall exclusively on them (UNICEF, 2016).

In line with the national average, in Oromia 35% of women (aged 15-49) decide for themselves to marry, while parents make the decision for 61% (CSA, 2016).

## Borana zone

Borana zone is located in the southern part of Oromia Region. The population of the project area is estimated at about 347,000, of whom 52.3% are male and 49.7% are female. There is a total of approximately 43,000 households, of which approximately 30,000 are male-headed and 13,000 are female-headed. The majority (68%) live in urban areas, while 85% reside in rural areas. The average family size is about 5.6 (OWMEB, 2018).

The climate of the area is characterized as arid and semi-arid. The average annual temperature ranges from 14.3°C to 27.9°C with an average rainfall of approximately 450 mm per year. There are two rainy seasons in a year for crop production and the growth of pasture: February to March, June to July, August to September or October to December.

The topography is characterized by the presence of low-lying hills and flats at certain intervals, with variation of ground elevation which range from 1,100 m to 2,495 meters above sea-level (masl). Broadly, the area is divided into three main physiographic regions: namely, the western mountainous ridge and associated valleys; the central low-lying hills and flats; and the eastern plains.

The land-use patterns of Borana Zone consist of grazing land, built-up areas, protected forest land, shrub and bushland, marshy meadows, cultivated fields, water bodies (lakes), Traditional Areas, commonly held lands, and private lands. In Borana society, all men are born into a clan society. At marriage, they acquire rights to land and water in Borana Zone, with no gender, age, or social discrimination. However, there is now a growing trend of women acquiring these rights through their own efforts and the equitable distribution of resources and partly because of the desire to accumulate wealth (OWMEB, 2018).

The livelihoods of most households are based on livestock production which includes mostly animal husbandry and small crop production. Other supplementary off-farm activities, such as petty trades, sales of charcoal and firewood, and daily labor in towns, are also undertaken by some households (OWMEB, 2018).

Crop production is often constrained by poor soil fertility, unreliable rainfall, and termite infestation. The crops produced in small pockets include teff, maize, haricot bean, sorghum, and sugarcane. However, lack of rain and limited supply of inputs such as fertilizers and tractors further constrain crop production (OWMEB, 2018).


---
**Page 185**
---

The Borana people have traditionally depended on livestock rearing. It is a source of milk, milk and meat, as well as protein. However, the number of livestock is declining due to lack of food and water. Land degradation, genetic erosion, climate change and prevalent diseases are cited as key reasons for the increased scarcity of feed and water. The sales of livestock and their products at local markets constitute a significant source of income for Borana households. However, they face challenges such as low market prices, weak market linkages and pastoral extension services are among the challenges the community faces in accessing these resources (OWIMBE, 2018). Many households are temporarily or permanently in a state of food insecurity and depend on food aid (OWIMBE, 2018).

The average monthly income about of 82% of the households is both urban and rural areas in the region is below B5,000. The average monthly income between from 3,000-5,000, and only about 6% of households earn over B11,500 (OWIMBE, 2018).

There is water shortage in the zone and is extreme in the low-lying areas, in terms of quantity, quality and access. The rainfall pattern is erratic with a high degree of variability and is mostly seasonally and locally seasonal. As a result, individuals, and sometimes even all family members, often migrate or travel long distances to find water sources. In addition, livestock are also forced to drink from the same sources water with livestock and wildlife because of tradition as well as scarcity (OWIMBE, 2018).

As a coping mechanism for water scarcity and other external shocks, man-made traditional ponds, Eleta, which can be used by both livestock and humans, are very common. As an additional coping mechanism, farmers often seasonally migrate to areas in the south-western part of the zone with their herds, where grazing is better. Other people and livestock spend long hours under tree or bush shade for water (OWIMBE, 2018).

The existing sources of water include man-made water wells or ponds (Eleta), groundwater harvesting, cisterns, unprotected springs, roof-water harvesting, unprotected wells, protected wells, and public taps. Of these available sources, only 35% are protected while the remaining 65% are unprotected. Most of the sources have been developed by NGOs and the government, at different times, to supply water to the surrounding communities. The most common types of water sources in the zone include: 10 hand dug wells, 21 hand dug wells and 6 developed springs) and have low capacity to serve the entire community. Currently, only 35% of the community use protected wells and springs, while 47% use unprotected wells and springs, 12% of the community use unprotected wells and springs, with 3.2% harvest rainwater. The remaining obtain their water from public taps (OWIMBE, 2018). In addition to these sources, there are also boreholes that contain potable water. In addition, they pay up to 30 ETB 20 liters of water to local vendors in many locations (OWIMBE, 2018).

In times of need, the Borana community migrate to other areas within the Borana zone or the adjacent West-Guj zone and even beyond into the Southern Nationalities and Peoples Regional State. During drought periods, women and children are more vulnerable than men because they have less access to water sources. Women and girls are the most affected groups, and they have the higher share in search of water from long distances (OWIMBE, 2018).

Based on the sampling collected during a 2018 survey, the daily amount of water consumed per household is in the range of 6-26 liters. Approximately half (52.7%) of households use between 6-10 liters per day, 15% use more than 20 liters and only about 3% consume less than 10 liters per day (OWIMBE, 2018). The WHO defines 5.3 liters/person/day as the minimum amount with high health concern (Howard G., et al., 2006).

Student enrollment and participation rates at primary and secondary schools are below 75%. The net enrolment rate for primary school is 49% and secondary (15%) schools in Borana are lower than those of the national level, which are 97% and 25% for primary and secondary schools, respectively (OWIMBE, 2018).

Borana zone has a total of 502 primary and secondary schools, 3 technical vocational education and training (TVET) institutions and 4 health facilities. The majority of the students in these schools are male and 49% are female. However, in the TVETs and university the participation of female students is

## V. Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment

The Government of Ethiopia through its Ministry of Women and Social Affairs (MoWSA) has drafted a Women Empowerment and Gender Equality policy which has provisions for Gender Based Violence. The policy is currently under review but once approved at public institutions including Ministry of Finance (MoF) are required to apply it.

The Ministry of Finance (MoF) has an employee code of conduct which states:

- **Complaining, attempting or facilitating conditions for sexual harassment, abuse, and/or violence against a colleague or customer shall be penalized.**
- **Employees and heads that shall not abuse their authorities and apply such authorities to get personal interest.**

Further, in order to ensure Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH) does not undermine the well-being of the communities and other stakeholders who will be involved in this project, guidelines are recommended to be in place. Specifically, the following potential risks are identified along with recommended mitigation actions:


---
**Page 187**
---

1. Lack of awareness or what SEAH constitutes and how it needs to be addressed
Create awareness on safe water, handling and reporting of SEAH in conjunction with MoNSWA and its regional and worsds level offices. This will be done to all those involved at the federal region and worrda levels by having dedicated sessions during project implementation team meetings.

2. Risk of SEAH during project delivery including trainings, irrigation system placements etc.
• Put in place a screening process to identify project activities that might have high risk of SEAH.
• Ensure any contracts to be signed between the project and partners (including project personnel) contain clauses on SEAH.

3. Risk of violence against women within household due to increased women empowerment
• Have a dedicated SEAH sessions during community consultations including woman-only consultations.
• Ensure they are clear on who to contact (and how) in case of any incidence.

4. Lack of reporting system
• Develop a Grievance Redress Mechanism for the project and ensure all stakeholders are aware of it.
• Ensure the reporting mechanisms are simple and safe.
• Ensure that at least one person from each community, project staff, government counterparts are required to report suspected or actual SEAH cases.
• Establish a SEAH focal point at the community level and district level at the beginning of the project.
• Assign SEAH focal point both within the communities, project staff and women and social affair officers. The focal points will be responsible for ensuring that all reported cases are documented with support smooth communication and provide a sense of security to community members.
• Make different channels are available for reporting including telephone, in-person, police, community elders etc
• The safeguarding team to advise on how to resolve reported cases and refer it to the police if necessary.
• All SEAH reports will be kept confidential to protect those involved.

5. Lack of follow up and proper documentation
• As part of its project monitoring, the AE, will monitor the proper follow-up of reported cases and how they are managed.
• Reported cases will be included in the project report.
• Lessons learned from the project will be documented and used to improve the project processes as well as future projects and programs of the AE.


---
**Page 188**
---

### Recommendations

This gender assessment and gender action plan is prepared based on information from the available literature and stakeholder consultation. For Kobo, consultation has indicated irrigation as the priority of the community. Based on the challenges identified and prioritized during the stakeholder consultation, the following actions are recommended to contribute towards the empowerment of women and female headed households.

#### 1. Gender Analysis and Baseline Setting

Carry out gender analysis and establish baseline within the first two quarters of project implementation. It is recommended that at least one gender specialist be assigned in each region and worlds project offices, the region and worlds women and social affair experts and the CRGE. Facility gender specialist will be specialized to carry out gender analysis and gender action plan. The following key actors are to be consulted in the process: Community representatives, women association representatives, men association representatives, youth representatives, religious leaders, elders, parents, children, and any other relevant stakeholder need to be engaged in the process. Consultations planned under 2.1.1 and 2.1.2 on the GAP will be used to share and gather relevant information. The outcomes of the gender analysis will be an update/adjust the GAP as needed. Changes that are recommended, if any, will be shared with the CRGE.

#### 2. Project Implementing Team – Gender expertise

The CRGE Facility gender specialist will support the project coordinators at the federal level and provide oversight on the implementation of the gender action plan. The regional and worlds gender focal points will be assigned to each region and world office. The MILA Programme Coordination team will include a gender expert. A representative from the women and social affairs directorate of the ministry will be assigned to support and follow up on the project delivery focusing on the implementation of the GAP.

The project officers at the region and worlds level will work with sector bureaus and offices including the women and social affairs departments to implement the project. The assigned gender experts will be responsible for the overall implementation of the project focusing on the GAP and will have a necessary budget to support the project.

#### 3. Consultations

All project consultations should take into consideration the importance of inclusive participation with at least 35% of women present in all meetings and more than 50% in stakeholder meetings. Consultations should present and discuss the project activities, gender action plan, SEAH roles and responsibilities of various stakeholders and the progress made and challenges faced. It should also be used as a platform for women to share their experiences with other women and to learn from each other within their households. Any lesson learned through this process will inform the project implementation throughout its duration.

Led by worlds and region project officers, and the region and worlds gender officer representatives, at least one woman-only consultations (at least 35% of members of ‘coops’ are women) per year will be organized to ensure that women’s voices are heard. These meetings should not only be a platform for them not only to voice their needs, ideas and challenges freely but also helps them to fully understand what their role, responsibilities and rights are as members of the different cooperatives, including what they can do and whom to contact in case of SEAH incidents.

#### 4. Access to irrigation and potable water


---
**Page 189**
---

water user groups should make sure that at least 35% of the members (with the aim of reaching 50% by the end of the project) and 25% of the executive committee are women. This could be female headed households or women in male dominated positions. It is important to ensure that women are part of the decision-making process and that irrigation activities include women priorities.

In Borana, where the project also focuses on potable water, at least 50% of the female headed households should be able to access water from the rehabilitated wells.

## Training

The project should carry out needs assessment and provide a number of training sessions to make sure implementing personnel as well as beneficiaries have the capacity to fully deliver and benefit from the project. The following training is recommended:

- Technical and leadership trainings should be organized for elected officers (irrigation and water user groups) based on capacity needs.
- Financial management trainings are recommended for organized for cooperative officers with additional training for women officers and members.
- Training in gender-specific technical skills, based on capacity needs, should be delivered for women officers and members.

Regional and woreda irrigation bureaus in collaboration with the CRGE facility to provide guidance for project delivery personnel (including region and woreda offices and key federal, regional, woreda, and kebele government officials) on how to deliver the GAP and align with the QCF gender policy.
- Increased participation of women in all vocational training programs for local technicians of the entire irrigation system.

## Lessons / knowledge sharing

Gender results, challenges and gaps identified through the project implementation should be documented and shared to inform subsequent initiatives.

### Grievance

There should be a safeguard desk committee established for the project with both men and women as part of the team responsible for receiving grievances. The grievances should be recorded and reported.

## Monitoring and evaluation

Project activity and MAE reports include sex disaggregated data and gender results are evaluated and reported.

### Conclusion

As captured in this gender assessment, in Ethiopia women are given the majority of household responsibilities, including provision of water supply, energy for cooking and heating, and food security, in addition to childcare and other domestic chores. Women also have a significant role in crop production and marketing. These responsibilities are more demanding for female-headed households, which have less labor and assets at their disposal. As a result, rural women are more vulnerable to, and less able to adapt to, drought, uncertainty of rainfall, and climate change.

Despite their significant role in supporting their households and the community at large, evidence shows that women are often excluded from decision-making processes related to water resources management due to norms and practices that further disempower them from being active, self-sufficient members of society.


---
**Page 190**
---

These conditions have contributed to women’s generally low access to education, health services and workforce participation as well as other key resources and services.

A number of actions, both at policy and on the ground levels, have been implemented by different actors to address these challenges. Although commendable successes have been recorded in different sectors, considerable gaps remain. The GCF project seeks to ensure that women are recognized as equal contributors to the country’s social, environmental, and economic development while equally benefiting from the gains of development.

The GCF project builds on results towards baseline efforts on the ground by identifying actions that will equally benefit women, especially in the context of access to drinking water and irrigating farmland in the project area.
